\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\text{exists a path $X_{1}\to X_{t,H}$ in $\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}$}\\
\text{labeled by $\pi$ }\end{subarray}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\cdot\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\pi^{\star}=\pi\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H-6}}+\frac{1}{\lvert\Pi_{1:H-1}\rvert}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\text{exists a path $X_{1}\to X_{t,H}$ in $\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}$}\\
\text{labeled by $\pi$ }\end{subarray}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H-7}}.

(4.10)

The first inequality follows by the calculation in
Lemma
4.6
, and the second inequality follows because there are at most
T
paths in the DAG representation of
\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}
.

Completing Induction for
Claim
4.6
.

By combining Eqs. (
4.7
)–(
4.10
) we see that as long as
T\leq 2^{O(H)}
, then

\displaystyle\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{R,t}^{c}\right]\leq(t-1)\cdot\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H-7}}+\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H-7}}=t\cdot\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H-7}}.

This proves the claim.

Final Bounds for
Lemma
4.3
.

To prove the first inequality, we have directly by
Claim
4.6

\displaystyle\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\right]\leq\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{R,t}^{c}\right]\leq t\cdot\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H-7}}.

To prove the second inequality in the lemma statement, we can get a similar bound as Eq. (
4.5
):

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H-2}}+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\cdot\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H-2}}+\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}^{c}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}^{c}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\cdot\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H-2}}+(t-1)\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H-5}}+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}^{c}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\cdot\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\right],

and from here one can replicate the above argument to get a bound on this quantity. The details are omitted. This concludes the proof of
Lemma
4.3
.
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}[\mathcal{E}_{F}^{c}]\leq\frac{HT\cdot 2^{H}}{2^{2H}-2T}
.

Proof.

Let us consider the set
\mathcal{I}
(which is a random variable that depends on the interaction of
\mathsf{Alg}
with
\nu_{0}
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}=\{(t,h):X_{t,h}\notin\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\}.

We have

\displaystyle\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{F}^{c}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{h=2}^{H}\mathbbm{1}\left\{(t,h)\in\mathcal{I}\text{ and }\phi(X_{t,h})=\mathsf{g}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{h=2}^{H}\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\Pr\left[(t,h)\in\mathcal{I}\text{ and }\phi(X_{t,h})=\mathsf{g}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{h=2}^{H}\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\Pr\left[\phi(X_{t,h})=\mathsf{g}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1},(t,h)\in\mathcal{I}\right]\right].

(4.11)

Now we will bound the quantity
\Pr\left[\phi(X_{t,h})=\mathsf{g}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1},(t,h)\in\mathcal{I}\right]
for any
t\in[T]
,
h\geq 2
. Consider the annotated filtration

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}^{\prime}\coloneqq\sigma\left(\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1},\left\{\phi(X):X\in\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right\}\right)

which includes the decoder label for all observations seen thus far. We compute that for any
t\in[T],h\geq 2
:

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\phi(X_{t,h})=\mathsf{g}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}^{\prime},(t,h)\in\mathcal{I}\right]=\frac{2^{H}-\lvert\{X\in\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}:\phi(X)=\mathsf{g}\}\rvert}{2^{2H}-2t+1},

(4.12)

since once we have fixed the value of the decoder on the
2t-1
seen examples at layer
h
, the label of a new state is uniform over all remaining possibilities.

Continuing the calcuation from Eq. (
4.11
):

\displaystyle\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{F}^{c}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{h=2}^{H}\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\Pr\left[\phi(X_{t,h})=\mathsf{g}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1},(t,h)\in\mathcal{I}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{h=2}^{H}\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\En\left[\Pr\left[\phi(X_{t,h})=\mathsf{g}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}^{\prime},(t,h)\in\mathcal{I}\right]\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1},(t,h)\in\mathcal{I}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{h=2}^{H}\frac{2^{H}}{2^{2H}-2T}\leq\frac{HT\cdot 2^{H}}{2^{2H}-2T}.

The second inequality uses Eq.
(
4.12
)
. This completes the proof of
Lemma
4.4
.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}[\mathcal{E}_{N}^{c}]\leq\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H}}
.

Proof.

Any sampled transition
X_{t,h}^{\prime}
has probability at most
T/2^{H}
of being a repeated state (which is maximized if
X_{t,h}^{\prime}
has a good label and we have already sampled
T
such observations from that given latent). Applying union bound over
T(H-1)
transition samples gives us the final bound.
∎

Lemma 4.6

(Posterior of
\pi^{\star}
)
.

Fix any
t\in[T]
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\cdot\left\|\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\pi^{\star}=\cdot\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]-\mathrm{Unif}(\Pi_{1:H-1})\right\|_{1}\leq\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H-6}}.

Proof.

In what follows all of the probabilities are taken with respect to
\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}
. We can compute that

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\cdot\left\|\Pr\left[\pi^{\star}=\cdot\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]-\mathrm{Unif}(\Pi_{1:H-1})\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\cdot\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\left\lvert\Pr\left[\pi^{\star}=\pi\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]-\frac{1}{2^{H-1}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\cdot 2\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\left[\Pr\left[\pi^{\star}=\pi\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]-\frac{1}{2^{H-1}}\right]_{+}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\cdot\frac{2}{2^{H-1}}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\left[\frac{\Pr\left[\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]}{\Pr\left[\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]}-1\right]_{+}

\displaystyle\leq 2\max_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\left[\frac{\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\cdot\Pr\left[\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]}{\Pr\left[\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]}-1\right]_{+}.

(4.13)

Now we will provide explicit calculations for the conditional distribution of
\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}
for every choice of optimal policy
\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}
. Fix any
\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}
such that
R_{i,H}=0
for all
i\in[t-1]
and no repeated transitions (otherwise we can trivially upper bound Eq. (
4.13
) by 0). By chain rule we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{t}\prod_{h=1}^{H-1}\Pr\left[X^{\prime}_{i,h}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,h-1}\right]\right)\times\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\Pr\left[R_{i,H}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,H-1}\right].

We bound the transition and reward probabilities separately using
Claim
1
and
Claim
2
.

Claim 1
.

Fix any
i\in[t]
and
h\in[H-1]
. We have for every
\pi\in\Pi
:

\displaystyle\Pr\left[X^{\prime}_{i,h}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,h-1}\right]\in\frac{1}{2^{2H}}\cdot\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right),\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)\right].

To prove this claim, we can compute that

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\Pr\left[X^{\prime}_{i,h}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,h-1}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell\in\{\mathsf{g},\mathsf{b}\}}\Pr\left[X^{\prime}_{i,h}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,h-1},\phi_{h}(X_{i,h})=\ell\right]\Pr\left[\phi_{h}(X_{i,h})=\ell\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,h-1}\right]

Case 1: if
A_{i,h}=\pi^{\star}_{h}
.
If we started in a good state then we would transition to the good state, so

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\Pr\left[X^{\prime}_{i,h}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,h-1}\right]

\displaystyle=\Pr\left[\phi_{h}(X_{i,h})=\mathsf{g}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,h-1}\right]\cdot\frac{\Pr\left[\phi_{h+1}(X_{i,h}^{\prime})=\mathsf{g}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,h-1},\phi_{h}(X_{i,h})=\mathsf{g}\right]}{2^{2H}-2^{H}}

\displaystyle\quad+\Pr\left[\phi_{h}(X_{i,h})=\mathsf{b}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,h-1}\right]\cdot\frac{\Pr\left[\phi_{h+1}(X_{i,h}^{\prime})=\mathsf{b}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,h-1},\phi_{h}(X_{i,h})=\mathsf{b}\right]}{2^{H}}

Case 2: if
A_{i,h}\neq\pi^{\star}_{h}
.
In this case we know that regardless of the label of
X_{i,h}
we transition to a bad state, so

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\Pr\left[X^{\prime}_{i,h}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,h-1}\right]

\displaystyle=\Pr\left[\phi_{h}(X_{i,h})=\mathsf{g}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,h-1}\right]\cdot\frac{\Pr\left[\phi_{h+1}(X_{i,h}^{\prime})=\mathsf{b}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,h-1},\phi_{h}(X_{i,h})=\mathsf{g}\right]}{2^{2H}-2^{H}}

\displaystyle\quad+\Pr\left[\phi_{h}(X_{i,h})=\mathsf{b}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,h-1}\right]\cdot\frac{\Pr\left[\phi_{h+1}(X_{i,h}^{\prime})=\mathsf{b}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,h-1},\phi_{h}(X_{i,h})=\mathsf{b}\right]}{2^{2H}-2^{H}}

Either way, applying
Lemma
4.7
concludes the proof of
Claim
1
.

Claim 2
.

Fix any
i\in[t-1]
. We have for every
\pi\in\Pi
:

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{F}\right\}\Pr\left[R_{i,H}=1\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,H-1}\right]\leq\mathbbm{1}\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{exists a path $X_{1}\to X_{H}$ in $\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}$}\\
\text{labeled by $\pi$ }\end{subarray}\right\}.

To prove this claim, we use casework.

Case 1: if
\mathsf{RootLayer}(X_{i,H}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i,H-1})\geq 2
.
Then we must have

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{F}\right\}\cdot\Pr\left[R_{i,H}=1\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,H-1}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{F}\right\}\Pr\left[\phi(\mathsf{Root}(X_{i,H}))=\mathsf{g}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,H-1}\right]=0.

The equality holds because
\mathcal{E}_{F}\Rightarrow\{\phi(\mathsf{Root}(X_{i,H}))=\mathsf{b}\}
. This proves
Claim
2
in this case.

Case 2: if
\mathsf{RootLayer}(X_{i,h}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i,H-1})=1
.
In this case we can compare the path witnessing
\mathsf{RootLayer}=1
with the labeling
\pi^{\star}
, and we get

\displaystyle\Pr\left[R_{i,H}=1\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,H-1}\right]\leq\mathbbm{1}\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{exists a path $X_{1}\to X_{H}$ in $\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}$}\\
\text{labeled by $\pi$ }\end{subarray}\right\}.

This concludes the proof of
Claim
2
.

With
Claim
1
and
Claim
2
in hand, we return to the analysis of the posterior
\Pr\left[\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]
. Letting
O\coloneqq t(H-1)
be the number of transitions we observe in
\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}
, we get that

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]\leq\frac{1}{2^{2H\cdot O}}\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{HT}.

(4.14)

We also have the lower bound that

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\geq\frac{1}{2^{H-1}}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\Pr\left[\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]\geq\frac{2^{H-1}-T}{2^{H-1}}\cdot\frac{1}{2^{2H\cdot O}}\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{HT},

(4.15)

where the last inequality follows because for any filtration
\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}
we must have

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{no path $X_{1}\to X_{H}$ in $\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}$}\\
\text{labeled by $\pi$ }\end{subarray}\right\}=1

for at least
2^{H-1}-T
such policies in
\Pi_{1:H-1}
.

Putting Eq. (
4.14
) and (
4.15
) together we get that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\cdot\frac{\Pr\left[\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]}{\Pr\left[\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]}\leq\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H-2}}\right)^{2HT+1},

which in turn using Eq. (
4.13
) implies that

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\cdot\left\|\Pr\left[\pi^{\star}=\cdot\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]-\mathrm{Unif}(\Pi_{1:H-1})\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 2\max_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\left[\frac{\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\cdot\Pr\left[\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]}{\Pr\left[\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]}-1\right]_{+}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H-2}}\right)^{2HT+1}-1\right)\leq\frac{2HT^{2}+T}{2^{H-3}}\exp\left(\frac{2HT^{2}+T}{2^{H-2}}\right)\leq\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H-6}}.

We use the numerical inequalities
1+y\leq\exp(y)
and
\exp(y)-1\leq y\exp y
. This concludes the proof of
Lemma
4.6
.
∎

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be any filtration of
HT
generative model samples as well as annotations
\phi(x)
for a subset of observations
x\in\mathcal{F}
. Let
\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}
be any policy. Fix any
h\geq 2
, and let
x_{\mathrm{new}}\in\mathcal{X}_{h}-\mathcal{F}
. Then

\displaystyle\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(x_{\mathrm{new}})=\mathsf{g}\mid\mathcal{F},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]-\left(1-\frac{1}{2^{H}}\right)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{T}{2^{H}},

\displaystyle\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(x_{\mathrm{new}})=\mathsf{b}\mid\mathcal{F},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]-\frac{1}{2^{H}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{T}{2^{H}}.

Proof of
Lemma
4.7
.

Let us denote
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
to be the completely annotated
\mathcal{F}
which includes all labels
\{\phi(X):X\in\mathcal{F}\}
. We will show that the conclusion of the lemma applies to every completion
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
, and since

\displaystyle\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(x_{\mathrm{new}})=\cdot\mid\mathcal{F},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]=\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(x_{\mathrm{new}})=\cdot\mid\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]\mid\mathcal{F},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right],

this will imply the result by Jensen’s inequality and convexity of
\lvert\cdot\rvert
.

We calculate the good label probability:

\displaystyle\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(x_{\mathrm{new}})=\mathsf{g}\mid\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]=\frac{2^{H}-\lvert\left\{X\in\mathcal{F}:\phi(X)=\mathsf{g}\right\}\rvert}{2^{2H}-\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert}.

For the lower bound we have

\displaystyle\frac{2^{H}-\lvert\left\{X\in\mathcal{F}:\phi(X)=\mathsf{g}\right\}\rvert}{2^{2H}-\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert}\geq\frac{2^{H}-T}{2^{2H}}=\frac{1}{2^{H}}\cdot\left(1-\frac{T}{{2^{H}}}\right).

For the upper bound we have

\displaystyle\frac{2^{H}-\lvert\left\{X\in\mathcal{F}:\phi(X)=\mathsf{g}\right\}\rvert}{2^{2H}-\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert}\leq\frac{2^{H}}{2^{2H}-T}=\frac{1}{2^{H}}\cdot\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{2H}}\right)^{-1}\leq\frac{1}{2^{H}}\cdot\left(1+\frac{T}{{2^{H}}}\right),

which holds as long as
T\leq 2^{H}
. Combining both upper and lower bounds proves the lemma for the good label. The calculation for
\phi(x_{\mathrm{new}})=\mathsf{b}
is similar, so we omit it. This concludes the proof of
Lemma
4.7
.
∎

4.4  Bibliographical Remarks

Exponential Lower Bounds for RL.

We highlight the influential paper
[
DKWY19
]
, which shows an exponential (in horizon
H
) lower bound for policy learning with a generative model when the policy class
\Pi
is comprised of
linear policies
which take the form
\pi_{\theta}(x)\coloneqq\argmax_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\phi(x,a)^{\top}\theta
. In fact, our lower bound of
Theorem
4.2
recovers these results as a special case, since it is a well known fact that linear policies have infinite threshold dimension, and spanning capacity is lower bounded by threshold dimension (
Theorem
3.4
).

Policy Eluder Dimension.

[
MWC20
]
study policy learning with a generative model when the policy class has bounded policy eluder dimension (
Definition
3.2
)—see their Thm. 1. However, their result requires additional assumptions: that the policy class contains the optimal policy and that the optimal value function has a gap.

Chapter 5Online RL

Now we study the online RL interaction protocol. An overview of our results is provided in
Section
5.1
. First, we show in
Section
5.2
that unlike the generative model, bounded spanning capacity by itself is insufficient for PAC RL with online RL interaction. Next, to circumvent this lower bound, we introduce and study a new structural complexity measure called the sunflower property in
Section
5.3
. We give a positive result on the sample complexity of PAC RL for policy classes which satisfy bounded spanning capacity and the sunflower property. We conclude with several open problems in
Section
5.4
. Proofs of our main results are deferred to
Section
5.5
.

5.1  Overview

We define
n_{\mathsf{on}}(\Pi;\varepsilon,\delta)
to be the minimax sample complexity of online RL (c.f.
Definition
4.1
).

Definition 5.1
.

Fix any
(\varepsilon,\delta)\in(0,1)
as well as a policy class
\Pi
. We denote the minimax sample complexity
n_{\mathsf{on}}(\Pi;\varepsilon,\delta)
to be the smallest
n\in\mathbb{N}
for which there exists an algorithm
\mathsf{Alg}
which for any MDP
M
collects at most
n_{\mathsf{on}}(\Pi;\varepsilon,\delta)
samples using online RL access to
M
and returns an
\varepsilon
-optimal policy for
M
with probability at least
1-\delta
.

A Tempting Conjecture.

The central question we ask in this chapter is:

Is
n_{\mathsf{on}}(\Pi;\varepsilon,\delta)
always
\Theta(\mathrm{poly}(\mathfrak{C}(\Pi)))
for every policy class
\Pi
?

The lower bound is already clear. Since online RL is at least as hard as learning with a generative model, by
Theorem
4.2
, we know that
n_{\mathsf{on}}(\Pi;\varepsilon,\delta)\geq n_{\mathsf{gen}}(\Pi;\varepsilon,\delta)\geq\Omega(\mathfrak{C}(\Pi))
. But is spanning capacity also sufficient? In other words, can we design an algorithm which always achieves
\mathrm{poly}(\mathfrak{C}(\Pi))
sample complexity with online RL access?

Note that the
\mathsf{TrajectoryTree}
algorithm used to prove
Theorem
4.1
crucially relied on the ability to perform local resets. If the MDP has deterministic transitions (and fixed initial state
x_{1}
), then online RL access can be used to perform local resets, since for any state
x
which the learner has previously observed, we know the exact sequence of actions which will land the learner in that state again. In stochastic MDPs over a large state space, the learner might never see the same state again, and it seems unlikely that the
\mathsf{TrajectoryTree}
algorithm can be adapted to online RL access.

We now state the key results in this chapter which address this central question.

1.

In
Theorem
5.1
, we give a lower bound which refutes the conjecture: namely, we construct a policy class
\Pi
for which the minimax sample complexity is at least

\displaystyle n_{\mathsf{on}}(\Pi;\varepsilon,1/8)=\varepsilon^{-\Omega(\log\mathfrak{C}(\Pi))}.

In particular, this rules out any algorithm that achieves
\mathrm{poly}(\mathfrak{C}(\Pi))
sample complexity. The lower bound exemplifies the challenge of policy learning in large state spaces with online interaction: since the learner is unlikely to to see the same state more than once, they cannot perform (optimistic) exploration to reduce the uncertainty about the optimal policy. On a technical level,
Theorem
5.1
relies on a probabilistic construction of certain “block-free” matrices, which may also be of independent interest.

2.

Interestingly, for all of the policy class examples considered in
Section
2.4.2
, it
is
possible to show
\mathrm{poly}(\mathfrak{C}(\Pi))
sample complexity. In
Section
5.3
, we show that all of these policy class examples satisfy a general structural condition which we call the
sunflower property
. The sunflower property has two parameters
K
and
D
. For these examples we have
K,D=\mathrm{poly}(H)
(in fact, the policy class from
Theorem
5.1
is the only construction we know of which has
\mathfrak{C}(\Pi)=\mathrm{poly}(H)
but
K,D=2^{\mathrm{poly}(H)}
). Intuitively, the sunflower property allows us to leverage the two main algorithmic tools available:
tabular algorithms
which adaptively explore the environment when encountering previously-seen states, and
importance sampling
, which enables sample-efficient estimation of many policies simultaneously.

In
Theorem
5.2
, we design a new algorithm called
\mathsf{POPLER}
which exploits the sunflower structure of a policy class to achieve a sample complexity which is

\displaystyle\mathrm{poly}\left(\mathfrak{C}(\Pi),K,D,\varepsilon^{-1},\log\lvert\Pi\rvert,\delta^{-1}\right).

To perform policy evaluation over a large policy class
\Pi
,
\mathsf{POPLER}
utilizes a new technical tool called the
policy-specific Markov Reward Process (MRP)
, which for every
\pi\in\Pi
collapses the original MDP onto a smaller tabular MRP. The transitions/rewards of every policy-specific MRP can be simultaneously estimated via an approach that interpolates between tabular algorithms and importance sampling.

5.2  Spanning Capacity is Insufficient

We prove a somewhat surprising negative result showing that bounded spanning capacity by itself is insufficient to characterize the minimax sample complexity in online RL. In particular, we provide an example for which we have a
superpolynomial
(in
H
) lower bound on the number of trajectories needed for learning in online RL, that is not captured by any polynomial function of spanning capacity. This implies that, contrary to RL with a generative model, one can not hope for
n_{\mathsf{on}}(\Pi;\varepsilon,\delta)=\widetilde{\Theta}(\mathrm{poly}(\mathfrak{C}(\Pi),H,\varepsilon^{-1},\log\delta^{-1}))
in online RL.

Theorem 5.1

(Lower Bound for Online RL)
.

Fix any sufficiently large
H
. Let
\varepsilon\in(1/2^{\mathcal{O}(H)},\mathcal{O}(1/H))
and
\ell\in\{2,\dots,H\}
such that
1/\varepsilon^{\ell}\leq 2^{H}
. There exists a policy class
\Pi^{(\ell)}
of size
\mathcal{O}(1/\varepsilon^{\ell})
with
\mathfrak{C}(\Pi^{(\ell)})\leq\mathcal{O}(H^{4\ell+2})
and a family of MDPs
\mathcal{M}
with state space
\mathcal{X}
of size
2^{\mathcal{O}(H)}
, binary action space, and horizon
H
such that: for any
(\varepsilon,1/8)
-PAC algorithm, there exists an MDP
M\in\mathcal{M}
for which the algorithm must collect at least

\displaystyle\Omega\left(\min\left\{\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{\ell}},2^{H/3}\right\}\right)\quad\text{online trajectories in expectation.}

Informally speaking, the lower bound shows that there exists a policy class
\Pi
for which
n_{\mathsf{on}}(\Pi;\varepsilon,\delta)=\varepsilon^{-\Omega(\log_{H}\mathfrak{C}(\Pi))}
. In order to interpret this theorem, we can instantiate choices of
\varepsilon=1/2^{\sqrt{H}}
and
\ell=\sqrt{H}
to show an explicit separation.

Corollary 2
.

For any sufficiently large
H
, there exists a policy class
\Pi
with
\mathfrak{C}(\Pi)=2^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{H}\log H)}
such that for any
(1/2^{\sqrt{H}},1/8)
-PAC algorithm, there exists an MDP for which the algorithm must collect at least
2^{\Omega(H)}
online trajectories in expectation.

Theorem
5.1
shows that (1) online RL is
strictly harder
than RL with generative access, and (2) online RL for stochastic MDPs is
strictly harder
than online RL for MDPs with deterministic transitions.

Remark.
The seminal work of
[
FKQR21
]
provides a unified complexity measure called Decision-Estimation Coefficient (DEC) that characterizes the complexity of model-based RL. Given a realizable model class
\mathcal{M}
, they give an upper bound of
\mathsf{DEC}\cdot\log\lvert\mathcal{M}\rvert
. However, in our policy learning setup, this guarantee is too loose to be useful, since the size of the implicit model class is at least
\log\lvert\mathcal{M}\rvert\propto\lvert\mathcal{X}\rvert
. Followup works
[
FGQRS24
,
CFHQRX24
,
CR25
]
study whether the
\log\lvert\mathcal{M}\rvert
dependence can be improved. We observe that
Theorem
5.1
rules out an improvement of
\mathsf{DEC}\cdot\log\lvert\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}\rvert
, where
\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}
is the induced policy class. This follows because it is know that coverability is an upper bound on the DEC
[
XFBJK22
]
, and our lower bound rules out
\mathrm{poly}(C_{\mathsf{cov}},\log\lvert\Pi\rvert)
guarantees.

Figure 5.1:
Illustration of the lower bound from
Theorem
5.1
.
Blue
arrows represent taking the action
{\pi^{\star}}(x)
, while
red
arrows represent taking the action
1-{\pi^{\star}}(x)
.
Purple
arrows denote uniform transition to the states in the next layer, regardless of action. The MDP
M_{{\pi^{\star}},\phi}
is a uniform distribution of
2^{2H}
combination locks of two types. In the
relevant
combination locks (such as Lock 1 in the figure), following
{\pi^{\star}}
keeps one in the “good” chain and gives reward of
\mathrm{Ber}(3/4)
in the last layer, while deviating from
{\pi^{\star}}
leads one to the “bad” chain and gives reward of
\mathrm{Ber}(1/2)
. In
irrelevant
combination locks (such as Lock 50 in the figure), the next state is uniform regardless of action, and all rewards at the last layer are
\mathrm{Ber}(1/2)
.

Proof Sketch for
Theorem
5.1
.

We defer the full proof of
Theorem
5.1
to
Section
5.5.1
and sketch the main ideas here. An illustration of an MDP in the family
\mathcal{M}
can be found in
Figure
5.1
.

The basic building block for our lower bound is the combination lock, a prototypical construction used in prior works
[
KAL16
,
DKJADL19
,
SDMMS21
]
. The MDPs we construct are essentially a uniform distribution over
2^{2H}
different combination locks. In order to receive positive feedback, the learner must play a sequence of
H
correct actions in a particular combination lock. Intuitively, figuring out this sequence requires multiple revisits to the same lock. However, under online access, it is unlikely that the learner will get to see the same lock multiple times unless they use an exponential number of samples. Note that under generative access, this is not an issue, since the learner can reset to any state they like.

In more detail, each hard MDP
M_{{\pi^{\star}},\phi}\in\mathcal{M}
is parameterized by a policy
{\pi^{\star}}\in\Pi^{(\ell)}
(which is optimal for that MDP) and a decoder
\phi:\mathcal{X}\mapsto\{\textsc{Good},\textsc{Bad}\}
. In the MDP
M_{{\pi^{\star}},\phi}
there will be a
planted set
of “relevant” combination locks on which running
{\pi^{\star}}
achieves
\mathrm{Ber}(3/4)
reward; on the rest of the combination locks any policy
\pi\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{X}}
achieves
\mathrm{Ber}(1/2)
reward. Since the planted set is an
\varepsilon
-fraction of the total, the learner must solve an
\Omega(\varepsilon)
-fraction of the relevant locks in order to find an
O(\varepsilon)
-optimal policy. However, as we have established, the learner will never see the same combination lock multiple times, so their only hope is to try to identify
{\pi^{\star}}
through alternative means (e.g. via elimination).

In the vanilla combination lock, it becomes easy to learn
{\pi^{\star}}
via trajectory data, since once the learner observes a jump to the “bad” chain, they can immediately eliminate many candidate policies. Our construction utilizes a
contextual
variant of the combination lock which minimizes information leakage about
{\pi^{\star}}
from transition data. This is formalized by the decoder
\phi
, which randomly assigns states to be in the “good” chain and the “bad” chain. In this way, the learner, upon a single visit to a certain lock, cannot know when they have jumped to the “bad” chain (or even whether they are in a planted combination lock or not!) unless they can identify whether the reward at level
H
in that combination lock is
\mathrm{Ber}(1/2)
instead of
\mathrm{Ber}(3/4)
.

The last key to the puzzle is to prove that there exists such a policy class
\Pi^{(\ell)}
which satisfies these properties yet still has bounded spanning capacity. We reduce this problem to showing the existence of certain “block-free” binary matrices, whose existence is shown using the probabilistic method. Further discussion on this is deferred to
Section
5.5.1.5
.

Remark.
On a technical level, the lower bound argument relies on a stopping-time argument pioneered in the works
[
GMS19
,
DMKV21
,
SDMMS21
]
. It is possible to prove the result using the machinery of interactive lower bounds
[
CFHQRX24
]
, as is done in several other results in this thesis (e.g.,
Theorem
4.3
and
7.5
).

5.3  Spanning Capacity + Sunflower Property Enable Statistically Efficient Learning

The lower bound in
Theorem
5.1
suggests that further structural assumptions on
\Pi
are needed for statistically efficient agnostic PAC RL under the online interaction protocol. Essentially, the lower bound construction provided in
Theorem
5.1
is hard to learn because any two distinct policies
\pi,\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi
can differ substantially on a large subset of states (of size at least
\varepsilon\cdot 2^{2H}
). Thus, we cannot hope to learn “in parallel” via a low variance importance sampling strategy that utilizes extrapolation to evaluate all policies
\pi\in\Pi
.

In the sequel, we consider the following sunflower property to rule out such problematic scenarios, and show how bounded spanning capacity along with the sunflower property enable sample-efficient PAC RL with the online interaction protocol. The sunflower property only depends on the state space, action space, and policy class, and is independent of the transition dynamics and rewards of the underlying MDP. We first define a petal, a key ingredient of a sunflower.

Definition 5.2

(Petal)
.

For a policy set
\bar{\Pi}
, and states
\bar{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
, a policy
\pi
is said to be a
\bar{\mathcal{X}}
-petal on
\bar{\Pi}
if for all
1\leq h\leq h^{\prime}\leq H
, and partial trajectories
\tau=(x_{h},a_{h},\cdots,x_{h^{\prime}},a_{h^{\prime}})
that are consistent with
\pi
: either
\tau
is also consistent with some
\pi^{\prime}\in\bar{\Pi}
, or there exists
i\in(h,h^{\prime}]
s.t.
x_{i}\in\bar{\mathcal{X}}
.

Informally,
\pi
is a
\bar{\mathcal{X}}
-petal on
\bar{\Pi}
if any trajectory that can be obtained using
\pi
can either also be obtained using a policy in
\bar{\Pi}
or must pass through
\bar{\mathcal{X}}
. Thus, any policy that is a
\bar{\mathcal{X}}
-petal on
\bar{\Pi}
can only differentiate from
\bar{\Pi}
in a structured way. A policy class is said to be a sunflower if it is a union of petals as defined below:

Definition 5.3

(Sunflower)
.

A policy class
\Pi
is said to be a
(K,D)
-sunflower if there exists a set
\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}
of Markovian policies with
|\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}|\leq K
such that for every policy
\pi\in\Pi
there exists a set
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
, of size at most
D
, so that
\pi
is an
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}
-petal on
\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}
.

Our next theorem provides a sample complexity bound for agnostic PAC RL for policy classes that have
(K,D)
-sunflower structure. This bound is obtained via a new exploration algorithm called
\mathsf{POPLER}
that takes as input the set
\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}
and corresponding petals
\{\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\}_{\pi\in\Pi}
and leverages importance sampling as well as reachable state identification techniques to simultaneously estimate the value of every policy in
\Pi
. Algorithm details are deferred to
Section
5.3.1
.

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
\varepsilon,\delta>0
. Suppose the policy class
\Pi
satisfies
\mathfrak{C}(\Pi)
and is a
(K,D)
-sunflower. Then, for any MDP
M
, with probability at least
1-\delta
,
\mathsf{POPLER}
(
Algorithm
3
) succeeds in returning a policy
\widehat{\pi}
that satisfies
V^{\widehat{\pi}}\geq\max_{\pi\in\Pi}V^{\pi}-\varepsilon
, after collecting

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{HD^{6}\mathfrak{C}(\Pi)}{\varepsilon^{4}}\right)\cdot K^{2}\log\frac{|\Pi|}{\delta}\right)\quad\text{online trajectories in $M$.}

The proof of
Theorem
5.2
, and the corresponding hyperparameters in
\mathsf{POPLER}
needed to obtain the above bound, can be found in
Section
5.5.2
. Before diving into the algorithm and proof details, let us highlight several key aspects of the above sample complexity bound:

\bullet

Note that a class
\Pi
may be a
(K,D)
-sunflower for many different choices of
K
and
D
. For example, every policy class trivially satisfies the sunflower property with

\displaystyle\left(K=\min\{A^{H},\lvert\Pi\rvert\},D=0\right)\quad\text{or}\quad\left(K=0,D=\lvert\mathcal{X}\rvert\right).

These extremes correspond to a vanilla importance sampling approach or a tabular approach.
Barring computational issues, one can enumerate over all choices of
\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}
and
\{\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\}_{\pi\in\Pi}
and check if
\Pi
is a
(K,D)
-sunflower for that choice of
K=\lvert\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}\rvert
and
D=\max_{\pi\in\Pi}\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\rvert
. Since our bound in
Theorem
5.2
scales with
K
and
D
, we are free to choose
K
and
D
to minimize the corresponding sample complexity bound. In this sense, the sunflower property maps out a
pareto frontier of algorithms
which interpolate between the vanilla importance sampling and tabular approaches.

\bullet

In order to achieve polynomial sample complexity in
Theorem
5.2
, both
\mathfrak{C}(\Pi)
and
(K,D)
are required to be
\mathrm{poly}(H,\log\lvert\Pi\rvert)
. All of the policy classes considered in
Section
2.4.2
have the sunflower property, with both
K,D=\mathrm{poly}(H)
, and thus our sample complexity bound apply to all these classes. See
Section
5.3.2
for details.

\bullet

Notice that for
Theorem
5.2
to hold, we need both bounded spanning capacity as well as the sunflower structure on the policy class with bounded
(K,D)
. Thus, one may wonder if we can obtain a similar polynomial sample complexity guarantee in online RL under weaker assumptions. In
Theorem
5.1
, we already showed that bounded
\mathfrak{C}(\Pi)
alone is not sufficient to obtain polynomial sample complexity in online RL. Likewise, as we show in
Section
5.3.2
, sunflower property with bounded
(K,D)
alone is also not sufficient for polynomial sample complexity, and hence both assumptions cannot be individually removed. However, it is an interesting question if there is some other structural assumption that combines both spanning capacity and the sunflower property, and is both sufficient and necessary for agnostic PAC learning in online RL. See
Section
5.4
for further discussions on this.

How does the sunflower property enable sample-efficient learning?

Intuitively, the sunflower property captures the intuition of simultaneous estimation of all policies
\pi\in\Pi
via importance sampling (IS), and allows control of both bias and variance. Let
\pi
be a
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}
-petal on
\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}
. Any trajectory
\tau\rightsquigarrow\pi
that avoids
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}
must be consistent with some policy in
\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}
, and will thus be covered by the data collected using
\pi^{\prime}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\Pi_{\mathrm{core}})
. Thus, using IS with variance scaling with
K
, one can create a biased estimator for
V^{\pi}
, where the bias is
only due
to trajectories that pass through
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}
. There are two cases.

•

If every state in
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}
has small reachability under
\pi
, i.e.
d^{\pi}(x)\ll\varepsilon
for every
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}
, then the IS estimate will have a low bias (linear in
\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\rvert
), so we can compute
V^{\pi}
up to error at most
\varepsilon\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\rvert
.

•

On the other hand, if
d^{\pi}(x)
is large for some
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}
, it is possible to explicitly control the bias that arises from trajectories passing through them since there are at most
D
of them. We accomplish this by explicit exploration starting from this state
x
.

5.3.1  Algorithm and Proof Ideas

\mathsf{POPLER}
(
Algorithm
3
) takes as input a policy class
\Pi
, as well as sets
\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}
and
\{\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\}_{\pi\in\Pi}
, which can be computed beforehand by enumeration.
\mathsf{POPLER}
has two phases: a
state identification phase
, where it finds “petal” states
x\in\bigcup_{\pi\in\Pi}\mathcal{X}_{\pi}
that are reachable with sufficiently large probability; and an
evaluation phase
where it computes estimates
\widehat{V}^{\pi}
for every
\pi\in\Pi
. It uses three subroutines
\mathsf{DataCollector}
,
\mathsf{EstReachability}
, and
\mathsf{Evaluate}
, whose pseudocodes are stated in
Section
5.5.2.1
.

Key Tool: Policy-Specific Markov Reward Process (MRP).

MRPs are key technical tools used by our main algorithm. An MRP
\mathfrak{M}=\mathrm{MRP}(\mathcal{X},P,R,H,x_{\top},x_{\bot})
is defined over the state space
\mathcal{X}
with start state
x_{\top}
and end state
x_{\bot}
, for trajectory length
H+2
. Without loss of generality, we assume that
\{x_{\top},x_{\bot}\}\in\mathcal{X}
. The transition kernel is denoted by
P:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to[0,1]
, such that for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
,
\sum_{x^{\prime}}P_{x\to x^{\prime}}=1
. The reward kernel is denoted
R:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\Delta([0,1])
. Throughout, we use the notation
\rightarrow
to signify that the transitions and rewards are defined along the edges of the MRP. At an intuitive level, an MRP is an MDP with singleton action space, i.e. the actions have no effect. For technical reasons, the state space in the MRPs is not layered.

Now we elaborate on the MRP construction, which is the key technical tool used in both the identification and evaluation phases of the algorithm. To build intuition, let us consider a fixed policy
\pi\in\Pi
with petal states
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}
and define a population version of policy-specific MRP. In particular, let
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{+}=\mathcal{X}\cup\{x_{\bot},x_{\top}\}
, and associated with
\pi
define
\mathfrak{M}^{\pi}=\mathrm{MRP}(\mathcal{X}^{+}_{\pi},P^{\pi},R^{\pi},H,x_{\top},x_{\bot})
which essentially compresses the transition and reward information in the original MDP relevant to the policy
\pi
.

For any states
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\cup\{x_{\top}\}
and
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\cup\{x_{\bot}\}
residing in different layers
h<h^{\prime}
in the underlying MDP,
1
1

1

For the simplicity of analysis, we slightly abuse the notation and assume that all trajectories in the underlying MDP start at
x_{\top}
at time step
0
and terminate at
x_{\bot}
at time step
H+1
, and do not observe
x_{\bot}
and
x_{\top}
in between from time steps
h=1,\dots,H
. However, recall that
x_{\bot}
and
x_{\top}
are not part of the original layered state space
\mathcal{X}
for the MDP.
we define:

•

Transition
\bm{P_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}}
as:

\displaystyle P_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}\coloneqq\mathbb{P}^{\pi}\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}$ goes from $x$ to $x^{\prime}$}\\
\text{without passing through any other $x^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}$}\end{subarray}\mid\text{$\tau_{h}=x$}\right].

•

Rewards
\bm{R_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}}
as:

\displaystyle R_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}\coloneqq\En^{\pi}\left[R(\tau_{h:h^{\prime}})\mathbbm{1}\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$\tau_{h,h^{\prime}}$ goes from $x$ to $x^{\prime}$}\\
\text{without passing through any other $x^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}$ }\end{subarray}\right\}\mid\text{$\tau_{h}=x$}\right].

where
\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}
denotes a partial trajectory from layer
h
to
h^{\prime}
, and
R(\tau_{h:h^{\prime}})
denotes the cumulative rewards from layer
h
to
h^{\prime}
along the partial trajectory
\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}
. Furthermore,

P_{x_{\bot}\to x_{\bot}}^{\pi}=1
and
R_{x_{\bot}\to x_{\bot}}^{\pi}=0
.

The key technical benefit of using policy-specific MRPs is that the value
V^{\pi}
for the policy
\pi
in the original MDP is identical to the value of policy-specific MRP
\mathfrak{M}^{\pi}
(starting from
x_{\bot}
). Thus, if one knew the transitions and rewards in
\mathfrak{M}^{\pi}
, one could calculate the value of the policy
\pi
via dynamic programming on
\mathfrak{M}^{\pi}
. Of course, we do not know these quantities, so we must estimate them by interacting with the original MDP. A naive approach is to simply run
\pi
many times to get estimates for each transition and reward—but since we want to estimate
V^{\pi}
for every
\pi\in\Pi
simultaneously, this approach would incur an
\lvert\Pi\rvert
dependency in the sample complexity. Instead, our algorithm uses importance sampling to estimate transitions and rewards in the corresponding
\mathfrak{M}^{\pi}
for many policies simultaneously.

We next describe the key algorithmic ideas, as well as the empirical estimation of policy-specific MRPs, in the two phases of
Algorithm
3
.

Algorithm 3

P
olicy
OP
timization by
L
earning
\bm{\varepsilon}
-
R
eachable States (
\mathsf{POPLER}
)

1:

Policy class
\Pi
, Sets
\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}
and
\{\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\}_{\pi\in\Pi}
, Parameters
K,D,n_{1},n_{2},\varepsilon,\delta
.

2:

Define an additional start state
x_{\top}
(at
h=0
) and end state
x_{\bot}
(at
h=H+1
).

3:

Initialize
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}=\{x_{\top}\}
,
\mathcal{T}\leftarrow\{(x_{\top},\mathrm{Null})\}
; for every
\pi\in\Pi
, set
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{+}\vcentcolon={}\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\cup\{x_{\top},x_{\bot}\}
.

4:

\mathcal{D}_{\top}\leftarrow\mathsf{DataCollector}(x_{\top},\mathrm{Null},\Pi_{\mathrm{core}},n_{1})

5:

/*
Identification of Petal States that are Reachable with
\Omega(\varepsilon/D)
Probability */

6:

while

\mathsf{Terminate}=\mathsf{False}

do

7:

Set
\mathsf{Terminate}=\mathsf{True}
.

8:

for

\pi\in\Pi

do

9:

Compute the sets:

\displaystyle\text{already-explored reachable states:}\quad\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rch}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{X}^{+}_{\pi}\cap\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}

\displaystyle\text{remaining states:}\quad\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\setminus(\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rch}}\cup\{x_{\bot}\})

10:

Estimate the policy-specific MRP
\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}^{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}}
according to (
5.1
) and (
5.2
).

11:

for

\bar{x}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}}

do

12:

Estimate prob of reaching
\bar{x}
under
\pi
:
\widehat{d}^{\pi}(\bar{x})\leftarrow\mathsf{EstReachability}(\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{+},\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}^{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}},\bar{x})
.

13:

if

\widehat{d}^{\pi}(\bar{x})\geq\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{6D}}

then

14:

Update
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}\leftarrow\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}\cup\{\bar{x}\}
,
\mathcal{T}\leftarrow\mathcal{T}\cup\{(\bar{x},\pi)\}
and set
\mathsf{Terminate}=\mathsf{False}
.

15:

Collect dataset
\mathcal{D}_{\bar{x}}\leftarrow\mathsf{DataCollector}(\bar{x},\pi,\Pi_{\mathrm{core}},n_{2})
.

16:

/*
Policy Evaluation and Optimization */

17:

for

\pi\in\Pi

do

18:

\widehat{V}^{\pi}\leftarrow\mathsf{Evaluate}(\Pi_{\mathrm{core}},\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}},\{\mathcal{D}_{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}},\pi)
.

19:

Return

\widehat{\pi}\in\argmax_{\pi}\widehat{V}^{\pi}
.

State Identification Phase

The goal of the state identification phase is to discover all such petal states that are reachable with probability
\Omega(\varepsilon/D)
. The algorithm proceeds in a loop and sequentially grows the set
\mathcal{T}
, which contains tuples of the form
(x,\pi_{x})
, where
x\in\bigcup_{\pi\in\Pi}\mathcal{X}_{\pi}
is a sufficiently reachable petal state (for some policy) and
\pi_{x}
denotes a policy that reaches
x
with probability
\Omega(\varepsilon/D)
. We also denote
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}\coloneqq\left\{x:(x,\pi_{x})\in\mathcal{T}\right\}
to denote the set of reachable states in
\mathcal{T}
. Initially,
\mathcal{T}
only contains a dummy start state
x_{\top}
and a null policy. We will collect data using the
\mathsf{DataCollector}
subroutine that: for a given
(x,\pi_{x})\in\mathcal{T}
, first run
\pi_{x}
to reach state
x
, and if we succeed in reaching
x
, restart exploration by sampling a policy from
\mathrm{Unif}(\Pi_{\mathrm{exp}})
. Note that
\mathsf{DataCollector}
will be sample-efficient for any
(x,\pi_{x})
since
\Omega(\varepsilon/D)
fraction of the trajectories obtained via
\pi_{x}
are guaranteed to reach
x
(by definition of
\pi_{x}
and construction of set
\mathcal{T}
). Initially, we run
\mathsf{DataCollector}
using
\mathrm{Unif}(\Pi_{\mathrm{exp}})
from the start, where we slightly abuse the notation and assume that all trajectories in the MDP start at the dummy state
x_{\top}
at time step
h=0
.

In every loop, the algorithm attempts to find a new petal state
\bar{x}
for some
\pi\in\Pi
that is guaranteed to be
\Omega(\varepsilon/D)
-reachable by
\pi
. This is accomplished by constructing a (estimated and partial) version of the policy-specific MRP using the datasets collected up until that loop (
line
10
). In particular given a policy
\pi
and a set
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rch}}=\mathcal{X}^{+}_{\pi}\cap\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}
, we construct
\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}^{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}}=\mathrm{MRP}(\mathcal{X}^{+}_{\pi},\widehat{P}^{\pi},\widehat{R}^{\pi},H,x_{\top},x_{\bot})
which essentially compresses our empirical knowledge of the original MDP relevant to the policy
\pi
. In particular, for any states
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\cup\{x_{\top}\}
and
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\cup\{x_{\bot}\}
residing in different layers
h<h^{\prime}
in the underlying MDP, we define:

\bullet

Transition
\bm{\widehat{P}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}}
as:

\displaystyle\widehat{P}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{D}_{x}|}\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{D}_{x}}\frac{\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi\rightsquigarrow\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}\right\}}{\tfrac{1}{|\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}|}\sum_{\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}\right\}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}$ goes from $x$ to $x^{\prime}$}\\
\text{without passing through any other $x^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}$}\end{subarray}\right\}.

(5.1)

\bullet

Transition
\bm{\widehat{R}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}}
as:

\displaystyle\widehat{R}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{D}_{x}|}\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{D}_{x}}\frac{R(\tau_{h:h^{\prime}})\cdot\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi\rightsquigarrow\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}\right\}}{\tfrac{1}{|\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}|}\sum_{\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}\right\}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}$ goes from $x$ to $x^{\prime}$}\\
\text{without passing through any other $x^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}$}\end{subarray}\right\}.

(5.2)

Clearly, the above definition implies that
\widehat{P}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}=0
and
\widehat{R}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}=0
for any
x\notin\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rch}}
since
\mathcal{D}_{x}
would be empty corresponding to these unexplored states. Furthermore,
P_{x_{\bot}\to x_{\bot}}^{\pi}=1
and
R_{x_{\bot}\to x_{\bot}}^{\pi}=0
.

Note that since the set
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}
is changing in each iteration of the loop as the algorithm collects more data, the policy-specific MRP
\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}^{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}}
also changes in every iteration of the loop — in particular, more and more transitions/rewards are assigned nonzero values due to new states being added to
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}
. More details on policy-specific MRPs is given in
Section
5.5.2.1
.

The key advantage of constructing the empirical versions of policy-specific MRPs is that they allow us to explore and find new leaf states in
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}
which are reachable with probability at least
\Omega(\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{6D}})
. In particular, using standard dynamic programming (subroutine
\mathsf{EstReachability}
), we can check whether a candidate petal
\bar{x}
is reachable with decent probability by
\pi
(lines
12
-
13
); If it is, then we add
(\bar{x},\pi)
to the set
\mathcal{T}
and collect a fresh dataset using
\mathsf{DataCollector}
(lines
14
-
15
). Crucially, the importance sampling technique enables us to be sample efficient, since the same dataset
\mathcal{D}_{x}
can be used to evaluate transitions/rewards in Eqs. (
5.1
) and (
5.2
) for multiple
\pi\in\Pi
for which
x
is a petal state. Furthermore, the number of such datasets we collect must be bounded—each
(x,\pi_{x})\in\mathcal{T}
contributes
\Omega(\varepsilon/D)
to cumulative reachability, but since cumulative reachability is bounded from above by
\mathfrak{C}(\Pi)
(
Lemma
2.1
), we know that
\lvert\mathcal{T}\rvert\leq\mathcal{O}(D\cdot\mathfrak{C}(\Pi)/\varepsilon)
.

Evaluation Phase

Next,
\mathsf{POPLER}
moves to the evaluation phase. Using the collected data, it executes the
\mathsf{Evaluate}
subroutine for every
\pi\in\Pi
to get estimates
\widehat{V}^{\pi}
(
line
18
) corresponding to
V^{\pi}
. For a given
\pi\in\Pi
, the
\mathsf{Evaluate}
subroutine also constructs an empirical policy-specific MRP
\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}^{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}}
for every
\pi\in\Pi
and computes the value of
\pi
via dynamic programming on
\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}^{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}}
. While the returned estimate
\widehat{V}^{\pi}
is biased, in the complete proof, we will show that the bias is negligible since it is now only due to the states in the petal
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}
which are
not

\Omega(\varepsilon/D)
-reachable. Thus, we can guarantee that
\widehat{V}^{\pi}
closely estimates
V^{\pi}
for every
\pi\in\Pi
, and therefore
\mathsf{POPLER}
returns a near-optimal policy.

5.3.2  Sunflower Property Examples

We will show that the examples in
Section
2.4.2
satisfy the sunflower property (
Definition
5.3
) with small
K
and
D
. At the end, we also give an example of a policy class which satisfies the sunflower property but has large spanning capacity, thus indicating that sunflower property itself is insufficient for sample-efficient RL.

Tabular MDP.

We choose
\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}=\{\pi_{a}:\pi_{a}(x)=a,a\in\mathcal{A}\}
to be the set of policies which play the constant
a
for each
a\in\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}=\mathcal{X}
for every
\pi\in\Pi
, then any partial trajectory which satisfies the condition in
Definition
5.3
is of the form
(x_{h},a_{h})
, which is consistent with
\pi_{a_{h}}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}
. Hence
\Pi
is a
(A,\lvert\mathcal{X}\rvert)
-sunflower.

Contextual Bandit.

We choose
\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}=\{\pi_{a}:\pi_{a}(x)\equiv a,a\in\mathcal{A}\}
, and
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}=\emptyset
for every
\pi\in\Pi
, then any partial trajectory which satisfies the condition in
Definition
5.3
is in the form
(x,a)
, which is consistent with
\pi_{a}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}
. Hence
\Pi
is a
(A,0)
-sunflower.

H
-Layer Contextual Bandit.

We choose

\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}=\{\pi_{a_{1},\cdots,a_{H}}:\pi_{a_{1},\cdots,a_{H}}(x_{h})\equiv a_{h},a_{1},\cdots,a_{H}\in\mathcal{A}\}

and
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}=\emptyset
for every
\pi\in\Pi
, then any partial trajectory which satisfies the condition in
Definition
5.3
is in the form
(x_{1},a_{1},\cdots,x_{H},a_{H})
, which is consistent with
\pi_{a_{1},a_{2},\cdots,a_{H}}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}
. Hence
\Pi
is a
(A^{H},0)
-sunflower.

\ell
-tons.

We choose

\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}=\{\pi_{0}\}\cup\{\pi_{h}:1\leq h\leq H\},

where
\pi_{0}(x)\equiv 0
, and
\pi_{h}
chooses the action
1
on all the states at layer
h
, i.e.,
\pi_{h}(x)\coloneqq\mathbbm{1}\left\{x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}\right\}
. For every
\pi\in\Pi_{\ell}
, we choose
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}
to be the states for which
\pi(x)=1
(there are at most
\ell
such states). Fix any partial trajectory
\tau=(x_{h},a_{h}\cdots,x_{h^{\prime}},a_{h^{\prime}})
which satisfies
\pi\rightsquigarrow\tau
. Suppose that for all
i\in(h,h^{\prime}]
,
x_{i}\not\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}
. Then we must have
a_{i}=0
for all
i\in(h,h^{\prime}]
. Hence
\pi_{h}\rightsquigarrow\tau
(if
a_{h}=1
) or
\pi_{0}\rightsquigarrow\tau
(if
a_{h}=0
), and
\tau
is consistent with some policy in
\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}
. Therefore,
\Pi_{\ell}
is an
(H+1,\ell)
-sunflower.

1
-Active Policies.

We choose
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}=\{x_{(1,1)},x_{(1,2)},\cdots,x_{(1,H)}\}
for all
\pi\in\Pi
as well as

\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}=\{\pi_{0}\}\cup\{\pi_{h}:1\leq h\leq H\},

where
\pi_{0}(x)\equiv 0
and
\pi_{h}(x)\coloneqq\mathbbm{1}\left\{x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}\right\}
. Fix any partial trajectory
\tau=(x_{h},a_{h},\cdots,x_{h^{\prime}},a_{h^{\prime}})
which satisfies
\pi\rightsquigarrow\tau
. If we have
i\in(h,h^{\prime}]
,
x_{i}\not\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}
, then we must have
a_{i}=0
. Thus,
\pi_{h}\rightsquigarrow\tau
(if
a_{h}=1
) or
\pi_{0}\rightsquigarrow\tau
(if
a_{h}=0
), so
\tau
is consistent with some policy in
\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}
. Therefore,
\Pi_{\mathrm{1-act}}
is a
(H+1,H)
-sunflower.

All-Active Policies.

We choose
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}=\{x_{(j,1)},\cdots,x_{(j,H)}\}
for all
\pi\in\Pi_{j\mathrm{-act}}
, as well as

\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}=\{\pi_{0}\}\cup\{\pi_{h}:1\leq h\leq H\},

where
\pi_{0}(x)\coloneqq 0
and
\pi_{h}(x)\coloneqq\mathbbm{1}\left\{x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}\right\}
. Fix any partial trajectory
\tau=(x_{h},a_{h}\cdots,x_{h^{\prime}},a_{h^{\prime}})
which satisfies
\pi\rightsquigarrow\tau
. If we have
i\in(h,h^{\prime}]
,
x_{i}\not\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}
, then we must have
a_{i}=0
. Thus,
\pi_{h}\rightsquigarrow\tau
(if
a_{h}=1
) or
\pi_{0}\rightsquigarrow\tau
(if
a_{h}=0
), so
\tau
is consistent with some policy in
\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}
. Therefore,
\Pi_{\mathrm{act}}
is a
(H+1,H)
-sunflower.

Sunflower property is insufficient by itself.

We give an example of a policy class
\Pi
for which the sunflower property holds for
K,D=\mathrm{poly}(H)
but
\mathfrak{C}(\Pi)=2^{H}
. Therefore, in light of
Theorem
4.2
, the sunflower property by itself cannot ensure statistically efficient agnostic PAC RL in the online access model.

The example is as follows: Consider a binary tree MDP with
2^{H}-1
states and action space
\mathcal{A}=\{0,1\}
. The policy class
\Pi
will be able to get to every
(x,a)
pair in layer
H
. To define the policies, we consider each possible trajectory
\tau=(x_{1},a_{1},\cdots,x_{H},a_{H})
and let:

\displaystyle\Pi\coloneqq\left\{\pi_{\tau}:\pi_{\tau}(x)=\begin{cases}a_{i}&\text{if }x_{i}\in\tau,\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}\right\}.

Thus it is clear that
\mathfrak{C}(\Pi)=2^{H}
, but the sunflower property holds with
K=1
,
D=H
by taking
\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}=\{\pi_{0}\}
(the policy which always picks
a=0
).

5.4  Open Problem: Sample Complexity of Online RL

The results in this chapter establish when learning is possible in online RL, but fall short of giving a complete characterization of learnability. We highlight two concrete directions for progress on this question.

Question 5.1
.

Are policy classes with bounded spanning capacity “almost learnable”: can we show a sample complexity guarantee of the form
n_{\mathsf{on}}(\Pi;\varepsilon,\delta)\lesssim\varepsilon^{-O(\log\mathfrak{C}(\Pi))}
?

In conjunction with the other results in this thesis, this would establish that the minimax sample complexity of agnostic policy learning with online RL interaction (ignoring other problem parameters) is:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{C}(\Pi)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot\log\frac{1}{\delta}\lesssim n_{\mathsf{on}}(\Pi;\varepsilon,\delta)\lesssim\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{O(\log\mathfrak{C}(\Pi)}}\cdot\log\frac{\lvert\Pi\rvert}{\delta},

and that neither the upper bound nor the lower bound can be improved significantly in terms of the dependence on
\mathfrak{C}(\Pi)
. We suspect that one might be able to extend the algorithmic ideas of our upper bound in
Theorem
5.2
to discover an MDP-dependent sunflower core/petal set. It would also be interesting to try to improve the lower bound: as we discuss in
Section
5.5.1.5
, the current construction via “block-free” matrices cannot be readily improved, but alternative constructions might yield stronger lower bounds.

Question 5.2
.

Is (some variant of) the sunflower property necessary for online RL? More formally, for any policy class
\Pi
, is the sample complexity in online RL at least

\displaystyle\min_{K,D\in\mathbb{N}}\penalty\ \{K+D\}\quad\text{such that $\Pi$ is a $(K,D)$-sunflower}?

We have strong evidence: this statement is true for every example considered in this thesis.

5.5  Deferred Proofs

5.5.1  Proof of
Theorem
5.1

Now we prove
Theorem
5.1
. We restate the theorem below with the precise constants:

Theorem
5.1

(Lower Bound for Online RL)
.

Let
h_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
and
c\in(0,1)
be universal constants. Fix any
H\geq h_{0}
. Let
\varepsilon\in(1/2^{cH},1/(100H))
and
\ell\in\{2,\dots,H\}
such that
1/\varepsilon^{\ell}\leq 2^{H}
. There exists a policy class
\Pi^{(\ell)}
of size
1/(6\varepsilon^{\ell})
with
\mathfrak{C}(\Pi^{(\ell)})\leq O(H^{4\ell+2})
and a family of MDPs
\mathcal{M}
with state space
\mathcal{X}
of size
H\cdot 2^{2H+1}
, binary action space, horizon
H
such that: for any
(\varepsilon/16,1/8)
-PAC algorithm, there exists an
M\in\mathcal{M}
in which the algorithm has to collect at least

\displaystyle\min\left\{\frac{1}{120\varepsilon^{\ell}},2^{H/3-3}\right\}\quad\text{online trajectories in expectation.}

5.5.1.1  Construction of State Space, Action Space, and Policy Class

State and Action Spaces.

We define the state space
\mathcal{X}
. In every layer
h\in[H]
, there will be
2^{2H+1}
states. The states will be paired up, and each state will be denoted by either
j[h]
or
j^{\prime}[h]
, so
\mathcal{X}_{h}=\{j[h]:j\in[2^{2H}]\}\cup\{j^{\prime}[h]:j\in[2^{2H}]\}
. For any state
x\in\mathcal{X}
, we define the
index
of
x
, denoted
\mathrm{idx}(x)
as the unique
j\in[2^{2H}]
such that
x\in\{j[h]\}_{h\in[H]}\cup\{j^{\prime}[h]\}_{h\in[H]}
. In total there are
H\cdot 2^{2H+1}
states. The action space is
\mathcal{A}=\{0,1\}
.

Policy Class.

For the given
\varepsilon
and
\ell\in\{2,\dots,H\}
, we show via a probabilistic argument the existence of a large policy class
\Pi^{(\ell)}
which has bounded spanning capacity but is hard to explore. We state several properties in
Lemma
5.1
which will be exploited in the lower bound.

We introduce some additional notation. For any
j\in[2^{2H}]
we denote

\displaystyle\Pi^{(\ell)}_{j}\coloneqq\{\pi\in\Pi^{(\ell)}:\exists h\in[H],\pi(j[h])=1\},

that is,
\Pi^{(\ell)}_{j}
are the policies which take an action
a=1
on at least one state with index
j
.

We also define the set of
relevant state indices
for a given policy
\pi\in\Pi^{(\ell)}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{rel}}^{\pi}\coloneqq\{j\in[2^{2H}]:\pi\in\Pi^{(\ell)}_{j}\}.

For any policy
\pi
we denote
\pi(j_{1:H})\coloneqq(\pi(j[1]),\dots,\pi(j[H]))\in\{0,1\}^{H}
to be the vector that represents the actions that
\pi
takes on the states in index
j
. The vector
\pi(j^{\prime}_{1:H})
is defined similarly.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
H
,
\varepsilon
, and
\ell
satisfy the assumptions of
Theorem
5.1
. There exists a policy class
\Pi^{(\ell)}
of size
N=1/(6\varepsilon^{\ell})
which satisfies the following properties.

(1)

For every
j\in[2^{2H}]
we have
\lvert\Pi^{(\ell)}_{j}\rvert\in[\varepsilon N/2,2\varepsilon N]
.

(2)

For every
\pi\in\Pi
we have
\lvert\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{rel}}^{\pi}\rvert\geq\varepsilon/2\cdot 2^{2H}
.

(3)

For every
\pi\in\Pi^{(\ell)}_{j}
, the vector
\pi(j_{1:H})
is unique and always equal to
\pi(j^{\prime}_{1:H})
.

(4)

Bounded spanning capacity:
\mathfrak{C}(\Pi^{(\ell)})\leq c\cdot H^{4\ell+2}
for some universal constant
c>0
.

5.5.1.2  Construction of MDP Family

The family
\mathcal{M}=\{M_{{\pi^{\star}},\phi}\}_{{\pi^{\star}}\in\Pi^{(\ell)},\phi\in\Phi}
will be a family of MDPs which are indexed by a policy
{\pi^{\star}}
as well as a
decoder
function
\phi:\mathcal{X}\mapsto\{\textsc{good},\textsc{bad}\}
, which assigns each state to be “good” or “bad” in a sense that will be described later on. An example construction of an MDP
M_{{\pi^{\star}},\phi}
is illustrated in
Figure
5.1
. For brevity, the bracket notation used to denote the layer that each state lies in has been omitted in the figure.

Decoder Function Class.

The decoder function class
\Phi
will be the set of all possible mappings which for every
j\in[2^{2H}]
and
h\geq 2
assign exactly one of
j[h]
or
j^{\prime}[h]
to the label
Good
(where the other is assigned to the label
Bad
). There are
(2^{H-1})^{2^{2H}}
such functions. The label of a state will be used to describe the transition dynamics. Intuitively, a learner who does not know the decoder function
\phi
will not be able to tell if a certain state has the label
Good
or
Bad
when visiting that state for the first time.

Transition Dynamics.

The MDP
M_{{\pi^{\star}},\phi}
will be a uniform distribution over
2^{2H}
combination locks
\{\mathsf{CL}_{j}\}_{j\in[2^{2H}]}
with disjoint states. More formally,
x_{1}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\{j[1]\}_{j\in[2^{2H}]})
. From each start state
j[1]
, only the
2H-2
states corresponding to index
j
at layers
h\geq 2
will be reachable in the combination lock
\mathsf{CL}_{j}
.

In the following, we will describe each combination lock
\mathsf{CL}_{j}
, which forms the basic building block of the MDP construction.

\bullet

Good/Bad Set.
At every layer
h\in[H]
, for each
j[h]
and
j^{\prime}[h]
, the decoder function
\phi
assigns one of them to be
Good
and one of them to be
Bad
. We will henceforth denote
j_{g}[h]
to be the good state and
j_{b}[h]
to be the bad state. Observe that by construction in Eq. (
5.6
), for every
\pi\in\Pi^{(\ell)}
and
h\in[H]
we have
\pi(j_{g}[h])=\pi(j_{b}[h])
.

\bullet

Dynamics of
\mathsf{CL}_{j}
, if
j\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{rel}}^{\pi^{\star}}
.
Here, the transition dynamics of the combination locks are deterministic. For every
h\in[H]
,

–

On good states
j_{g}[h]
we transit to the next good state iff the action is
{\pi^{\star}}
:

\displaystyle P(x^{\prime}\penalty\ |\penalty\ j_{g}[h],a)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathbbm{1}\left\{x^{\prime}=j_{g}[h+1]\right\},&\text{if }a={\pi^{\star}}(j_{g}[h])\\
\mathbbm{1}\left\{x^{\prime}=j_{b}[h+1]\right\},&\text{if }a\neq{\pi^{\star}}(j_{g}[h]).\end{cases}

–

On bad states
j_{b}[h]
we always transit to the next bad state:

\displaystyle P(x^{\prime}\penalty\ |\penalty\ j_{b}[h],a)=\mathbbm{1}\left\{x^{\prime}=j_{b}[h+1]\right\},\quad\text{for all }a\in\mathcal{A}.

\bullet

Dynamics of
\mathsf{CL}_{j}
, if
j\notin\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{rel}}^{\pi^{\star}}
.
If
j
is not a relevant index for
{\pi^{\star}}
, then the transitions are uniformly random regardless of the current state/action. For every
h\in[H]
,

\displaystyle P(\cdot\penalty\ |\penalty\ j_{g}[h],a)=P(\cdot\penalty\ |\penalty\ j_{b}[h],a)=\mathrm{Unif}\left(\{j_{g}[h+1],j_{b}[h+1]\}\right),\quad\text{for all }a\in\mathcal{A}.

\bullet

Reward Structure.
The reward function is nonzero only at layer
H
, and is defined as

\displaystyle R(x,a)=\mathrm{Ber}\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{4}\cdot\mathbbm{1}\{{\pi^{\star}}\in\Pi^{(\ell)}_{j}\}\cdot\mathbbm{1}\{x=j_{g}[H],a={\pi^{\star}}(j_{g}[H])\}\right)

That is, we get
3/4
whenever we reach the
H
-th good state for an index
j
which is relevant for
{\pi^{\star}}
, and
1/2
reward otherwise.

Reference MDPs.

We define several reference MDPs.

\bullet

In the reference MDP
M_{0}
, the initial start state is again taken to be the uniform distribution, i.e.,
x_{1}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\{j[1]\}_{j\in[2^{2H}]})
, and all the combination locks behave the same and have uniform transitions to the next state along the chain: for every
h\in[H]
and
j\in[2^{2H}]
,

\displaystyle P(\cdot\penalty\ |\penalty\ j[h],a)=P(\cdot\penalty\ |\penalty\ j^{\prime}[h],a)=\mathrm{Unif}\left(\{j[h+1],j^{\prime}[h+1]\}\right),\quad\text{for all }a\in\mathcal{A}.

The rewards for
M_{0}
are
\mathrm{Ber}(1/2)
for every
(x,a)\in\mathcal{X}_{H}\times\mathcal{A}
.

\bullet

For any decoder
\phi\in\Phi
, the reference MDP
M_{0,{\pi^{\star}},\phi}
has the same transitions as
M_{{\pi^{\star}},\phi}
but the rewards are
\mathrm{Ber}(1/2)
for every
(x,a)\in\mathcal{X}_{H}\times\mathcal{A}
.

5.5.1.3  Proof of
Theorem
5.1

We are now ready to prove the lower bound using the construction of the MDP family
\mathcal{M}
.

Value Calculation.

Consider any
M_{{\pi^{\star}},\phi}\in\mathcal{M}
. For any policy
\pi\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{X}}
we use
V_{{\pi^{\star}},\phi}(\pi)
to denote the value of running
\pi
in MDP
M_{{\pi^{\star}},\phi}
. By construction we can see that

\displaystyle V_{{\pi^{\star}},\phi}(\pi)=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{4}\cdot\Pr_{{\pi^{\star}},\phi}\left[\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{rel}}^{\pi^{\star}}\text{ and }\pi(\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})_{1:H})={\pi^{\star}}(\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})_{1:H})\right],

(5.3)

where in the above, we defined for any
x_{1}
,
\pi(\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})_{1:H})\ =\pi(j_{1:H})=(\pi(j[1]),\dots,\pi(j[H]))
, where
j
denotes
\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})
. Informally speaking, the second term counts the additional reward that
\pi
gets for solving a combination lock rooted at a relevant state index
\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{rel}}^{\pi^{\star}}
. By Property (2) and (3) of
Lemma
5.1
, we additionally have
V_{{\pi^{\star}},\phi}({\pi^{\star}})\geq 1/2+\varepsilon/8
, as well as
V_{{\pi^{\star}},\phi}(\pi)=1/2
for all other
\pi\neq{\pi^{\star}}\in\Pi^{(\ell)}
.

By Eq. (
5.3
), if
\pi
is an
\varepsilon/16
-optimal policy on
M_{{\pi^{\star}},\phi}
it must satisfy

\displaystyle\Pr_{{\pi^{\star}},\phi}\left[\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{rel}}^{\pi^{\star}}\text{ and }\pi(\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})_{1:H})={\pi^{\star}}(\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})_{1:H})\right]\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}.

Averaged Measures.

We define the following measures which will be used in the analysis.

\bullet

Define
\Pr_{\pi^{\star}}[\cdot]=\frac{1}{\lvert\Phi\rvert}\sum_{\phi\in\Phi}\Pr_{{\pi^{\star}},\phi}[\cdot]
to be the averaged measure where we first pick
\phi
uniformly among all decoders and then consider the distribution induced by
M_{{\pi^{\star}},\phi}
.

\bullet

Define the averaged measure
\Pr_{0,{\pi^{\star}}}[\cdot]=\frac{1}{\lvert\Phi\rvert}\sum_{\phi\in\Phi}\Pr_{0,{\pi^{\star}},\phi}[\cdot]
where we pick
\phi
uniformly and then consider the distribution induced by
M_{0,{\pi^{\star}},\phi}
.

For both averaged measures the expectations
\En_{{\pi^{\star}}}
and
\En_{0,{\pi^{\star}}}
are defined analogously.

Algorithm and Stopping Time.

Recall that an algorithm
\mathsf{Alg}
is comprised of two phases. In the first phase, it collects some number of trajectories by interacting with the MDP in episodes. We use
\eta
to denote the (random) number of episodes after which
\mathsf{Alg}
terminates. We also use
\mathsf{Alg}_{t}
to denote the intermediate policy that the algorithm runs in round
t
for
t\in[\eta]
. In the second phase,
\mathsf{Alg}
outputs
2
2

2

We present the lower bound for the class of deterministic algorithms that output a deterministic policy. However, all the arguments could be extended to stochastic algorithms.
a policy
{\widehat{\pi}}
. We use the notation
\mathsf{Alg}_{f}:\{\tau^{(t)}\}_{t\in[\eta]}\mapsto\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{X}}
to denote the second phase of
\mathsf{Alg}
which outputs
{\widehat{\pi}}
as a measurable function of collected data.

For any policy
{\pi^{\star}}
, decoder
\phi
, and dataset
\mathcal{D}
we define the event

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}({\pi^{\star}},\phi,\mathsf{Alg}_{f}(\mathcal{D})):=\left\{\Pr_{{\pi^{\star}},\phi}\left[\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{rel}}^{\pi^{\star}}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \mathsf{Alg}_{f}(\mathcal{D})(\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})_{1:H})={\pi^{\star}}(\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})_{1:H})\right]\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\right\}.

The event
\mathcal{E}({\pi^{\star}},\phi,\mathsf{Alg}_{f}(\mathcal{D}))
is measurable with respect to the random variable
\mathcal{D}
, which denotes the collected data.

Under this notation, the PAC learning guarantee on
\mathsf{Alg}
implies that for every
{\pi^{\star}}\in\Pi^{(\ell)}
,
\phi\in\Phi
we have

\displaystyle\Pr_{{\pi^{\star}},\phi}\left[\mathcal{E}({\pi^{\star}},\phi,\mathsf{Alg}_{f}(\mathcal{D}))\right]\geq 7/8.

Moreover via an averaging argument we also have

\displaystyle\Pr_{{\pi^{\star}}}\left[\mathcal{E}({\pi^{\star}},\phi,\mathsf{Alg}_{f}(\mathcal{D}))\right]\geq 7/8.

(5.4)

Lower Bound Argument.

We apply a truncation to the stopping time
\eta
. Define
{T_{\mathrm{max}}}:=2^{H/3}
. Observe that if
\Pr_{\pi^{\star}}[\eta>{T_{\mathrm{max}}}]>1/8
for some
{\pi^{\star}}\in\Pi^{(\ell)}
then the lower bound immediately follows, since

\displaystyle\max_{\phi\in\Phi}\En_{{\pi^{\star}},\phi}[\eta]\penalty\ >\penalty\ \En_{{\pi^{\star}}}[\eta]\penalty\ \geq\penalty\ \Pr_{{\pi^{\star}}}[\eta>{T_{\mathrm{max}}}]\cdot{T_{\mathrm{max}}}\penalty\ \geq\penalty\ {T_{\mathrm{max}}}/8,

so there must exist an MDP
M_{{\pi^{\star}},\phi}
for which
\mathsf{Alg}
collects at least
{T_{\mathrm{max}}}/8=2^{H/3-3}
samples in expectation.

Otherwise we have
\Pr_{\pi^{\star}}[\eta>{T_{\mathrm{max}}}]\leq 1/8
for all
{\pi^{\star}}\in\Pi^{(\ell)}
. This further implies that for all
{\pi^{\star}}\in\Pi^{(\ell)}
,

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\Pr_{\pi^{\star}}\left[\eta<{T_{\mathrm{max}}}\text{ and }\mathcal{E}({\pi^{\star}},\phi,\mathsf{Alg}_{f}(\mathcal{D}))\right]

\displaystyle=\Pr_{\pi^{\star}}\left[\mathcal{E}({\pi^{\star}},\phi,\mathsf{Alg}_{f}(\mathcal{D}))\right]-\Pr_{\pi^{\star}}\left[\eta>{T_{\mathrm{max}}}\text{ and }\mathcal{E}({\pi^{\star}},\phi,\mathsf{Alg}_{f}(\mathcal{D}))\right]

\displaystyle\geq 3/4.

(5.5)

However, in the following, we will argue that if Eq. (
5.5
) holds then
\mathsf{Alg}
must query a significant number of samples in
M_{0}
.

Lemma 5.2

(Stopping Time Lemma)
.

Let
\delta\in(0,1/8]
. Let
\mathsf{Alg}
be an
(\varepsilon/16,\delta)
-PAC algorithm. Let
{T_{\mathrm{max}}}\in\mathbb{N}
. Suppose that
\Pr_{\pi^{\star}}\left[\eta<{T_{\mathrm{max}}}\text{ and }\mathcal{E}({\pi^{\star}},\phi,\mathsf{Alg}_{f}(\mathcal{D}))\right]\geq 1-2\delta
for all
{\pi^{\star}}\in\Pi^{(\ell)}
. The expected stopping time for
\mathsf{Alg}
on
M_{0}
is at least

\displaystyle\En_{0}\left[\eta\right]\geq\left(\frac{\lvert\Pi^{(\ell)}\rvert}{2}-\frac{4}{\varepsilon}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{7}\log\left(\frac{1}{2\delta}\right)-\lvert\Pi^{(\ell)}\rvert\cdot\frac{{T_{\mathrm{max}}}^{2}}{2^{H+3}}\left({T_{\mathrm{max}}}+\frac{1}{7}\log\left(\frac{1}{2\delta}\right)\right).

Using
Lemma
5.2
with
\delta=1/8
and plugging in the value of
\lvert\Pi^{(\ell)}\rvert
and
{T_{\mathrm{max}}}
, we see that

\displaystyle\En_{0}[\eta]

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{\lvert\Pi^{(\ell)}\rvert}{2}-\frac{4}{\varepsilon}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{7}\log\left(\frac{1}{2\delta}\right)-\lvert\Pi^{(\ell)}\rvert\cdot\frac{{T_{\mathrm{max}}}^{2}}{2^{H+3}}\left({T_{\mathrm{max}}}+\frac{1}{7}\log\left(\frac{1}{2\delta}\right)\right)\geq\frac{\lvert\Pi^{(\ell)}\rvert}{20}.

For the second inequality, we used the fact that
\ell\geq 2
,
H\geq 10^{5}
, and
\varepsilon<1/10^{7}
.

We have shown that either there exists some MDP
M_{{\pi^{\star}},\phi}
for which
\mathsf{Alg}
collects at least
{T_{\mathrm{max}}}/8=2^{H/3-3}
samples in expectation, or
\mathsf{Alg}
must query at least
\lvert\Pi^{(\ell)}\rvert/20=1/(120\varepsilon^{\ell})
trajectories in expectation in
M_{0}
. Putting it all together, the lower bound on the sample complexity is at least

\displaystyle\min\left\{\frac{1}{120\varepsilon^{\ell}},2^{H/3-3}\right\}.

This concludes the proof of
Theorem
5.1
.∎

5.5.1.4  Proof of
Lemma
5.1

To prove
Lemma
5.1
, we first use a probabilistic argument to construct a certain binary matrix
B
which satisfies several properties, and then construct
\Pi^{(\ell)}
using
B
and verify it satisfies Properties (1)-(4).

Binary Matrix Construction.

First, we define a block-free property of binary matrices.

Definition 5.4

(Block-free Matrices)
.

Fix parameters
k,\ell,N,d\in\mathbb{N}
where
k\leq N
and
l\leq d
. We say a binary matrix
B\in\{0,1\}^{N\times d}
is
(k,\ell)
-block-free if the following holds: for every
I\subseteq[N]
with
\lvert I\rvert=k
, and
J\subseteq[d]
with
\lvert J\rvert=\ell
there exists some
(i,j)\in I\times J
with
B_{ij}=0
.

In words, matrices which are
(k,\ell)
-block-free do not contain a
k\times\ell
block of all 1s.

Lemma 5.3
.

Fix any
\varepsilon\in(0,1/10)
and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. For any

\displaystyle d\in\Big[\frac{16\ell\cdot\log(1/\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon},\frac{1}{20}\cdot\exp\Big(\frac{1}{48\varepsilon^{\ell-1}}\Big)\Big],

there exists a binary matrix
B\in\{0,1\}^{N\times d}
with
N=1/(6\cdot\varepsilon^{\ell})
such that:

(1)

(Row sum): for every row
i\in[N]
, we have
\sum_{j}B_{ij}\geq\varepsilon d/2
.

(2)

(Column sum): for every column
j\in[d]
, we have
\sum_{i}B_{ij}\in[\varepsilon N/2,2\varepsilon N]
.

(3)

The matrix
B
is
(\ell\log d,\ell)
-block-free.

Proof of
Lemma
5.3
.

The existence of
B
is proven using the probabilistic method. Let
\widetilde{B}\in\{0,1\}^{N\times d}
be a random matrix where each entry is i.i.d. chosen to be 1 with probability
\varepsilon
.

By Chernoff bounds (
Lemma
A.2
), for every row
i\in[N]
, we have
\Pr[\sum_{j}B_{ij}\leq\tfrac{\varepsilon d}{2}]\leq\exp(-\varepsilon d/8)
; likewise for every column
j\in[d]
, we have
\Pr[\sum_{j}B_{ij}\notin[\tfrac{\varepsilon N}{2},2\varepsilon N]]\leq 2\exp(-\varepsilon N/8)
. By union bound, the matrix
\widetilde{B}
satisfies the first two properties with probability at least
0.8
as long as

\displaystyle d\geq(8\log 10N)/\varepsilon,\quad\text{and}\quad N\geq(8\log 20d)/\varepsilon.

One can check that under the choice of
N=1/(6\cdot\varepsilon^{\ell})
and the assumption on
d
, both constraints are met.

Now we examine the probability of
\widetilde{B}
satisfies the block-free property with parameters
(k\coloneqq\ell\log d,\ell)
. Let
X
be the random variable which denotes the number of submatrices which violate the block-free property in
\widetilde{B}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle X=\lvert\{I\times J:I\subset[N],\lvert I\rvert=k,J\subset[d],\lvert J\rvert=\ell,\widetilde{B}_{ij}=1\ \forall\ (i,j)\in I\times J\}\rvert.

By linearity of expectation, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[X]\leq N^{k}d^{\ell}\varepsilon^{k\ell}.

We now plug in the choice
k=\ell\log d
and observe that as long as
N\leq 1/(2e\cdot\varepsilon^{\ell})
we have
\mathbb{E}[X]\leq 1/2
. By Markov’s inequality,
\Pr[X=0]\geq 1/2
.

Therefore with positive probability,
\widetilde{B}
satisfies all 3 properties (otherwise we would have a contradiction via inclusion-exlusion principle). Thus, there exists a matrix
B
which satisfies all of the above three properties, proving the result of
Lemma
5.3
.
∎

Policy Class Construction.

For the given
\varepsilon
and
\ell\in\{2,\dots,H\}
we will use
Lemma
5.3
to construct a policy class
\Pi^{(\ell)}
which has bounded spanning capacity but is hard to explore. We instantiate
Lemma
5.3
with the given
\ell
and
d=2^{2H}
, and use the resulting matrix
B
to construct
\Pi^{(\ell)}=\{\pi_{i}\}_{i\in[N]}
with
\lvert\Pi^{(\ell)}\rvert=N=1/(6\varepsilon^{\ell})
.

Recall that we assume that

\displaystyle H\geq h_{0},\quad\text{and}\quad\varepsilon\in\left[\frac{1}{2^{cH}},\frac{1}{100H}\right].

We claim that under these assumptions, the requirement of
Lemma
5.3
is met:

\displaystyle d=2^{2H}\in\left[\frac{16\ell\cdot\log(1/\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon},\frac{1}{20}\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{48\varepsilon^{\ell-1}}\right)\right].

For the lower bound, we can check that:

\displaystyle\frac{16\ell\cdot\log(1/\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}\leq 16H\cdot cH\cdot 2^{cH}\leq 2^{2H},

where we use the bound
\ell\leq H
and
\varepsilon\geq 2^{-cH}
. The last inequality holds for sufficiently small universal constant
c\in(0,1)
and sufficiently large
H\geq h_{0}
.

For the upper bound, we can also check that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{20}\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{48\varepsilon^{\ell-1}}\right)\geq\frac{1}{20}\cdot\exp\left(\frac{100H}{48}\right)\geq 2^{2H},

where we use the bound
\ell\geq 2
and
\varepsilon\leq 1/(100H)
. The last inequality holds for sufficiently large
H
.
We define the policies as follows: for every
\pi_{i}\in\Pi^{(\ell)}
we set

\displaystyle\text{for every }j\in[2^{2H}]:\quad

\displaystyle\pi_{i}(j[h])=\pi_{i}(j^{\prime}[h])=\begin{cases}\mathrm{bit}_{h}(\sum_{a\leq i}B_{aj})&\text{if}\ B_{ij}=1,\\
0&\text{if}\ B_{ij}=0.\end{cases}

(5.6)

The function
\mathrm{bit}_{h}:[2^{H}-1]\mapsto\{0,1\}
selects the
h
-th bit in the binary representation of the input.

Verifying Properties
(1)-(4)
of
Lemma
5.1
.

Properties
(1)-(3)
are straightforward from the construction of
B
and
\Pi^{(\ell)}
, since
\pi_{i}\in\Pi^{(\ell)}_{j}
if and only if
B_{ij}=1
. The only detail which requires some care is that we require that
2\varepsilon N<2^{H}
in order for Property (3) to hold, since otherwise we cannot assign the behaviors of the policies according to Eq. (
5.6
). However, by assumption, this always holds, since
2\varepsilon N=1/(3\varepsilon^{\ell-1})\leq 2^{H}.

We now prove Property (4) that
\Pi^{(\ell)}
has bounded spanning capacity. To prove this we will use the block-free property of the underlying binary matrix
B
.

Fix any deterministic MDP
M^{\star}
which witnesses
\mathfrak{C}(\Pi^{(\ell)})
at layer
h^{\star}
. To bound
\mathfrak{C}(\Pi^{(\ell)})
, we need to count the contribution to
C^{\mathsf{reach}}_{h^{\star}}(\Pi;M^{\star})
from trajectories
\tau
which are produced by some
\pi\in\Pi^{(\ell)}
on
M
. We first define a
layer decomposition
for a trajectory
\tau=(x_{1},a_{1},x_{2},a_{2},\dots,x_{H},a_{H})
as the unique tuple of indices
(h_{1},h_{2},\dots h_{m})
, where each
h_{k}\in[H]
, that satisfies the following properties:

\bullet

The layers satisfy
h_{1}<h_{2}<\dots<h_{m}
.

\bullet

The layer
h_{1}
represents the first layer where
a_{h_{1}}=1
.

\bullet

The layer
h_{2}
represents the first layer where
a_{h_{2}}=1
on some state
x_{h_{2}}
such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{idx}(x_{h_{2}})\notin\{\mathrm{idx}(x_{h_{1}})\}.

\bullet

The layer
h_{3}
represents the first layer where
a_{h_{3}}=1
on some state
x_{h_{3}}
such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{idx}(x_{h_{3}})\notin\{\mathrm{idx}(x_{h_{1}}),\mathrm{idx}(x_{h_{2}})\}.

\bullet

More generally the layer
h_{k}
,
k\in[m]
represents the first layer where
a_{h_{k}}=1
on some state
x_{h_{k}}
such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{idx}(x_{h_{k}})\notin\{\mathrm{idx}(x_{h_{1}}),\dots,\mathrm{idx}(x_{h_{k-1}})\}.

In other words, the layer
h_{k}
represents the
k
-th layer for where action is
a=1
on a new state index which
\tau
has never played
a=1
on before.

We will count the contribution to
C^{\mathsf{reach}}_{h^{\star}}(\Pi;M^{\star})
by doing casework on the length of the layer decomposition for any
\tau
. That is, for every length
m\in\{0,\dots,H\}
, we will bound
C_{h^{\star}}(m)
, which is defined to be the total number of
(x,a)
at layer
h^{\star}
which, for some
\pi\in\Pi^{(\ell)}
, a trajectory
\pi\rightsquigarrow\tau
that has a
m
-length layer decomposition visits. Then we apply the bound

\displaystyle C^{\mathsf{reach}}_{h^{\star}}(\Pi;M^{\star})\leq\sum_{m=0}^{H}C_{h^{\star}}(m).

(5.7)

Note that this will overcount, since the same
(x,a)
pair can belong to multiple different trajectories with different length layer decompositions.

Lemma 5.4
.

The following bounds hold:

\bullet

For any
m\leq\ell
,
C_{h^{\star}}(m)\leq H^{m}\cdot\prod_{k=1}^{m}(2kH)=\mathcal{O}(H^{4m})
.

\bullet

We have
\sum_{m\geq\ell+1}C_{h^{\star}}(m)\leq\mathcal{O}(\ell\cdot H^{4\ell+1})
.

Therefore, applying
Lemma
5.4
to Eq. (
5.7
), we have the bound that
