\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}(\Pi^{(\ell)})\leq\left(\sum_{m\leq\ell}O(H^{4m})\right)+O(\ell\cdot H^{4\ell+1})\leq O(H^{4\ell+2}).

This concludes the proof of
Lemma
5.1
.∎

Proof of
Lemma
5.4
.

All of our upper bounds will be monotone in the value of
h^{\star}
, so we will prove the bounds for
C_{H}(m)
. In the following, fix any deterministic MDP
M^{\star}
.

First we start with the case where
m=0
. The trajectory
\tau
must play
a=0
at all times; since there is only one such
\tau
, we have
C_{H}(0)=1
.

Now we will bound
C_{H}(m)
, for any
m\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}
. Observe that there are
\binom{H}{m}\leq H^{m}
ways to pick the tuple
(h_{1},\dots,h_{m})
. Now we will fix
(h_{1},\dots,h_{m})
and count the contributions to
C_{H}(m)
for trajectories
\tau
which have this fixed layer decomposition, and then sum up over all possible choices of
(h_{1},\dots,h_{m})
.

In the MDP
M^{\star}
, there is a unique state
x_{h_{1}}
which
\tau
must visit. In the layers between
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
, all trajectories are only allowed take
1
on states with index
\mathrm{idx}(x_{h_{1}})
, but they are not required to. Thus we can compute that the contribution to
C_{h_{2}}(m)
from trajectories with the fixed layer decomposition to be at most
2H
. The reasoning is as follows. At
h_{1}
, there is exactly one
(x,a)
pair which is reachable by trajectories with this fixed layer decomposition, since any
\tau
must take
a=1
at
x_{h_{1}}
. Subsequently we can add at most two reachable pairs in every layer
h\in\{h_{1}+1,\dots,h_{2}-1\}
due to encountering a state
j[h]
or
j^{\prime}[h]
where
j=\mathrm{idx}(x_{h_{1}})
, and at layer
h_{2}
we must play
a=1
, for a total of
1+2(h_{2}-h_{1}-1)\leq 2H
. Using similar reasoning the contribution to
C_{h_{3}}(m)
from trajectories with this fixed layer decomposition is at most
(2H)\cdot(4H)
, and so on. Continuing in this way, we have the final bound of
\prod_{k=1}^{m}(2kH)
. Since this holds for a fixed choice of
(h_{1},\dots,h_{m})
in total we have
C_{H}(m)\leq H^{m}\cdot\prod_{k=1}^{m}(2kH)=\mathcal{O}(H^{4m})
.

When
m\geq\ell+1
, observe that the block-free property on
B
implies that for any
J\subseteq[2^{H}]
with
\lvert J\rvert=\ell
we have
\lvert\cap_{j\in J}\Pi_{j}\rvert\leq\ell\log 2^{2H}
. So for any trajectory
\tau
with layer decomposition such that
m\geq\ell
we can redo the previous analysis and argue that there is at most
\ell\log 2^{2H}
multiplicative factor contribution to the value
C_{H}(m)
due to
all
trajectories which have layer decompositions longer than
\ell
. Thus we arrive at the bound

\displaystyle\sum_{m\geq\ell+1}C_{H}(m)\leq\mathcal{O}(H^{4\ell})\cdot\ell\log 2^{2H}\leq\mathcal{O}(\ell\cdot H^{4\ell+1}).

This concludes the proof of
Lemma
5.4
.
∎

5.5.1.5  Remarks on Tightness of
Lemma
5.1

At the heart of our lower bound construction is
Lemma
5.1
, which constructs a large policy class of size
\lvert\Pi^{(\ell)}\rvert\asymp 1/\varepsilon^{\ell}
with bounded spanning capacity
\mathfrak{C}(\Pi^{(\ell)})\lesssim H^{\ell}
. The size of the policy class directly corresponds to the lower bound on sample complexity in
Theorem
5.1
. Thus, it is natural to ask: can we improve upon
Lemma
5.1
to construct even larger policy classes? In this section, we explain how the current strategy of constructing
\Pi^{(\ell)}
via “block-free” matrices (
Lemma
5.3
)
cannot
yield an improved size of
\Pi^{(\ell)}
, thus ruling out the most direct way of improving our lower bound.

The Zarankiewicz Problem.

Block-free matrices are studied in the context of a problem in extremal graph theory called the Zarankiewicz problem
[
Zar51
]
.
3
3

3

We are aware of only one other work in learning theory which relates to the Zarankiewicz problem:
[
BES02
]
uses bounds on
z(N,d;k,\ell)
to prove separations between sign rank and VC dimension. Coincidentally, we used techniques from later improvements on these results
[
AMY16
]
to prove our separations between eluder dimension and sign rank in
Section
3.3
.
In the language of graph theory, binary matrices
B\in\{0,1\}^{N\times d}
correspond to bipartite graphs
G=(V=[N]\cup[d],E)
, where an edge
e_{ij}
is drawn if and only if the matrix entry
B_{ij}=1
. Denote
K_{k,\ell}
to be a complete bipartite graph with
k
vertices on the left and
\ell
vertices on the right. We define the Zarankiewicz function as follows:

Definition 5.5
.

The Zarankiewicz function
z(N,d;k,\ell)
denotes the maximum possible number of edges in a bipartite graph
G
with vertex set
V=[N]\cup[d]
which does not contain the subgraph
K_{k,\ell}
.

Alternatively,
z(N,d;k,\ell)
denotes the maximum number of 1s in an
N\times d
binary matrix that is
(k,\ell)
block-free. The Zarankiewicz problem is to determine tight bounds on
z(N,d;k,\ell)
. The following upper bound is shown in
[
Bol78
]
:

\displaystyle z(N,d;k,\ell)\leq(k-1)^{1/\ell}(d-\ell+1)N^{1-1/\ell}+(\ell-1)N.

(5.8)

Implications for Our Construction.

In
Lemma
5.3
, we construct a binary matrix of size
N\times d
which is block-free yet has a minimum density of 1s. In fact, either property (1) or (2) implies that the total number of 1s is at least
\varepsilon/2\cdot Nd
. On the other hand, (
5.8
) implies that

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\cdot Nd

\displaystyle\leq(\ell\log d)^{1/\ell}\cdot d\cdot N^{1-1/\ell}+\ell N

Since we are interested in the regime where
\ell
is a constant (and therefore
\ell\ll\varepsilon d
, we can ignore the last term. Rearranging we get that

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{\ell\log d}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\ell}.

Thus, it is not possible to significantly increase the size of the policy class
\Pi^{(\ell)}
beyond
1/\varepsilon^{\ell}
via this construction!

5.5.1.6  Proof of
Lemma
5.2

The proof of this stopping time lemma follows standard machinery for PAC lower bounds
[
GMS19
,
DMKV21
,
SDMMS21
]
. In the following we use
\mathrm{KL}{\left(P\|Q\right)}
to denote the Kullback-Leibler divergence between two distributions
P
and
Q
and
\mathrm{kl}{\left(p\|q\right)}
to denote the Kullback-Leibler divergence between two Bernoulli distributions with parameters
p,q\in[0,1]
.

For any
{\pi^{\star}}\in\Pi^{(\ell)}
we denote the random variable

\displaystyle N^{\pi^{\star}}=\sum_{t=1}^{\eta\wedge{T_{\mathrm{max}}}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathsf{Alg}_{t}(\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})_{1:H})={\pi^{\star}}(\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})_{1:H})\text{ and }\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{rel}}^{\pi^{\star}}\right\},

the number of episodes for which the algorithm’s policy at round
t\in[\eta\wedge{T_{\mathrm{max}}}]
matches that of
{\pi^{\star}}
on a certain relevant state of
{\pi^{\star}}
.

In the sequel we will prove upper and lower bounds on the intermediate quantity
\sum_{{\pi^{\star}}\in\Pi}\En_{0}[N^{\pi^{\star}}]
and relate these quantities to
\En_{0}[\eta]
.

Step 1: Upper Bound.

First we prove an upper bound. We can compute that

\displaystyle\sum_{{\pi^{\star}}\in\Pi}\En_{0}\left[N^{\pi^{\star}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T_{\mathrm{max}}}\sum_{{\pi^{\star}}\in\Pi}\En_{0}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\eta>t-1\right\}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathsf{Alg}_{t}(\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})_{1:H})={\pi^{\star}}(\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})_{1:H})\text{ and }\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{rel}}^{\pi^{\star}}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T_{\mathrm{max}}}\En_{0}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\eta>t-1\right\}\sum_{{\pi^{\star}}\in\Pi}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathsf{Alg}_{t}(\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})_{1:H})={\pi^{\star}}(\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})_{1:H})\text{ and }\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{rel}}^{\pi^{\star}}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\leq{}}\sum_{t=1}^{T_{\mathrm{max}}}\En_{0}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\eta>t-1\right\}\right]\leq\En_{0}\left[\eta\wedge{T_{\mathrm{max}}}\right]\leq\En_{0}\left[\eta\right].

(5.9)

Here, the first inequality follows because for every index
j
and every
{\pi^{\star}}\in\Pi^{(\ell)}_{j}
, each
{\pi^{\star}}
admits a unique sequence of actions (by Property (3) of
Lemma
5.1
), so any policy
\mathsf{Alg}_{t}
can completely match with at most one of the
{\pi^{\star}}
.

Step 2: Lower Bound.

Now we turn to the lower bound. We use a change of measure argument.

\displaystyle\En_{0}\left[N^{\pi^{\star}}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\geq{}}\En_{0,{\pi^{\star}}}\left[N^{\pi^{\star}}\right]-{T_{\mathrm{max}}}\Delta({T_{\mathrm{max}}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lvert\Phi\rvert}\sum_{\phi\in\Phi}\En_{0,{\pi^{\star}},\phi}\left[N^{\pi^{\star}}\right]-{T_{\mathrm{max}}}\Delta({T_{\mathrm{max}}})

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{\geq{}}\frac{1}{7}\cdot\frac{1}{\lvert\Phi\rvert}\sum_{\phi\in\Phi}\mathrm{KL}{\left(\Pr_{0,{\pi^{\star}},\phi}^{\mathcal{F}_{\eta\wedge{T_{\mathrm{max}}}}}\|\Pr_{{\pi^{\star}},\phi}^{\mathcal{F}_{\eta\wedge{T_{\mathrm{max}}}}}\right)}-{T_{\mathrm{max}}}\Delta({T_{\mathrm{max}}})

\displaystyle\overset{(iii)}{\geq{}}\frac{1}{7}\cdot\mathrm{KL}{\left(\Pr_{0,{\pi^{\star}}}^{\mathcal{F}_{\eta\wedge{T_{\mathrm{max}}}}}\|\Pr_{{\pi^{\star}}}^{\mathcal{F}_{\eta\wedge{T_{\mathrm{max}}}}}\right)}-{T_{\mathrm{max}}}\Delta({T_{\mathrm{max}}})

The inequality
(i)
follows from a change of measure argument using
Lemma
5.5
, with
\Delta({T_{\mathrm{max}}})\coloneqq{T_{\mathrm{max}}}^{2}/2^{H+3}
. Here,
\mathcal{F}_{\eta\wedge{T_{\mathrm{max}}}}
denotes the natural filtration generated by the first
\eta\wedge{T_{\mathrm{max}}}
episodes. The inequality
(ii)
follows from
Lemma
5.6
, using the fact that
M_{0,{\pi^{\star}},\phi}
and
M_{{\pi^{\star}},\phi}
have identical transitions and only differ in rewards at layer
H
for the trajectories which reach the end of a relevant combination lock. The number of times this occurs is exactly
N^{\pi^{\star}}
. The factor
1/7
is a lower bound on
\mathrm{kl}{\left(1/2\|3/4\right)}
. The inequality
(iii)
follows by the convexity of KL divergence.

Now we apply
Lemma
A.5
to lower bound the expectation for any
\mathcal{F}_{\eta\wedge{T_{\mathrm{max}}}}
-measurable random variable
Z\in[0,1]
as

\displaystyle\En_{0}\left[N^{\pi^{\star}}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{7}\cdot\mathrm{kl}{\left(\En_{0,{\pi^{\star}}}\left[Z\right]\|\En_{{\pi^{\star}}}\left[Z\right]\right)}-{T_{\mathrm{max}}}\Delta({T_{\mathrm{max}}})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{7}\cdot(1-\En_{0,{\pi^{\star}}}\left[Z\right])\log\left(\frac{1}{1-\En_{{\pi^{\star}}}\left[Z\right]}\right)-\frac{\log(2)}{7}-{T_{\mathrm{max}}}\Delta({T_{\mathrm{max}}}),

where the second inequality follows from the bound
\mathrm{kl}{\left(p\|q\right)}\geq(1-p)\log(1/(1-q))-\log(2)

[
DMKV21
, Lemma 15]
.

Now we pick
Z=Z_{\pi^{\star}}\coloneqq\mathbbm{1}\left\{\eta<{T_{\mathrm{max}}}\text{ and }\mathcal{E}({\pi^{\star}},\phi,\mathsf{Alg}_{f}(\mathcal{D}))\right\}
and note that
\En_{{\pi^{\star}}}[Z_{\pi^{\star}}]\geq 1-2\delta
by assumption. This implies that

\displaystyle\En_{0}\left[N^{\pi^{\star}}\right]

\displaystyle\geq(1-\En_{0,{\pi^{\star}}}\left[Z_{\pi^{\star}}\right])\cdot\frac{1}{7}\log\left(\frac{1}{2\delta}\right)-\frac{\log(2)}{7}-{T_{\mathrm{max}}}\Delta({T_{\mathrm{max}}}).

Another application of
Lemma
5.5
gives

\displaystyle\En_{0}\left[N^{\pi^{\star}}\right]

\displaystyle\geq(1-\En_{0}\left[Z_{\pi^{\star}}\right])\cdot\frac{1}{7}\log\left(\frac{1}{2\delta}\right)-\frac{\log(2)}{7}-\Delta({T_{\mathrm{max}}})\left({T_{\mathrm{max}}}+\frac{1}{7}\log\left(\frac{1}{2\delta}\right)\right).

Summing the above over
{\pi^{\star}}\in\Pi^{(\ell)}
, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{{\pi^{\star}}}\En_{0}\left[N^{\pi^{\star}}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\left(\lvert\Pi^{(\ell)}\rvert-\sum_{{\pi^{\star}}}\En_{0}\left[Z_{\pi^{\star}}\right]\right)\cdot\frac{1}{7}\log\left(\frac{1}{2\delta}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\lvert\Pi^{(\ell)}\rvert\cdot\frac{\log(2)}{7}-\lvert\Pi^{(\ell)}\rvert\cdot\Delta({T_{\mathrm{max}}})\left({T_{\mathrm{max}}}+\frac{1}{7}\log\left(\frac{1}{2\delta}\right)\right).

(5.10)

It remains to prove an upper bound on
\sum_{{\pi^{\star}}}\En_{0}\left[Z_{\pi^{\star}}\right]
. We calculate that

\displaystyle\sum_{{\pi^{\star}}}\En_{0}\left[Z_{\pi^{\star}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\pi^{\star}}}\En_{0}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\eta<{T_{\mathrm{max}}}\text{ and }\mathcal{E}({\pi^{\star}},\phi,\mathsf{Alg}_{f}(\mathcal{D}))\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{{\pi^{\star}}}\En_{0}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\Pr_{\pi^{\star}}\left[\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{rel}}^{\pi^{\star}}\text{ and }\mathsf{Alg}_{f}(\mathcal{D})(\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})_{1:H})={\pi^{\star}}(\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})_{1:H})\right]\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{\varepsilon}\cdot\En_{0}\left[\sum_{{\pi^{\star}}}\Pr_{\pi^{\star}}\left[\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{rel}}^{\pi^{\star}}\text{ and }\mathsf{Alg}_{f}(\mathcal{D})(\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})_{1:H})={\pi^{\star}}(\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})_{1:H})\right]\right]

(5.11)

The last inequality is an application of Markov’s inequality.

Now we carefully investigate the sum. For any
\phi\in\Phi
, the sum can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\hskip-21.68121pt\sum_{{\pi^{\star}}}\Pr_{{\pi^{\star}},\phi}\left[\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{rel}}^{\pi^{\star}}\text{ and }\mathsf{Alg}_{f}(\mathcal{D})(\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})_{1:H})={\pi^{\star}}(\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})_{1:H})\right]

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\sum_{{\pi^{\star}}}\sum_{x_{1}\in\mathcal{X}_{1}}\Pr_{{\pi^{\star}},\phi}\left[x_{1}\right]\Pr_{{\pi^{\star}},\phi}\left[\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{rel}}^{\pi^{\star}}\text{ and }\mathsf{Alg}_{f}(\mathcal{D})(\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})_{1:H})={\pi^{\star}}(\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})_{1:H})\penalty\ \mid\penalty\ x_{1}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{=}\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{X}_{1}\rvert}\sum_{x_{1}\in\mathcal{X}_{1}}\sum_{{\pi^{\star}}}\Pr_{{\pi^{\star}},\phi}\left[\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{rel}}^{\pi^{\star}}\text{ and }\mathsf{Alg}_{f}(\mathcal{D})(\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})_{1:H})={\pi^{\star}}(\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})_{1:H})\penalty\ \mid\penalty\ x_{1}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{=}\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{X}_{1}\rvert}\sum_{x_{1}\in\mathcal{X}_{1}}\sum_{{\pi^{\star}}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{rel}}^{\pi^{\star}}\text{ and }\mathsf{Alg}_{f}(\mathcal{D})(\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})_{1:H})={\pi^{\star}}(\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})_{1:H})\right\}.

(5.12)

The equality
(i)
follows because regardless of which MDP
M_{\pi^{\star}}
we are in, the first state is distributed uniformly over
\mathcal{X}_{1}
. The equality
(ii)
follows because once we condition on the first state
x_{1}
, the probability is either 0 or 1.

Fix any start state
x_{1}
. We can write

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\sum_{{\pi^{\star}}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{rel}}^{\pi^{\star}}\text{ and }\mathsf{Alg}_{f}(\mathcal{D})(\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})_{1:H}){\pi^{\star}}(\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})_{1:H})\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\pi^{\star}}\in\Pi^{(\ell)}_{\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathsf{Alg}_{f}(\mathcal{D})(\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})_{1:H})={\pi^{\star}}(\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})_{1:H})\right\}=1,

where the second equality uses the fact that on any index
j
, each
{\pi^{\star}}\in\Pi^{(\ell)}_{j}
behaves differently (Property (3) of
Lemma
5.1
), so
\mathsf{Alg}_{f}(\mathcal{D})
can match at most one of these behaviors. Plugging this back into Eq. (
5.12
), averaging over
\phi\in\Phi
, and combining with Eq. (
5.11
), we arrive at the bound

\displaystyle\sum_{{\pi^{\star}}}\En_{0}\left[Z_{\pi^{\star}}\right]\leq\frac{4}{\varepsilon}.

We now use this in conjunction with Eq. (
5.10
) to arrive at the final lower bound

\displaystyle\sum_{{\pi^{\star}}}\En_{0}\left[N^{\pi^{\star}}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\left(\lvert\Pi^{(\ell)}\rvert-\frac{4}{\varepsilon}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{7}\log\left(\frac{1}{2\delta}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\lvert\Pi^{(\ell)}\rvert\cdot\frac{\log(2)}{7}-\lvert\Pi^{(\ell)}\rvert\cdot\Delta({T_{\mathrm{max}}})\left({T_{\mathrm{max}}}+\frac{1}{7}\log\left(\frac{1}{2\delta}\right)\right).

(5.13)

Step 3: Putting it All Together.

Combining Eqs. (
5.9
) and (
5.13
), plugging in our choice of
\Delta({T_{\mathrm{max}}})
, and simplifying we get

\displaystyle\En_{0}\left[\eta\right]

\displaystyle\geq\left(\lvert\Pi^{(\ell)}\rvert-\frac{4}{\varepsilon}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{7}\log\left(\frac{1}{2\delta}\right)-\lvert\Pi^{(\ell)}\rvert\cdot\frac{\log(2)}{7}-\lvert\Pi^{(\ell)}\rvert\cdot\Delta({T_{\mathrm{max}}})\left({T_{\mathrm{max}}}+\frac{1}{7}\log\left(\frac{1}{2\delta}\right)\right).

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{\lvert\Pi^{(\ell)}\rvert}{2}-\frac{4}{\varepsilon}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{7}\log\left(\frac{1}{2\delta}\right)-\lvert\Pi^{(\ell)}\rvert\cdot\frac{{T_{\mathrm{max}}}^{2}}{2^{H+3}}\left({T_{\mathrm{max}}}+\frac{1}{7}\log\left(\frac{1}{2\delta}\right)\right).

The last inequality follows since
\delta\leq 1/8
implies
\log(1/(2\delta))\geq 2\log(2)
.

This concludes the proof of
Lemma
5.2
.∎

5.5.1.7  Change of Measure Lemma

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
Z\in[0,1]
be a
\mathcal{F}_{{T_{\mathrm{max}}}}
-measurable random variable. Then, for every
{\pi^{\star}}\in\Pi^{(\ell)}
,

\displaystyle\lvert\En_{0}\left[Z\right]-\En_{0,{\pi^{\star}}}\left[Z\right]\rvert\leq\Delta({T_{\mathrm{max}}}):=\frac{{T_{\mathrm{max}}}^{2}}{2^{H+3}}

Proof.

First, we note that

\displaystyle\lvert\En_{0}\left[Z\right]-\En_{0,{\pi^{\star}}}\left[Z\right]\rvert\leq D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr_{0}^{\mathcal{F}_{T_{\mathrm{max}}}},\Pr_{0,{\pi^{\star}}}^{\mathcal{F}_{T_{\mathrm{max}}}}\right)\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T_{\mathrm{max}}}\En_{0}\left[D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr_{0}[\cdot|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}],\Pr_{0,{\pi^{\star}}}[\cdot|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\right)\right].

Here
\Pr_{0}[\cdot|\mathcal{F}_{t}]
denotes the conditional distribution of the
t
-th trajectory given the first
t-1
trajectories. Similarly
\Pr_{0,{\pi^{\star}}}[\cdot|\mathcal{F}_{t}]
is the averaged over decoders condition distribution of the
t
-th trajectory given the first
t-1
trajectories. The second inequality follows by chain rule of TV distance (
Lemma
A.4
).

Now we examine each term
D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr_{0}[\cdot|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}],\Pr_{0,{\pi^{\star}}}[\cdot|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\right)
. Fix a history
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
and sequence
x_{1:H}
where all
x_{i}
have the same index. We want to bound the quantity

\displaystyle\left\lvert\Pr_{0,{\pi^{\star}}}\left[X_{1:H}^{(t)}=x_{1:H}\penalty\ \mid\penalty\ \mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]-\Pr_{0}\left[X_{1:H}^{(t)}=x_{1:H}\penalty\ \mid\penalty\ \mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]\right\rvert,

where it is understood that the random variable
x_{1:H}^{(t)}
is drawn according to the MDP dynamics and algorithm’s policy
\mathsf{Alg}_{t}
(which is in turn a measurable function of
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
).

We observe that the second term is exactly

\displaystyle\Pr_{0}\left[X_{1:H}^{(t)}=x_{1:H}\penalty\ \mid\penalty\ \mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{X}_{1}\rvert}\cdot\frac{1}{2^{H-1}},

since the state
x_{1}
appears with probability
1/\lvert\mathcal{X}_{1}\rvert
and the transitions in
M_{0}
are uniform to the next state in the combination lock, so each sequence is equally as likely.

For the first term, again the state
x_{1}
appears with probability
1/\lvert\mathcal{X}_{1}\rvert
. Suppose that
\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})\notin\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{rel}}^{\pi^{\star}}
. Then the dynamics of
\Pr_{0,\pi^{\star},\phi}
for all
\phi\in\Phi
are exactly the same as
M_{0}
, so again the probability in this case is
1/(\lvert\mathcal{X}_{1}\rvert 2^{H-1})
. Now consider when
\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{rel}}^{\pi^{\star}}
. At some point
\widehat{h}\in[H+1]
, the policy
\mathsf{Alg}_{t}
will deviate from
{\pi^{\star}}
for the first time (if
\mathsf{Alg}_{t}
never deviates from
{\pi^{\star}}
we set
\widehat{h}=H+1)
. The layer
\widehat{h}
is only a function of
x_{1}
and
\mathsf{Alg}_{t}
and does not depend on the MDP dynamics. The correct decoder must assign
\phi(x_{1:\widehat{h}-1})=\textsc{Good}
and
\phi(x_{\widehat{h}:H})=\textsc{Bad}
, so therefore we have

\displaystyle\Pr_{0,{\pi^{\star}}}\left[X_{1:H}^{(t)}=x_{1:H}\penalty\ \mid\penalty\ \mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]

\displaystyle=\Pr_{0,{\pi^{\star}}}\left[\phi(x_{1:\widehat{h}-1})=\textsc{Good}\text{ and }\phi(x_{\widehat{h}:H})=\textsc{Bad}\penalty\ \mid\penalty\ \mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]

If
x_{1}\notin\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
, i.e., we are seeing
x_{1}
for the first time, then the conditional distribution over the labels given by
\phi
is the same as the unconditioned distribution:

\displaystyle\Pr_{0,{\pi^{\star}}}\left[\phi(x_{1:\widehat{h}-1})=\textsc{Good}\text{ and }\phi(x_{\widehat{h}:H})=\textsc{Bad}\penalty\ \mid\penalty\ \mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{X}_{1}\rvert}\cdot\frac{1}{2^{H-1}}.

Otherwise, if
x_{1}\in\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
then we bound the conditional probability by 1.

\displaystyle\Pr_{0,{\pi^{\star}}}\left[X_{1:H}^{(t)}=x_{1:H}\penalty\ \mid\penalty\ \mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]\leq\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{X}_{1}\rvert}.

Putting this all together we can compute

\displaystyle\Pr_{0,{\pi^{\star}}}\left[X_{1:H}^{(t)}=x_{1:H}\penalty\ \mid\penalty\ \mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]\quad\begin{cases}=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{X}_{1}\rvert}\cdot\frac{1}{2^{H-1}}&\text{if}\quad\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})\notin\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{rel}}^{\pi^{\star}},\\[5.0pt]
=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{X}_{1}\rvert}\cdot\frac{1}{2^{H-1}}&\text{if}\quad\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{rel}}^{\pi^{\star}}\text{ and }x_{1}\notin\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\\[5.0pt]
\leq\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{X}_{1}\rvert}&\text{if}\quad\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{rel}}^{\pi^{\star}}\text{ and }x_{1}\in\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\\[5.0pt]
=0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Therefore we have the bound

\displaystyle\left\lvert\Pr_{0,{\pi^{\star}}}\left[X_{1:H}^{(t)}=x_{1:H}\penalty\ \mid\penalty\ \mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]-\Pr_{0}\left[X_{1:H}^{(t)}=x_{1:H}\penalty\ \mid\penalty\ \mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{X}_{1}\rvert}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathrm{idx}(x_{1})\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{rel}}^{\pi^{\star}},x_{1}\in\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right\}.

Summing over all possible sequences
x_{1:H}
we have

\displaystyle D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr_{0}[\cdot|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}],\Pr_{0,{\pi^{\star}}}[\cdot|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{(t-1)\cdot 2^{H-1}}{\lvert\mathcal{X}_{1}\rvert},

since the only sequences
x_{1:H}
for which the difference in the two measures are nonzero are the ones for which
x_{1}\in\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
, of which there are
(t-1)\cdot 2^{H-1}
of them.

Lastly, taking expectations and summing over
t=1
to
{T_{\mathrm{max}}}
and plugging in the value of
\lvert\mathcal{X}_{1}\rvert=2^{2H}
we have the final bound.
∎

The next lemma is a straightforward modification of
[
DMKV21
, Lemma 5]
, with varying rewards instead of varying transitions.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
M
and
M^{\prime}
be two MDPs that are identical in transition and differ in the reward distributions, denote
r_{h}(x,a)
and
r^{\prime}_{h}(x,a)
. Assume that for all
(x,a)
we have
r_{h}(x,a)\ll r^{\prime}_{h}(x,a)
. Then for any stopping time
\eta
with respect to
(\mathcal{F}^{t})_{t\geq 1}
that satisfies
\Pr_{M}[\eta<\infty]=1
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}{\left(\Pr_{M}^{I_{\eta}}\|\Pr_{M^{\prime}}^{I_{\eta}}\right)}=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X},a\in\mathcal{A},h\in[H]}\En_{M}[N^{\eta}_{s,a,h}]\cdot\mathrm{KL}{\left(r_{h}(x,a)\|r^{\prime}_{h}(x,a)\right)},

where
N^{\eta}_{s,a,h}:=\sum_{t=1}^{\eta}\mathbbm{1}\left\{(x_{h}^{(t)},A_{h}^{(t)})=(x,a)\right\}
and
I_{\eta}:\Omega\mapsto\bigcup_{t\geq 1}\mathcal{I}_{t}:\omega\mapsto I_{\eta(\omega)}(\omega)
is the random vector representing the history up to episode
\eta
.

5.5.2  Proof of
Theorem
5.2

5.5.2.1  Algorithmic Details and Preliminaries

In this section, we provide the details of the subroutines that do not appear in the main body, in Algorithms
4
,
5
, and
6
. The transitions and reward functions in
line
6
in
Algorithm
6
are computed using Eqs. (
5.17
) and (
5.18
), which are specified below, after introducing additional notation.

Algorithm 4

\mathsf{DataCollector}

1:

State:
x
, Reacher policy:
\pi_{x}
, Exploration policy set:
\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}
, Number of samples:
n
.

2:

if

x=x_{\top}

then

//
Uniform sampling for start state
x_{\top}

3:

for

t=1,\dots,n

do

4:

Sample
\pi^{\prime}\sim\mathrm{Uniform}(\Pi_{\mathrm{core}})
, and run
\pi^{\prime}
to collect
\tau=(x_{1},a_{1},\cdots,x_{H},a_{H})
.

5:

\mathcal{D}_{x}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}_{x}\cup\{\tau\}
.

6:

else
//

\pi_{x}
-based sampling for all other states
x\neq x_{\top}

7:

Identify the layer
h
such that
x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}
.

8:

for

t=1,\dots,n

do

9:

Run
\pi_{x}
for first
h-1
layers and collect trajectory
(x_{1},a_{1},\cdots,x_{h-1},a_{h-1},x_{h})
.

10:

if

x_{h}=x

then

11:

Sample
\pi^{\prime}\sim\mathrm{Uniform}(\Pi_{\mathrm{core}})
and run
\pi^{\prime}
to collect
(x_{h},a_{h},\cdots,x_{H},a_{H})
.

12:

\mathcal{D}_{x}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}_{x}\cup\{\tau=(x_{1},a_{1},\cdots,x_{H},a_{H})\}
.

13:

Return
dataset
\mathcal{D}_{x}
.

Algorithm 5

\mathsf{EstReachability}

1:

State space
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{tab}}
, MRP
\mathfrak{M}
, State
\bar{x}\in\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{tab}}
.

2:

Let
P
be the transition of
\mathfrak{M}
.

3:

Initialize
V(x)=\mathbbm{1}\left\{x=\bar{x}\right\}
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{tab}}
.

4:

Repeat

H+1
times:

5:

For all
x\in\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{tab}}
, calculate
V(x)\leftarrow\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{tab}}}P_{x\to x^{\prime}}\cdot V(x^{\prime}).

//
Dynamic Programming

6:

Return

V(x_{\top})
.

Algorithm 6

\mathsf{Evaluate}

1:

Policy set
\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}
, Reachable states
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}
, Datasets
\{\mathcal{D}_{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}}
, Policy
\pi
.

2:

Compute
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rch}}\leftarrow\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{+}\cap\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}
and
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{tab}}=\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rch}}\cup\{x_{\bot}\}
.

3:

for

x,x^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{tab}}

do

//
Compute transitions and rewards on
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{tab}}

4:

Let
h,h^{\prime}
be such that
x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}
and
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{h^{\prime}}

5:

if

h<h^{\prime}

then

6:

Calculate
\widehat{P}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi},\widehat{r}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}
according to (
5.17
) and (
5.18
);

7:

else

8:

Set
\widehat{P}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}\leftarrow 0
,
\widehat{r}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}\leftarrow 0
.

9:

Set
\widehat{V}(x)=0
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{tab}}
.

10:

Repeat
for
H+1
times:
//
Evaluate
\pi
by dynamic programming

11:

For all
x\in\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{tab}}
, calculate
\widehat{V}(x)\leftarrow\sum_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{tab}}}\widehat{P}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}\cdot\left(\widehat{r}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}+\widehat{V}(x^{\prime})\right).

12:

Return

\widehat{V}(x_{\top})
.

Additional notation.

Recall that we assumed that the state space
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\dots\mathcal{X}_{H}
is layered. Thus, given a state
x
, we can infer the layer
h
such that
x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}
. By definition
x_{\top}
belongs to the layer
h=0
and
x_{\bot}
belongs to the layer
h=H
. In the following, we define additional notation:

(a)

Sets
\mathfrak{T}(x\rightarrow x^{\prime};\neg\bar{}\mathcal{X})
: For any set
\bar{}\mathcal{X}
, and states
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
, we define
\mathfrak{T}(x\rightarrow x^{\prime};\neg\bar{}\mathcal{X})
as the set of all the trajectories that go from
x
to
x^{\prime}
without passing through any state in
\bar{}\mathcal{X}
in between.

More formally, let state
x
be at layer
h
, and
x^{\prime}
be at layer
h^{\prime}
. Then,
\mathfrak{T}(x\rightarrow x^{\prime};\neg\bar{}\mathcal{X})
denotes the set of all the trajectories
\tau=(x_{1},a_{1},\cdots,x_{H},a_{H})
that satisfy all of the following:

\bullet

x_{h}=x
, where
x_{h}
is the state at timestep
h
in
\tau
.

\bullet

x_{h^{\prime}}=x^{\prime}
, where
x_{h^{\prime}}
is the state at timestep
h^{\prime}
in
\tau
.

\bullet

For all
h<\widetilde{h}<h^{\prime}
, the state
x_{\widetilde{h}}
, at time step
\widetilde{h}
in
\tau
, does not lie in the set
\bar{}\mathcal{X}
.

Note that when
h^{\prime}\leq h
, we define

\mathfrak{T}(x\rightarrow x^{\prime};\neg\bar{}\mathcal{X})=\emptyset
. Additionally, we define
\mathfrak{T}(x_{\top}\rightarrow x;\neg\bar{}\mathcal{X})
as the set of all trajectories that go to
x^{\prime}
(from a start state) without going through any state in
\bar{}\mathcal{X}
in between. Finally, we define
\mathfrak{T}(x\rightarrow x_{\bot};\neg\bar{}\mathcal{X})
as the set of all the trajectories that go from
x
at time step
h
to the end of the episode without passing through any state in
\bar{}\mathcal{X}
in between.

Furthermore, we use the shorthand
\mathfrak{T}_{\pi}(x\rightarrow x^{\prime})\coloneqq\mathfrak{T}(x\rightarrow x^{\prime};\neg\mathcal{X}_{\pi})
to denote the set of all the trajectories that go from
x
to
x^{\prime}
without passing though any leaf state
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}
.

(b)

Using the above notation, for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
and set
\bar{}\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
, we define
\bar{d}^{\pi}(x;\neg\bar{\mathcal{X}})
as the probability of reaching
x
(from a start state) without passing through any state in
\bar{}\mathcal{X}
in between, i.e.

\displaystyle\bar{d}^{\pi}(x;\neg\bar{\mathcal{X}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}^{\pi}\left[\tau\penalty\ \text{reaches}\penalty\ x\penalty\ \text{without passing through any $x^{\prime}\in\bar{\mathcal{X}}$ before reaching $x$}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}^{\pi}\left[\tau\penalty\ \in\mathfrak{T}(x_{\top}\rightarrow x;\neg\bar{}\mathcal{X})\right].

(5.14)

We next recall the notation of Markov Reward Process and formally define both the population versions of policy-specific MRPs.

Markov Reward Process (MRP).

A Markov reward process

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}=\mathrm{MRP}(\mathcal{X},P,R,H,x_{\top},x_{\bot})

is defined over the state space
\mathcal{X}
with start state
x_{\top}
and end state
x_{\bot}
, for trajectory length
H+2
. Without loss of generality, we assume that
\{x_{\top},x_{\bot}\}\in\mathcal{X}
. The transition kernel is denoted by
P:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to[0,1]
, such that for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
,
\sum_{x^{\prime}}P_{x\to x^{\prime}}=1
; the reward kernel is denoted
R:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\Delta([0,1])
. Throughout, we use the notation
\rightarrow
to signify that the transitions and rewards are defined along the edges of the MRP.

A trajectory in
\mathfrak{M}
is of the form
\tau=(x_{\top},x_{1},\cdots,x_{H},x_{\bot})
, where
x_{h}\in\mathcal{X}
for all
h\in[H]
. Furthermore, from any state
x\in\mathcal{X}
, the MRP transitions
4
4

4

Our definition of Markov Reward Processes (MRP) deviates from MDPs that we considered, in the sense that we do not assume that the state space
\mathcal{X}
is layered in an MRP. This variation is only adapted to simplify the proofs and the notation.
to another state
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
with probability
P_{x\rightarrow x^{\prime}}
, and obtains the rewards
r_{x\rightarrow x^{\prime}}\sim R_{x\rightarrow x^{\prime}}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{M}}[\tau]=P_{x_{\top}\rightarrow x_{1}}\cdot\left(\prod_{h=1}^{H-1}P_{x_{h}\rightarrow x_{h+1}}\right)\cdot P_{x_{h}\rightarrow x_{\bot}},

and the rewards

\displaystyle R^{\mathfrak{M}}(\tau)=r_{x_{\top}\rightarrow x_{1}}+\sum_{h=1}^{H}r_{x_{h}\rightarrow x_{h+1}}+r_{x_{H}\rightarrow x_{\bot}}.

Furthermore, in all the MRPs that we consider in herein, we have
P_{x_{\bot}\rightarrow x_{\bot}}=1
and
r_{x_{\bot}\rightarrow x_{\bot}}=0
.

Policy-Specific Markov Reward Processes.

A key technical tool in our analysis will be policy-specific MRPs that depend on the set
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}
of the states that we have explored so far. Recall that for any policy
\pi
,
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{+}=\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\cup\{x_{\top},x_{\bot}\}
,
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rch}}=\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{+}\cap\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}
and
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}}=\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{+}\setminus(\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rch}}\cup\{x_{\bot}\})
. We define the expected and the empirical versions of policy-specific MRPs below; see
Figure
5.2
for an illustration.

(a)

Expected Version of Policy-Specific MRP.
We define

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}^{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}}=\mathrm{MRP}(\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{+},P^{\pi},r^{\pi},H,x_{\top},x_{\bot})

where

(a)

Transition Kernel
P^{\pi}
:
For any
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rch}}
and
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{+}
, we have

\displaystyle P_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}=\mathbb{E}^{\pi}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\tau\in\mathfrak{T}_{\pi}(x\rightarrow x^{\prime})\right\}\mid x_{h}=x\right],

(5.15)

where the expectation above is w.r.t. the trajectories drawn using
\pi
in the underlying MDP, and
h
denotes the time step such that
x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}
(again, in the underying MDP). Thus, the transition
P_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}
denotes the probability of taking policy
\pi
from
x
and directly transiting to
x^{\prime}
without visiting any other states in
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}
. Furthermore,
P^{\pi}_{x\rightarrow x^{\prime}}=\mathbbm{1}\left\{x^{\prime}=x_{\bot}\right\}
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}}\cup\{x_{\bot}\}
.

(b)

Reward Kernel
r^{\pi}
:

For any
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rch}}
and
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{+}
, we have

\displaystyle r_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{E}^{\pi}\left[R(\tau_{h:h^{\prime}})\mathbbm{1}\left\{\tau\in\mathfrak{T}_{\pi}(x\rightarrow x^{\prime})\right\}\mid x_{h}=x\right],

(5.16)

where
R(\tau_{h:h^{\prime}})
denotes the reward for the partial trajectory
\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}
in the underlying MDP. The reward
r_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}
denotes the expectation of rewards collected by taking policy
\pi
from
x
and directly transiting to
x^{\prime}
without visiting any other states in
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}
. Furthermore,
r^{\pi}_{x\rightarrow x^{\prime}}=0
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}}\cup\{x_{\bot}\}
.

Throughout the analysis, we use
\Pr^{\mathfrak{M}}[\cdot]\coloneqq\Pr^{\mathfrak{M}^{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}}}[\cdot]
and
\En^{\mathfrak{M}}[\cdot]\coloneqq\En^{\mathfrak{M}^{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}}}[\cdot]
as a shorthand, whenever clear from the context.

(b)

Empirical Version of Policy-Specific MRPs.
Since the learner only has sampling access to the underlying MDP, it can not directly construct the MRP
\mathfrak{M}^{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}}
. Instead, in
Algorithm
3
, the learner constructs an empirical estimate for
\mathfrak{M}^{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}}
, defined as

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}^{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}}=\mathrm{MRP}(\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{+},\widehat{P}^{\pi},\widehat{r}^{\pi},H,x_{\top},x_{\bot})

where

\bullet

Transition Kernel
\widehat{P}^{\pi}
:
For any
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rch}}
and
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{+}
, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{P}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}=\frac{|\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}|}{|\mathcal{D}_{x}|}\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{D}_{x}}\frac{\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi\rightsquigarrow\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}\right\}}{\sum_{\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}\right\}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\tau\in\mathfrak{T}_{\pi}(x\rightarrow x^{\prime})\right\},

(5.17)

where
\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}
denotes the core of the sunflower corresponding to
\Pi
and
\mathcal{D}_{x}
denotes a dataset of trajectories collected via
\mathsf{DataCollector}(x,\pi_{x},\Pi_{\mathrm{core}},n_{2})
. Furthermore,
\widehat{P}^{\pi}_{x\rightarrow x^{\prime}}=\mathbbm{1}\left\{x^{\prime}=x_{\bot}\right\}
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}}\cup\{x_{\bot}\}
.

\bullet

Reward Kernel
\widehat{r}^{\pi}
:

For any
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rch}}
and
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{+}
, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{r}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}=\frac{|\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}|}{|\mathcal{D}_{x}|}\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{D}_{x}}\frac{\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi\rightsquigarrow\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}\right\}}{\sum_{\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}\right\}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\tau\in\mathfrak{T}_{\pi}(x\rightarrow x^{\prime})\right\}R(\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}),

(5.18)

where
\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}
denotes the core of the sunflower corresponding to
\Pi
,
\mathcal{D}_{x}
denotes a dataset of trajectories collected via
\mathsf{DataCollector}(x,\pi_{x},\Pi_{\mathrm{core}},n_{2})
, and
R(\tau_{h:h^{\prime}})=\sum_{i=h}^{h^{\prime}-1}r_{i}
. Furthermore,
\widehat{r}^{\pi}_{x\rightarrow x^{\prime}}=0
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}}
.

The above approximates the MRP given by
(
5.1
)
and
(
5.2
)
.

Figure 5.2:
Illustration of an MRP
\mathfrak{M}^{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}}
with
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}=\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\}
and
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}=\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
. In the original MDP
M
,
x_{1}\in\mathcal{X}_{1}
,
x_{2}\in\mathcal{X}_{3}
, and
x_{3}\in\mathcal{X}_{2}
. The edges are labeled with the values of
P^{\pi}_{x\to x^{\prime}}
and
R^{\pi}_{x\to x^{\prime}}
. Notice that (1) there are no edges from
x_{2}\to x_{3}
or
x_{3}\to x_{2}
because trajectories cannot go from later layers to earlier ones; (2) since
x_{3}\notin\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}
, there is no edge from
x_{3}\to x_{2}
, and instead we have
P^{\pi}_{x_{3}\to x_{\bot}}=1
and
R^{\pi}_{x_{3}\to x_{\bot}}=0
; (3)
x_{\bot}
is an absorbing state with no rewards.

Properties of Trajectories in the Policy-Specific MRPs.

We state several properties of trajectories in the policy-specific MRPs, which will be used in the proofs. Let
\tau=(x_{\top},x_{1},\cdots,x_{H},x_{\bot})
denote a trajectory from either
\mathfrak{M}^{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}}
or
\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}^{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}}
.

(1)

For some
k\leq H
we have
x_{1},\cdots,x_{k}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}
and
x_{k+1}=\cdots=x_{H}=x_{\bot}
(if
k=H
we say the second condition is trivially met).

(2)

Each state in
x_{1},\cdots,x_{k}
is unique.

(3)

Letting
\mathsf{h}(x)
denote the layer that a state
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}
is in, we have
\mathsf{h}(x_{1})<\cdots<\mathsf{h}(x_{k})
.

(4)

Either (a)
x_{1},\cdots,x_{k}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rch}}
, or (b)
x_{1},\cdots,x_{k-1}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rch}}
and
x_{k}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}}
.

Parameters Used in
Algorithm
3
.

Here, we list all the parameters that are used in
Algorithm
3
and its subroutines:

\displaystyle n_{1}

\displaystyle=C_{1}\frac{(D+1)^{4}K^{2}\log(|\Pi|(D+1)/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}},

\displaystyle n_{2}

\displaystyle=C_{2}\frac{D^{3}(D+1)^{2}K^{2}\log(|\Pi|(D+1)^{2}/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{3}},

(5.19)

where
C_{1},C_{2}>0
are absolute numerical constants, which will be specified later in the proofs.

5.5.2.2  Supporting Technical Results

We start by stating the following variant of the classical simulation lemma
[
KS02
,
AJKS19
]
.

Lemma 5.7

(Simulation Lemma
[
FKQR21
, Lemma F.3]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}=(\mathcal{X},P,r,H,x_{\top},x_{\bot})
be a Markov Reward Process. Then, the empirical version
\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}=(\mathcal{X},\widehat{P},\widehat{r},H,x_{\top},x_{\bot})
corresponding to
\mathfrak{M}
satisfies:

|V_{\mathrm{MRP}}-\widehat{V}_{\mathrm{MRP}}|\leq\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}d_{\mathfrak{M}}(x)\cdot\left(\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}}|P_{x\to x^{\prime}}-\widehat{P}_{x\to x^{\prime}}|+\left|r_{x\to x^{\prime}}-\widehat{r}_{x\to x^{\prime}}\right|\right),

where
d_{\mathfrak{M}}(x)
is the probability of reaching
x
under
\mathfrak{M}
, and
V_{\mathrm{MRP}}
and
\widehat{V}_{\mathrm{MRP}}
denotes the value of
x_{\top}
under
\mathfrak{M}
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}
respectively.

The following technical lemma shows that for any policy
\pi
, the empirical version of policy-specific MRP closely approximates its expected version.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
Algorithm
3
be run with the parameters given in Eq.
(
5.19
)
, and consider any iteration of the while loop in
line
6
with the instantaneous set
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}
. Further, suppose that
|\mathcal{D}_{x}|\geq\tfrac{\varepsilon n_{2}}{24D}
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}
. Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
, the following hold:

(a)

For all
\pi\in\Pi
,
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rch}}
and
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\cup\{x_{\bot}\}
,

\displaystyle\max\left\{|P_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}-\widehat{P}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}|,\left|r_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}-\widehat{r}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}\right|\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{12D(D+1)}.

(b)

For all
\pi\in\Pi
and
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\cup\{x_{\bot}\}
,

\displaystyle\max\{|P_{x_{\top}\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}-\widehat{P}_{x_{\top}\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}|,|r^{\pi}_{x_{\top}\to x^{\prime}}-\widehat{r}^{\pi}_{x_{\top}\to x^{\prime}}|\}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{12(D+1)^{2}}.

In the sequel, we define the event that the conclusion of
Lemma
5.8
holds as
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{est}}
.

Proof.

Fix any
\pi\in\Pi
. We first prove the bound for
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rch}}
. Let
x
be at layer
h
.
Fix any policy
\pi\in\Pi
, and consider any state
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\cup\{x_{\bot}\}
, where
x^{\prime}
is at layer
h^{\prime}
. Note that since
\Pi
is a
(K,D)
-sunflower, with its core
\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}
and petals
{\{\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\}}_{\pi\in\Pi}
, we must have that any trajectory
\tau\in\mathfrak{T}_{\pi}(x\rightarrow x^{\prime})
is also consistent with at least one
\pi_{e}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}
. Furthermore, for any such
\pi_{e}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr^{\pi_{e}}\left[\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}\mid x_{h}=x\right]

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=h}^{h^{\prime}-1}P\left[x_{i+1}\mid x_{i},\pi_{e}(x_{i}),x_{h}=x\right]

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=h}^{h^{\prime}-1}P\left[x_{i+1}\mid x_{i},\pi(x_{i}),x_{h}=x\right]=\Pr^{\pi}\left[\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}\mid x_{h}=x\right],

(5.20)

where the second line holds because both
\pi\rightsquigarrow\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}
and
\pi_{e}\rightsquigarrow\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}
. Next, recall from Eq. (
5.15
), that

\displaystyle P_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{\pi}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\tau\in\mathfrak{T}_{\pi}(x\rightarrow x^{\prime})\right\}\mid x_{h}=x\right].

(5.21)

Furthermore, from Eq. (
5.17
), recall that the empirical estimate
\widehat{P}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}
of
{P}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}
is given by :

\displaystyle\widehat{P}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{D}_{x}|}\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{D}_{x}}\frac{\mathbbm{1}\left\{\tau\in\mathfrak{T}_{\pi}(x\rightarrow x^{\prime})\right\}}{\frac{1}{|\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}|}\sum_{\pi_{e}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi_{e}\rightsquigarrow\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}\right\}},

(5.22)

where the dataset
\mathcal{D}_{x}
consists of i.i.d. samples, and is collected in lines
10
-
12
in
Algorithm
4
(
\mathsf{DataCollector}
), by first running the policy
\pi_{x}
for
h
timesteps and if the trajectory reaches
x
, then executing
\pi_{e}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\Pi_{\mathrm{core}})
for the remaining time steps (otherwise this trajectory is rejected). Let the law of this process be
q
. We thus note that,

\displaystyle\hskip-28.90755pt\En_{\tau\sim q}\left[\widehat{P}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}\right]

\displaystyle=\En_{\tau\sim q}\left[\frac{\mathbbm{1}\left\{\tau\in\mathfrak{T}_{\pi}(x\rightarrow x^{\prime})\right\}}{\frac{1}{|\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}|}\sum_{\pi_{e}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi_{e}\rightsquigarrow\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}\right\}}\mid x_{h}=x\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in\mathfrak{T}_{\pi}(x\rightarrow x^{\prime})}\Pr_{q}\left[\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}\mid x_{h}=x\right]\cdot\frac{1}{\frac{1}{|\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}|}\sum_{\pi_{e}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi_{e}\rightsquigarrow\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}\right\}}

\displaystyle\overset{\left(i\right)}{=}\sum_{\tau\in\mathfrak{T}_{\pi}(x\rightarrow x^{\prime})}\frac{1}{|\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}|}\sum_{\pi^{\prime}_{e}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\tau\in\mathfrak{T}_{\pi^{\prime}_{e}}(x\rightarrow x^{\prime})\right\}\Pr^{\pi^{\prime}_{e}}\left[\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}\mid x_{h}=x\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 120.00018pt\cdot\frac{1}{\frac{1}{|\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}|}\sum_{\pi_{e}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi_{e}\rightsquigarrow\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}\right\}}

\displaystyle\overset{\left(ii\right)}{=}\sum_{\tau\in\mathfrak{T}_{\pi}(x\rightarrow x^{\prime})}\frac{1}{|\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}|}\sum_{\pi^{\prime}_{e}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}}\Pr^{\pi}\left[\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}\mid x_{h}=x\right]\cdot\frac{\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi^{\prime}_{e}\rightsquigarrow\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}\right\}}{\frac{1}{|\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}|}\sum_{\pi_{e}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi_{e}\rightsquigarrow\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}\right\}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in\mathfrak{T}_{\pi}(x\rightarrow x^{\prime})}\Pr^{\pi}\left[\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}\mid x_{h}=x\right]

\displaystyle\overset{\left(iii\right)}{=}\mathbb{E}^{\pi}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\tau\in\mathfrak{T}_{\pi}(x\rightarrow x^{\prime})\right\}\mid x_{h}=x\right]=P_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi},

where
\left(i\right)
follows from the sampling strategy in
Algorithm
4
after observing
x_{h}=x
, and
\left(ii\right)
simply uses the relation
(
5.20
)
since both
\pi^{\prime}_{e}\rightsquigarrow\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}
and
\pi\rightsquigarrow\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}
hold. Finally, in
\left(iii\right)
, we use the relation
(
5.21
)
.

We have shown that
\widehat{P}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}
is an unbiased estimate of
P^{\pi}_{x\to x^{\prime}}
for any
\pi
and
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{+}
. Thus, using Hoeffding’s inequality (
Lemma
A.1
), followed by a union bound, we get that with probability at least
1-\delta/4
, for all
\pi\in\Pi
,
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rch}}
, and
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\cup\{x_{\bot}\}
,

\displaystyle|\widehat{P}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}-P_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}|\leq K\sqrt{\frac{2\log(4|\Pi|D(D+1)/\delta)}{|\mathcal{D}_{x}|}},

where the additional factor of
K
appears because for any
\tau\in\mathfrak{T}_{\pi}(x\rightarrow x^{\prime})
, there must exist some
\pi_{e}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}
that is also consistent with
\tau
(as we showed above), which implies that each of the terms in Eq. (
5.22
) satisfies the bound a.s.:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\frac{\mathbbm{1}\left\{\tau\in\mathfrak{T}_{\pi}(x\rightarrow x^{\prime})\right\}}{\tfrac{1}{|\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}|}\sum_{\pi_{e}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi_{e}\rightsquigarrow\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}\right\}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lvert\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}\rvert=K.

Since
|\mathcal{D}_{x}|\geq\tfrac{\varepsilon n_{2}}{24D}
by assumption, we have

\displaystyle|\widehat{P}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}-P_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}|\leq K\sqrt{\frac{48D\log(4|\Pi|D(D+1)/\delta)}{\varepsilon n_{2}}}.

Repeating a similar argument for the empirical reward estimation in Eq.
(
5.17
)
, we get that with probability at least
1-\delta/4
, for all
\pi\in\Pi
, and
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rch}}
and
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\cup\{x_{\bot}\}
, we have that

\displaystyle|\widehat{r}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}-r_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}|\leq K\sqrt{\frac{48D\log(4|\Pi|D(D+1)/\delta)}{\varepsilon n_{2}}}.

Similarly, we can also get for any
\pi\in\Pi
and
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\cup\{x_{\bot}\}
, with probability at least
1-\delta/2
,

\displaystyle\max\left\{|\widehat{r}_{x_{\top}\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}-r_{x_{\top}\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}|,|\widehat{P}_{x_{\top}\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}-P_{x_{\top}\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}|\right\}

\displaystyle\leq K\sqrt{\frac{2\log(4|\Pi|(D+1)/\delta)}{|\mathcal{D}_{x_{\top}}|}}

\displaystyle=K\sqrt{\frac{2\log(4|\Pi|(D+1)/\delta)}{n_{1}}},

where the last line simply uses the fact that
\lvert\mathcal{D}_{x_{\top}}\rvert=n_{1}
.
The final statement is due to a union bound on the above results. This concludes the proof of
Lemma
5.8
.∎

Lemma 5.9
.

Fix a policy
\pi\in\Pi
and a set of reachable states
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}
, and consider the policy-specific MRP
\mathfrak{M}^{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}}
(as defined by Eqs. (
5.15
) and (
5.16
)). Then for any
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}}
, the quantity
\bar{d}^{\pi}(x;\neg\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}})=d^{\mathfrak{M}}(x)
, where
d^{\mathfrak{M}}(x)
is the occupancy of state
x
in
\mathfrak{M}^{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}}
.

Proof.

We use
\bar{\tau}
to denote a trajectory in
\mathfrak{M}^{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}}
and
\tau
to denote a “corresponding” (in a sense which will be described shortly) trajectory in the original MDP
M
. For any
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}}
, we have

\displaystyle d^{\mathfrak{M}}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bar{\tau}\penalty\ \text{s.t.}\penalty\ s\in\bar{\tau}}\Pr^{\mathfrak{M}}\left[\bar{\tau}\right]=\sum_{k=0}^{H-1}\sum_{\bar{x}_{1},\bar{x}_{2},\cdots,\bar{x}_{k}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rch}}}\Pr^{\mathfrak{M}}\left[\bar{\tau}=(x_{\top},\bar{x}_{1},\cdots,\bar{x}_{k},x,x_{\bot},\cdots)\right].

The first equality is simply due to the definition of
d^{\mathfrak{M}}
. For the second equality, we sum up over all possible sequences which start at
x_{\top}
, pass through some (variable length) sequence of states
\bar{x}_{1},\cdots,\bar{x}_{k}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rch}}
, then reach
x
and the terminal state
x_{\perp}
. By definition of the policy-specific MRP, we know that once the MRP transits to a state
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}}
, it must then transit to
x_{\bot}
and repeat
x_{\bot}
until the end of the episode.

Now fix a sequence
\bar{x}_{1},\cdots,\bar{x}_{k}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rch}}
. We relate the term in the summand to the probability of corresponding trajectories in the original MDP
M
. To avoid confusion, we let
x_{h_{1}},\dots,x_{h_{k}}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rch}}
denote the corresponding sequence of states in the original MDP, which are unique and satisfy
h_{1}<h_{2}<\cdots<h_{k}
. We also denote
x_{h_{k+1}}=x
.

Using the definition of
\mathfrak{M}^{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}}
, we write

\displaystyle\hskip-36.135pt\Pr^{\mathfrak{M}}\left[\bar{\tau}=(x_{\top},\bar{x}_{1},\cdots,\bar{x}_{k},x,x_{\bot},\cdots)\right]

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\Pr^{M,\pi}\left[\tau_{h_{i}:h_{i+1}}\in\mathfrak{T}_{\pi}(x_{h_{i}}\rightarrow x_{h_{i+1}})\mid\tau[h_{i}]=x_{h_{i}}\right]

\displaystyle=\Pr^{M,\pi}\left[\forall i\in[k+1],\penalty\ \tau[h_{i}]=x_{h_{i}}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \forall h\in[h_{k+1}]\backslash\{h_{1},\cdots,h_{k+1}\},\penalty\ \tau[h]\notin\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\right].

Now we sum over all sequences
\bar{x}_{1},\cdots,\bar{x}_{k}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rch}}
to get

\displaystyle d^{\mathfrak{M}}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{H-1}\sum_{x_{h_{1}},\cdots,x_{h_{k}}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rch}}}\Pr^{M,\pi}\left[\forall i\in[k+1],\penalty\ \tau[h_{i}]=x_{h_{i}}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \forall h\in[h_{k+1}]\backslash\{h_{1},\cdots,h_{k+1}\},\penalty\ \tau[h]\notin\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\right]

\displaystyle=\Pr^{M,\pi}\left[x\in\tau\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \forall h\in[h_{k+1}-1],\penalty\ \tau[h]\notin\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}}\right]

\displaystyle=\Pr^{\pi}\left[\tau\in\mathfrak{T}(x_{\top}\rightarrow x;\neg\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}})\right]=\bar{d}^{\pi}(x;\neg\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}}).

The second equality follows from the definition of
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}}
, and the last line is the definition of the
\bar{d}
notation. This concludes the proof of
Lemma
5.9
.
∎

Lemma 5.10
.

With probability at least
1-2\delta
, any
(\bar{x},\pi)
that is added into
\mathcal{T}
(in
line
14
of
Algorithm
3
) satisfies
d^{\pi}(\bar{x})\geq\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{12D}}
.

Proof.

For any
(\bar{x},\pi)\in\mathcal{T}
, when we collect
\mathcal{D}_{\bar{x}}
in
Algorithm
4
, the probability that a trajectory will be accepted (i.e. the trajectory satisfies the “if” statement in
line
10
) is exactly
d^{\pi}({\bar{x}})
. Thus, using Hoeffding’s inequality (
Lemma
A.1
), with probability at least
1-\nicefrac{{\delta}}{{D|\Pi|}}
,

\left|\frac{|\mathcal{D}_{\bar{x}}|}{n_{2}}-d^{\pi}({\bar{x}})\right|\leq\sqrt{\frac{2\log(D|\Pi|/\delta)}{n_{2}}}.

Since
|\mathcal{T}|\leq D|\Pi|
, by union bound, the above holds for every
(\bar{x},\pi)\in\mathcal{T}
with probability at least
1-\delta
. Let us denote this event as
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{data}}
. Under
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{data}}
, for any
(\bar{x},\pi)
that satisfies
d^{\pi}(\bar{x})\geq\tfrac{\varepsilon}{12D}
,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{D}_{\bar{x}}|

\displaystyle\geq n_{2}d^{\pi}(\bar{x})-\sqrt{2n_{2}\log(D|\Pi|/\delta)}\geq\frac{\varepsilon n_{2}}{12D}-\frac{\varepsilon n_{2}}{24D}=\frac{\varepsilon n_{2}}{24D},

(5.23)

where the second inequality follows by the bound on
d^{\pi}(\bar{x})
and our choice of parameter
n_{2}
in Eq. (
5.19
).

In the following, we prove by induction that every
(\bar{x},\pi)
that is added into
\mathcal{T}
in the while loop from lines
8
-
15
in
Algorithm
3
satisfies
d^{\pi}(\bar{x})\geq\tfrac{\varepsilon}{12D}
. This is trivially true at initialization when
\mathcal{T}=\{(x_{\top},\mathrm{Null})\}
, since every trajectory starts at the dummy state
x_{\top}
, for which we have
d^{\mathrm{Null}}(x_{\top})=1
.

We now proceed to the induction hypothesis. Suppose that in some iteration of the while loop, every tuple
(\bar{x},\pi)\in\mathcal{T}
satisfies
d^{\pi}(\bar{x})\geq\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{12D}}
, and that
(\bar{x}^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
is a new tuple that will be added to
\mathcal{T}
. We will show that
(\bar{x}^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
will also satisfy
d^{\pi^{\prime}}({\bar{x}^{\prime}})\geq\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{12D}}
.

Recall that
\mathcal{X}_{\pi^{\prime}}^{+}=\mathcal{X}_{\pi^{\prime}}\cup\{x_{\top},x_{\bot}\}
,
\mathcal{X}_{\pi^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{rch}}=\mathcal{X}_{\pi^{\prime}}^{+}\cap\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}
, and
\mathcal{X}_{\pi^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{rem}}=\mathcal{X}_{\pi^{\prime}}^{+}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{\pi^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{rch}}
. Let
\mathfrak{M}^{\pi^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}}=\mathrm{MRP}(\mathcal{X}_{\pi^{\prime}}^{+},P^{\pi^{\prime}},r^{\pi^{\prime}},H,x_{\top},x_{\bot})
be the policy-specific MRP, where
P^{\pi^{\prime}}
and
r^{\pi^{\prime}}
are defined in Eqs.
(
5.15
)
and
(
5.16
)
respectively for the policy
\pi^{\prime}
. Similarly let
\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}^{\pi^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}}=\mathrm{MRP}(\mathcal{X}_{\pi^{\prime}}^{+},\widehat{P}^{\pi^{\prime}},\widehat{r}^{\pi^{\prime}},H,x_{\top},x_{\bot})
denote the estimated policy-specific MRP, where
\widehat{P}^{\pi^{\prime}}
and
r^{\pi^{\prime}}
are defined using
(
5.17
)
and
(
5.18
)
respectively. Note that for any state
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{rch}}
, the bound in
(
5.23
)
holds.

For the rest of the proof, we assume that the event
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{est}}
, defined in
Lemma
5.8
, holds (this happens with probability at least
1-\delta
). By definition of
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{est}}
, we have

|P_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi^{\prime}}-\widehat{P}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi^{\prime}}|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{12D(D+1)},\qquad\text{for all}\qquad x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi^{\prime}}\cup\{x_{\bot}\}.

(5.24)

Furthermore, note that
\widehat{d}^{\pi^{\prime}}(\bar{x}^{\prime})\leftarrow\mathsf{EstReachability}(\mathcal{X}_{\pi^{\prime}}^{+},\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}^{\pi^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}},\bar{x}^{\prime})
since in
Algorithm
5
we start with
V(x)=\mathbbm{1}\left\{x=\bar{x}^{\prime}\right\}
. Furthermore, using
Lemma
5.7
, we have

\displaystyle|\widehat{d}^{\pi^{\prime}}(\bar{x}^{\prime})-d^{\mathfrak{M}}(\bar{x}^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq(D+1)\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{rch}},x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi^{\prime}}\cup\{x_{\bot}\}}|\widehat{P}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi^{\prime}}-P_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi^{\prime}}|

(5.25)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{12D(D+1)}\cdot(D+1)=\frac{\varepsilon}{12D}.

where the second inequality follows from (
5.24
). Additionally,
Lemma
5.9
states that
d^{\mathfrak{M}}(\bar{x}^{\prime})=\bar{d}^{\pi^{\prime}}(\bar{x}^{\prime};\neg\mathcal{X}_{\pi^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{rem}})
. Therefore we obtain

|\bar{d}^{\pi^{\prime}}(\bar{x}^{\prime};\neg\mathcal{X}_{\pi^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{rem}})-\widehat{d}^{\pi^{\prime}}(\bar{x}^{\prime})|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{12D}.

Thus, if the new state-policy pair
(\bar{x}^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
is added into
\mathcal{T}
, we will have

\bar{d}^{\pi^{\prime}}(\bar{x}^{\prime};\neg\mathcal{X}_{\pi^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{rem}})\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{6D}-\frac{\varepsilon}{12D}=\frac{\varepsilon}{12D}.

Furthermore, by definition of
\bar{d}
we have

\bar{d}^{\pi^{\prime}}(\bar{x}^{\prime};\neg\mathcal{X}_{\pi^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{rem}})=\Pr^{\pi^{\prime}}\left[\tau\in\mathfrak{T}(x_{\top}\rightarrow\bar{x}^{\prime};\neg\mathcal{X}_{\pi^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{rem}})\right]\leq\Pr^{\pi^{\prime}}[\bar{x}^{\prime}\in\tau]=d^{\pi^{\prime}}(\bar{x}^{\prime}),

so we have proved the induction hypothesis
d^{\pi^{\prime}}(\bar{x}^{\prime})\geq\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{12D}}
for the next round. This concludes the proof of
Lemma
5.10
.
∎

The next lemma establishes that
Algorithm
3
will terminate after finitely many rounds, and that after termination will have explored all sufficiently reachable states.

Lemma 5.11
.

With probability at least
1-2\delta
,

(a)

The while loop in
line
6
in
Algorithm
3
will terminate after at most
\tfrac{12HD\mathfrak{C}(\Pi)}{\varepsilon}
rounds.

(b)

After the termination of the while loop, for any
\pi\in\Pi
, the remaining states
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}}
that are not added to
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}

satisfy
\bar{d}^{\pi}(x;\neg\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}})\leq\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{4D}}
.

Notice that according to our algorithm, the same state cannot be added multiple times into
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}
. Therefore,
|\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}|\leq D|\Pi|
, and the maximum number of rounds of the while loop is
D|\Pi|
(i.e., the while loop eventually terminates).

Proof.

We prove each part separately.

(a)

First, note that from the definition of coverability and
Lemma
2.1
, we have

\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\sup_{\pi\in\Pi}d^{\pi}(x)\leq HC^{\mathsf{cov}}(\Pi;M)\leq H\mathfrak{C}(\Pi).

Furthermore,
Lemma
5.10
states that every
(x,\pi_{x})\in\mathcal{T}
satisfies
d^{\pi_{x}}(x)\geq\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{12D}}
. Thus, at any point in
Algorithm
3
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}}\sup_{\pi\in\Pi}d^{\pi}(x)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}}d^{\pi_{x}}(x)\geq\lvert\mathcal{T}\rvert\cdot\frac{\varepsilon}{12D}.

Since,
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
, the two bounds indicate that

|\mathcal{T}|\leq\frac{12HD\mathfrak{C}(\Pi)}{\varepsilon}.

Since every iteration of the while loop adds one new
(x,\pi_{x})
to
\mathcal{T}
, the while loop terminates after at most
\nicefrac{{12HD\mathfrak{C}(\Pi)}}{{\varepsilon}}
many rounds.

(b)

We know that once the while loop has terminated, for every
\pi\in\Pi
and
\bar{x}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}}
, we must have
\widehat{d}^{\pi}(\bar{x})\leq\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{6D}}
, or else the condition in
line
13
in
Algorithm
3
is violated.

Fix any such
(\bar{x},\pi)
pair. Inspecting the proof of
Lemma
5.10
, we see that

\displaystyle|\bar{d}^{\pi}(\bar{x};\neg\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}})-\widehat{d}^{\pi}(\bar{x})|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{12D}.

To conclude, we get

\bar{d}^{\pi}(x;\neg\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}})\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{6D}+\frac{\varepsilon}{12D}=\frac{\varepsilon}{4D}.

This concludes the proof of
Lemma
5.11
.
∎

Lemma 5.12
.

Suppose that the conclusions of Lemmas
5.8
and
5.11
hold. Then for every
\pi\in\Pi
, the estimated value
\widehat{V}^{\pi}
computed in
Algorithm
3
satisfies

|\widehat{V}^{\pi}-V^{\pi}|\leq\varepsilon.

Proof.

We will break up the proof into two steps. First, we show that for any
\pi
, the value estimate
\widehat{V}^{\pi}
obtained using the empirical policy-specific MRP
\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}^{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}}
is close to its value in the policy-specific MRP
\mathfrak{M}^{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}}
, as defined via (
5.15
) and (
5.16
). We denote this quantity as
V_{\mathrm{MRP}}^{\pi}
. Then, we will show that
V_{\mathrm{MRP}}^{\pi}
is close to
V^{\pi}
, the value of the policy
\pi
in the original MDP
M
.

Part 1:
\widehat{V}^{\pi}
is close to
V_{\mathrm{MRP}}^{\pi}
.

Note that the output
\widehat{V}^{\pi}
of
Algorithm
6
is exactly the value function of MRP
\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}^{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}}
defined by Eqs. (
5.17
) and (
5.18
). When
D=0
, by part (b) of
Lemma
5.8
, we obtain

|\widehat{V}^{\pi}-V_{\mathrm{MRP}}^{\pi}|=|\widehat{r}^{\pi}_{x_{\top}\to x_{\bot}}-r^{\pi}_{x_{\top}\to x_{\bot}}|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{12(D+1)^{2}}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

When
D\geq 1
, using
Lemma
5.8
, we have

\displaystyle\forall x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\cup\{x_{\bot}\}:

\displaystyle\qquad|r_{x_{\top}\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}-\widehat{r}_{x_{\top}\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{12(D+1)^{2}},\quad|P_{x_{\top}\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}-\widehat{P}_{x_{\top}\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{12(D+1)^{2}},

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rch}},x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{+}\cup\{x_{\bot}\}:

\displaystyle\qquad|r_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}-\widehat{r}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{12D(D+1)},\quad|P_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}-\widehat{P}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{12D(D+1)}.

By the simulation lemma (
Lemma
5.7
), we get

\displaystyle|\widehat{V}^{\pi}-V_{\mathrm{MRP}}^{\pi}|

\displaystyle\leq 2(D+2)\max_{s,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{+}}\left(\left|P_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}-\widehat{P}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}\right|+\left|r_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}-\widehat{r}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}\right|\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2(D+2)\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{12D(D+1)}+\frac{\varepsilon}{12D(D+1)}\right)\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

Part 2:
V_{\mathrm{MRP}}^{\pi}
is close to
V^{\pi}
.

As in the proof of
Lemma
5.9
, let us consider different trajectories
\bar{\tau}
that are possible in
\mathfrak{M}^{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}}
. We can represent
\bar{\tau}=(x_{\top},\bar{x}_{1},\cdots,\bar{x}_{k},x_{\bot},\cdots)
where the states
\bar{x}_{1},\cdots,\bar{x}_{k}
are distinct and all except possibly
\bar{x}_{k}
belong to
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rch}}
, and all subsequent states after
x_{\bot}
are repetitions of
x_{\bot}
until the end of the episode. Let
x_{h_{1}},x_{h_{2}},\dots,x_{h_{k}}
be the same sequence (in the original MDP
M
). Again, we have

\displaystyle\hskip-72.26999pt\Pr^{\mathfrak{M}}[\bar{\tau}=(x_{\top},\bar{x}_{1},\cdots,\bar{x}_{k},x_{\bot},\cdots)]

\displaystyle=\Pr^{\pi}\left[\forall i\in[k],\penalty\ \tau[h_{i}]=x_{h_{i}},\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \forall h\in[H]\backslash\{h_{1},\cdots,h_{k}\},\penalty\ \tau[h]\notin\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\right],

where recall that
\Pr^{\mathfrak{M}}
denotes probability under the
\mathfrak{M}^{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}}
, and
\Pr^{\pi}
denotes the probability under trajectories drawn according to
\pi
in the underlying MDP;
\mathbb{E}^{\mathfrak{M}}
and
\mathbb{E}^{\pi}
are defined similarly.

Furthermore, the expectation of rewards collected in
\mathfrak{M}^{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}}
with trajectories
\bar{\tau}
is

\displaystyle\hskip-72.26999pt\mathbb{E}^{\mathfrak{M}}\left[R[\bar{\tau}]\mathbbm{1}\left\{(x_{\top},\bar{x}_{1},\cdots,\bar{x}_{k},x_{\bot},\cdots)\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\pi}\left[R[\tau]\mathbbm{1}\left\{\forall i\in[k],\penalty\ \tau[h_{i}]=x_{h_{i}},\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \forall h\in[H]\backslash\{h_{1},\cdots,h_{k}\},\penalty\ \tau[h]\notin\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\right\}\right].

Next, we sum over all possible trajectories. However, note that the only trajectories that are possible in
M
whose corresponding trajectories are
not accounted for
in
\mathfrak{M}^{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rch}}}
are precisely those that visit states in
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}
, after visiting some
x_{h_{k}}
in the remaining states
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}}
(since, by construction, the MRP transitions directly to
x_{\bot}
after encountering a state in
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}}
). Thus,

V_{\mathrm{MRP}}^{\pi}=\mathbb{E}^{\pi}\left[R[\tau]\left(\mathbbm{1}\left\{\tau\cap\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}}=\emptyset\right\}+\mathbbm{1}\left\{\exists k\in[H]:x_{h_{k}}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}}\text{ and }\forall h>h_{k}:x_{h}\notin\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\right\}\right)\right],

where the first term corresponds to trajectories that do not pass through
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}}
, and the second term corresponds to trajectories that pass through some state in
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}}
but then does not go through any other state in
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}
. On the other hand,

\displaystyle V^{\pi}=\mathbb{E}^{\pi}\left[R[\tau]\right].

Clearly,
V_{\mathrm{MRP}}^{\pi}\leq V^{\pi}
. Furthermore, we also have

\displaystyle V^{\pi}-V_{\mathrm{MRP}}^{\pi}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{\pi}\left[R[\tau]\mathbbm{1}\left\{\tau\cap\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}}\neq\emptyset\right\}-\mathbbm{1}\left\{\exists k\in[H]:x_{h_{k}}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}}\text{ and }\forall h>h_{k}:x_{h}\notin\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}^{\pi}\left[R[\tau]\mathbbm{1}\left\{\tau\cap\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}}\neq\emptyset\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq D\cdot\frac{\varepsilon}{4D}=\frac{\varepsilon}{4},

where the first inequality follows by omitting the second indicator term, and the second inequality follows by taking a union bound over all possible values of
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}}
as well as the conclusion of
Lemma
5.11
.

Putting it all together, we get that

\displaystyle|\widehat{V}^{\pi}-V^{\pi}|\leq|V^{\pi}-V_{\mathrm{MRP}}^{\pi}|+|\widehat{V}^{\pi}-V_{\mathrm{MRP}}^{\pi}|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}<\varepsilon.

This concludes the proof of
Lemma
5.12
.
∎

5.5.2.3  Proof of
Theorem
5.2

We assume the events defined in Lemmas
5.8
,
5.10
and
5.11
hold (which happens with probability at least
1-2\delta
). With our choices of
n_{1},n_{2}
in Eq. (
5.19
), the total number of samples used in our algorithm is at most

n_{1}+n_{2}\cdot\frac{12HD\mathfrak{C}(\Pi)}{\varepsilon}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{HD^{6}\mathfrak{C}(\Pi)}{\varepsilon^{4}}\right)\cdot K^{2}\log\frac{|\Pi|}{\delta}\right).

After the termination of the while loop, we know that for any policy
\pi\in\Pi
and
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}}
we have

\bar{d}^{\pi}(x;\neg\mathcal{X}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{rem}})\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{4D}.

Therefore, by
Lemma
5.12
, we know for every
\pi\in\Pi
,
|\widehat{V}^{\pi}-V^{\pi}|\leq\varepsilon
. Hence the output policy
\widehat{\pi}\in\argmax_{\pi}\widehat{V}^{\pi}
satisfies

\max_{\pi\in\Pi}V^{\pi}-V^{\widehat{\pi}}\leq 2\varepsilon+\widehat{V}^{\pi}-\widehat{V}^{\widehat{\pi}}\leq 2\varepsilon.

Rescaling
\varepsilon
by
2\varepsilon
and
\delta
by
2\delta
concludes the proof of
Theorem
5.2
.∎

5.5.2.4  Extending
Theorem
5.2
to Infinite Policy Classes

The modified analysis for the online RL upper bound (
Theorem
5.2
) proceeds similarly as in
Section
4.2.2
; we sketch the ideas below.

There are two places in the proof of
Theorem
5.2
which require a union bound over
\lvert\Pi\rvert
: the event
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{est}}
(defined by
Lemma
5.8
) that the estimated transitions and rewards of the MRPs are close to their population versions, and the event
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{data}}
(defined by
Lemma
5.10
) that the datasets collected are large enough. The latter is easy to address, since we can simply modify the algorithm’s while loop to break after
\mathcal{O}\left(\tfrac{HD\mathfrak{C}(\Pi)}{\varepsilon}\right)
iterations and union bound over the size of the set
\lvert\mathcal{T}\rvert
instead of the worst-case bound on the size
D\lvert\Pi\rvert
. For
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{data}}
, we follow a similar strategy as the analysis for the generative model upper bound.

Fix a state
x
. Recall that the estimate for the probability transition kernel in the MDP in Eq. (
5.17
) takes the form

\displaystyle\widehat{P}_{x\to x^{\prime}}^{\pi}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{D}_{x}|}\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{D}_{x}}\frac{\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi\rightsquigarrow\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}\right\}}{\tfrac{1}{|\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}|}\sum_{\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi_{e}\rightsquigarrow\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}\right\}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\tau\in\mathfrak{T}_{\pi}(x\rightarrow x^{\prime})\right\}.

(The analysis for the rewards is similar, so we omit it from this proof sketch.)

We set up some notation. Define the function
p^{\pi}_{x\to x^{\prime}}:(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathbb{R})^{H}\to[0,\lvert\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}\rvert]
as

\displaystyle p^{\pi}_{x\to x^{\prime}}(\tau)\coloneqq\frac{\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi\rightsquigarrow\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}\right\}}{\tfrac{1}{|\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}|}\sum_{\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi_{e}\rightsquigarrow\tau_{h:h^{\prime}}\right\}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\tau\in\mathfrak{T}_{\pi}(x\rightarrow x^{\prime})\right\},

(5.26)

with the implicit restriction of the domain to trajectories
\tau
for which the denominator is nonzero. We have
\En[p^{\pi}_{x\to x^{\prime}}(\tau)]=P^{\pi}_{x\rightarrow x^{\prime}}
. Also let
\Pi_{x}=\left\{\pi\in\Pi:x\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\right\}
.

Restated in this notation, our objective is to show the uniform convergence guarantee

\displaystyle\text{w.p.\penalty\ at least}\penalty\ 1-\delta,\quad\sup_{\pi\in\Pi_{x},x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}}\Big|\frac{1}{|\mathcal{D}_{x}|}\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{D}_{x}}p^{\pi}_{x\to x^{\prime}}(\tau)-\En[p^{\pi}_{x\to x^{\prime}}(\tau)]\Big|\leq\varepsilon.

(5.27)

Again, in light of
Lemma
A.7
, we need to compute the pseudodimension for the function class
\mathcal{P}^{\Pi_{x}}=\left\{p^{\pi}_{x\to x^{\prime}}:\pi\in\Pi_{x},x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\right\}
, since these are all possible transitions that we might use the dataset
\mathcal{D}_{s}
to evaluate. Define the subgraph class

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\Pi_{x},+}\coloneqq\{(\tau,\theta)\mapsto\mathbbm{1}\left\{p^{\pi}_{x\to x^{\prime}}(\tau)\leq\theta\right\}:\pi\in\Pi_{x},x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\}.

Fix the set
Z=\left\{(\tau_{1},\theta_{1}),\cdots,(\tau_{d},\theta_{d})\right\}\in((\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathbb{R})^{H}\times\mathbb{R})^{d}
, where the trajectories
\tau_{1},\cdots,\tau_{d}
pass through
x
. We also denote
\mathcal{X}_{Z}
to be the union of all states which appear in
\tau_{1},\cdots,\tau_{d}
. In order to show a bound that
\mathrm{Pdim}(\mathcal{P}^{\Pi_{x}})\leq d
it suffices to prove that
\lvert\mathcal{P}^{\Pi_{x},+}\big|_{Z}\rvert<2^{d}
.

We first observe that

\displaystyle\lvert\mathcal{P}^{\Pi_{x},+}\big|_{Z}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{Z}}\left\lvert\left\{\left(\mathbbm{1}\left\{p^{\pi}_{x\to x^{\prime}}(\tau_{1})\leq\theta_{1}\right\},\cdots,\mathbbm{1}\left\{p^{\pi}_{x\to x^{\prime}}(\tau_{d})\leq\theta_{d}\right\}\right):\pi\in\Pi_{x}\right\}\right\rvert.

The inequality follows because for any choice
x^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{X}_{Z}
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\mathbbm{1}\left\{p^{\pi}_{x\to x^{\prime}}(\tau_{1})\leq\theta_{1}\right\},\cdots,\mathbbm{1}\left\{p^{\pi}_{x\to x^{\prime}}(\tau_{d})\leq\theta_{d}\right\}\right)=\vec{0},

no matter what
\pi
is, contributing at most 1 to the count. Furthermore, once we have fixed
x^{\prime}
and the
\{\tau_{1},\cdots,\tau_{d}\}
the quantities
\tfrac{1}{|\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}|}\sum_{\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi_{e}\rightsquigarrow\tau_{i,h:h^{\prime}}\right\}
for every
i\in[d]
are constant (do not depend on
\pi
), so we can reparameterize
\theta_{i}^{\prime}\coloneqq\theta_{i}\cdot\tfrac{1}{|\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}|}\sum_{\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{core}}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi_{e}\rightsquigarrow\tau_{i,h:h^{\prime}}\right\}
to get:

\displaystyle\lvert\mathcal{P}^{\Pi_{x},+}\big|_{Z}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{Z}}\left\lvert\left\{(b_{1}(\pi),\cdots,b_{d}(\pi)):\pi\in\Pi\right\}\right\rvert,

(5.28)

\displaystyle\quad\text{where}\quad b_{i}(\pi)\coloneqq\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi\rightsquigarrow\tau_{i,h:h^{\prime}}\right\}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\tau_{i}\in\mathfrak{T}_{\pi}(x\rightarrow x^{\prime})\right\}\leq\theta_{i}^{\prime}\right\}.

Now we count how many values the vector
(b_{1}(\pi),\cdots,b_{d}(\pi))
can take for different
\pi\in\Pi_{x}
. Without loss of generality, we can (1) assume that the
\theta_{i}^{\prime}=0
(since a product of indicators can only take values in
\{0,1\}
, and if
\theta^{\prime}\geq 1
then we must have
b_{i}(\pi)=1
for every
\pi
), and (2)
x^{\prime}\in\tau_{i}
for each
i\in[d]
(otherwise
b_{i}(\pi)=0
for every
\pi\in\Pi
). So we can rewrite
b_{i}(\pi)=\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi\rightsquigarrow\tau_{i,h:h^{\prime}}\right\}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\tau_{i}\in\mathfrak{T}_{\pi}(x\rightarrow x^{\prime})\right\}
. For every fixed choice of
x^{\prime}
we upper bound the size of the set as:

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\left\lvert\left\{\left(b_{1}(\pi),\cdots,b_{d}(\pi)\right):\pi\in\Pi\right\}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\overset{\left(i\right)}{\leq{}}

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\{\left(\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi\rightsquigarrow\tau_{1,h:h^{\prime}}\right\},\cdots,\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi\rightsquigarrow\tau_{d,h:h^{\prime}}\right\}\right):\pi\in\Pi\right\}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\left\lvert\left\{\left(\mathbbm{1}\left\{\tau_{1}\in\mathfrak{T}_{\pi}(x\rightarrow x^{\prime})\right\},\cdots,\mathbbm{1}\left\{\tau_{d}\in\mathfrak{T}_{\pi}(x\rightarrow x^{\prime})\right\}\right):\pi\in\Pi\right\}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\overset{\left(ii\right)}{\leq{}}

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\{\left(\pi(x^{(1)}),\pi(x^{(2)}),\cdots,\pi(x^{(dH)})\right):\pi\in\Pi\right\}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\quad\times\left\lvert\left\{\left(\mathbbm{1}\left\{x^{(1)}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\right\},\cdots,\mathbbm{1}\left\{x^{(dH)}\in\mathcal{X}_{\pi}\right\}\right):\pi\in\Pi\right\}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\overset{\left(iii\right)}{\leq{}}

\displaystyle\left(\frac{dH\cdot e(A+1)^{2}}{2\mathrm{Ndim}(\Pi)}\right)^{\mathrm{Ndim}(\Pi)}\times(dH)^{D}.

(5.29)

The inequality
\left(i\right)
follows by upper bounding by the Cartesian product. The inequality
\left(ii\right)
follows because (1) for the first term, the vector
\left(\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi\rightsquigarrow\tau_{1,h:h^{\prime}}\right\},\cdots,\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi\rightsquigarrow\tau_{d,h:h^{\prime}}\right\}\right)
is determined by the number of possible behaviors
\pi
has over all
dH
states in the trajectories, and (2) for the second term, the vector
\left(\mathbbm{1}\left\{\tau_{1}\in\mathfrak{T}_{\pi}(x\rightarrow x^{\prime})\right\},\cdots,\mathbbm{1}\left\{\tau_{d}\in\mathfrak{T}_{\pi}(x\rightarrow x^{\prime})\right\}\right)
is determined by which of the
dH
states lie in the petal set for
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}
. The inequality
\left(iii\right)
follows by applying
Lemma
A.6
to the first term and Sauer’s Lemma to the second term, further noting that every petal
\mathcal{X}_{\pi}
set has cardinality at most
D
.

Combining Eqs. (
5.28
) and (
5.29
) we get the final bound that

\displaystyle\lvert\mathcal{P}^{\Pi_{x},+}\big|_{Z}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 1+(dH)^{D+1}\cdot\left(\frac{dH\cdot e(A+1)^{2}}{2\mathrm{Ndim}(\Pi)}\right)^{\mathrm{Ndim}(\Pi)}.

To conclude the calculation, we observe that this bound is
<2^{d}
whenever
d=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(D+\mathrm{Ndim}(\Pi))
, which we can again use in conjunction with
Lemma
A.7
to prove the desired uniform convergence statement found in Eq. (
5.27
). Ultimately this allows us to replace the
\log\lvert\Pi\rvert
with
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(D+\mathrm{Ndim}(\Pi))
in the upper bound of
Theorem
5.2
; the precise details are omitted.

5.6  Bibliographical Remarks

Agnostic RL in Low-Rank MDPs.

[
SDMMS21
]
explored agnostic PAC RL in low-rank MDPs, and showed that one can perform agnostic learning with respect to any policy class for MDPs that have a small rank. Their results are complementary to the ones established in this chapter. They study how to solve (
2.1
) without access to a realizable model/value function class. The main difference is that they investigate the low-rank assumption on the underlying MDP dynamics and show how it enables sample-efficient learning for any given policy class, while our results establish what assumptions on the given policy class are necessary and sufficient for agnostic learning
for any MDP
.

Reward-Free RL.

From a technical viewpoint, our algorithm (
Algorithm
3
) share similaries to algorithms developed in the reward-free RL literature
[
JKSY20
]
. In reward-free RL, the goal of the learner is to output a dataset or a set of policies, after interacting with the underlying MDP, that can be later used for planning (with no further interaction with the MDP) for downstream reward functions. The key ideas in our
Algorithm
3
, in particular, that the learner first finds states
\mathcal{I}
that are
\Omega(\varepsilon)
-reachable and corresponding policies that can reach them, and then outputs datasets
\{\mathcal{D}_{s}\}_{s\in\mathcal{I}}
that can be later used for evaluating any policy
\pi\in\Pi
, share similarities to algorithmic ideas used in reward-free RL. However, our algorithm strictly generalizes prior works in reward-free RL, and in particular can work with large state-action spaces where the notion of reachability as well as the offline RL objective, is defined w.r.t. the given policy class. In comparison, prior reward-free RL works compete with the best policy for the underlying MDP, and make structure assumptions on the dynamics, e.g. tabular structure
[
JKSY20
,
MDJKLV21
,
LYCF23
]
or linear dynamics
[
WDYS20
,
ZLKB20
,
ZZG21
,
WCSDJ22
]
, to make the problem tractable.

Chapter 6Imitation Learning

This chapter studies
imitation learning
(IL), a setting which provides the learner with stronger feedback than considered earlier in this thesis. We introduce an interactive imitation learning setup in
Section
6.1
, where the learner has access to an “expert oracle” that returns value functions of an expert on queried state-action pairs. Our main result, presented in
Section
6.2
, is that the
realizability
of the expert policy plays a crucial role in determining whether the learner can effectively utilize this expert oracle. We show that if the expert policy does not lie in the given policy class
\Pi
, then the additional access of the expert oracle cannot be leveraged by the learner to get improved statistical guarantees.

6.1  Preliminaries

Motivation.

For many policy classes, the spanning capacity may be quite large, and our lower bounds (
Theorem
4.2
,
4.3
, and
5.1
) demonstrate an unavoidable dependence on the spanning capacity
\mathfrak{C}(\Pi)
. Now we investigate whether it is possible to achieve bounds which are independent of
\mathfrak{C}(\Pi)
and instead only depend on
\mathrm{poly}(H,A,\log\lvert\Pi\rvert)
under a stronger feedback model.

The motivation for our feedback model comes from practice. It is uncommon to learn from scratch: often we would like to utilize domain expertise or prior knowledge to learn with fewer samples. For example, during training one might have access to a simulator which can roll out trajectories to estimate the optimal value function
Q^{\star}
, or one might have access to expert advice/demonstrations. However, this access does not come for free; estimating value functions with a simulator may require significant computation, or the expert might be a human who is providing labels or feedback on the performance of the algorithm. Thus, we consider additional feedback in the form of an expert oracle.

Definition 6.1

(Expert Oracle)
.

An expert oracle
