\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{exp}}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R}
is a function which given an
(x,a)
pair as input returns the
Q
value of some expert policy
\pi_{\circ}
, denoted
Q^{\pi_{\circ}}(x,a)
.

Definition
6.1
is a natural formulation for understanding how expert feedback can be used for RL in large state spaces. We do not require
\pi_{\circ}
to be the optimal policy (or even the best within the policy class
\Pi
). The objective is to compete with
\pi_{\circ}
, i.e., with probability at least
1-\delta
, return a policy
\widehat{\pi}
such that
V^{\widehat{\pi}}\geq V^{\pi_{\circ}}-\varepsilon
using few online interactions with the MDP and calls to
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{exp}}
. A discussion of how
Definition
6.1
relates to other imitation learning settings is deferred to the Bibliographical Remarks in
Section
6.3
.

Any sample efficient algorithm (one which uses at most
\mathrm{poly}(H,A,\log\lvert\Pi\rvert,\varepsilon^{-1},\delta^{-1})
online trajectories and calls to the oracle) must use
both
forms of access. The aforementioned lower bounds show that an algorithm which only uses online access to the MDP must use
\Omega(\mathfrak{C}(\Pi))
samples. Likewise, an algorithm which only queries the expert oracle must use
\Omega(\lvert\mathcal{X}\rvert A)
queries because it does not know the dynamics of the MDP, so the best it can do is just learn the optimal action on every state.

Upper Bound Under Realizability.

Under realizability (namely,
\pi_{\circ}\in\Pi
), it is known that the dependence on
\mathfrak{C}(\Pi)
can be entirely removed with few queries to the expert oracle.

Algorithm 7

AggreVaTe

[
RB14
]

1:

Oracle Access
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{exp}}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R}
, Policy Class
\Pi
, Online RL Access to
M^{\star}

2:

Let
\rho_{0}=\mathrm{Unif}(\Pi)
. Let
\eta\coloneqq\sqrt{8\log\lvert\Pi\rvert/T}
.

3:

for

t=1,2,\cdots,T
:

4:

Let
h_{t}\sim\mathrm{Unif}([H])
.

5:

Sample a state
x_{t}\sim d^{\rho_{t}}_{h_{t}}
.

6:

Query oracle to get
Q^{\star}_{t}(x_{t},a)\leftarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{exp}}(x_{t},a)
for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
.

7:

Update policy distribution
\rho_{t+1}(\pi)\propto\exp\left(\eta\cdot\sum_{t=1}^{T}Q^{\star}(x_{t},\pi(x_{t}))\right)

8:

Return

\widehat{\rho}=\mathrm{Unif}(\{\rho_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T})
.

Theorem 6.1
.

For any
\Pi
such that
\pi_{\circ}\in\Pi
, with probability at least
1-\delta
, the
\mathsf{AggreVaTe}
algorithm (
Algorithm
7
)
computes an
\varepsilon
-optimal policy using

\displaystyle n_{1}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{H^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot\log\frac{\lvert\Pi\rvert}{\delta}\right)\penalty\ \text{online trajectories}\quad\text{and}\quad n_{2}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{H^{2}A}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot\log\frac{\lvert\Pi\rvert}{\delta}\right)\penalty\ \text{calls to }\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{exp}}.

\mathsf{AggreVaTe}
is a reduction to online learning with experts.
We note that we actually require a slightly weaker, “local” oracle:
\mathsf{AggreVaTe}
only queries the value of
Q^{\pi_{\circ}}
on
(x,a)
pairs which are encountered in online trajectories. Furthermore, it is also possible adapt the algorithm to work with only expert action feedback, i.e., queries of the form
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{exp}}(x)=\pi_{\circ}(x)
, by changing the loss to the indicator function
\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi(x_{t})\neq\pi_{\circ}(x_{t})\right\}
.

For completeness, we prove
Theorem
6.1
; this result can also be found in
[
RB14
,
AJKS19
]
.

Proof of
Theorem
6.1
.

Let
\Pi=\{\pi_{1},\cdots,\pi_{N}\}
. For any
\pi\in\Pi
, let us define the loss function

\displaystyle\bm{\ell}^{t}_{\pi}\coloneqq-Q^{\star}(x_{t},\pi(x_{t})),\quad\text{ and }\quad\bm{\ell}^{t}\coloneqq\left(\bm{\ell}^{t}_{\pi_{1}},\bm{\ell}^{t}_{\pi_{2}},\cdots,\bm{\ell}^{t}_{\pi_{N}}\right).

Note that for any
\pi
we have
\bm{\ell}^{t}_{\pi}\in[-1,0]
. Then the policy distribution update takes the form
\rho_{t+1}(\pi)\propto\exp\left(-\eta\sum_{t=1}^{T}\bm{\ell}^{t}_{\pi}\right)
. We have written the
AggreVaTe
update rule in the form of the exponential weights algorithm. Now we apply the standard regret bound
[
CL06
]
:

\displaystyle\textsf{Regret}=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle\rho_{t},\bm{\ell}^{t}\rangle-\min_{i\in[N]}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle e_{i},\bm{\ell}^{t}\rangle\leq\frac{\eta T}{8}+\frac{\log\lvert\Pi\rvert}{\eta}=\sqrt{\frac{T\log\lvert\Pi\rvert}{2}},

(6.1)

where the last equality uses the definition of
\eta
.

Now we use (
6.1
) to get a guarantee on the final policy
\widehat{\rho}
. Let us define

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{\ell}}^{t}_{\pi}\coloneqq\En_{h_{t}\sim\mathrm{Unif}([H]),x_{t}\sim d^{\rho_{t}}_{h_{t}}}\left[-Q^{\star}(x_{t},\pi(x_{t}))\right],\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{\bm{\ell}}^{t}\coloneqq\left(\widetilde{\bm{\ell}}^{t}_{\pi_{1}},\widetilde{\bm{\ell}}^{t}_{\pi_{2}},\cdots,\widetilde{\bm{\ell}}^{t}_{\pi_{N}}\right).

This can be viewed as the “expected” loss we should have incurred in a given round
t\in[T]
. Formally let
\En_{t}[\cdot]\coloneqq\En[\cdot\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{H}_{t}]
denote the conditional expectation, which is conditioned on all history up and including the end of iteration
t
. We have
\En_{t-1}[\langle\rho_{t},\bm{\ell}^{t}\rangle]=\langle\rho_{t},\widetilde{\bm{\ell}}^{t}\rangle
since
\rho_{t}
only depends on the history up until time
t-1
.

By Azuma-Hoeffding (
Lemma
A.3
) and union bound we get:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle\rho_{t},\bm{\ell}^{t}\rangle-\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle\rho_{t},\widetilde{\bm{\ell}}^{t}\rangle\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{T}},

\displaystyle\text{for all}\penalty\ i\in[N],\quad\left\lvert\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle e_{i},\bm{\ell}^{t}\rangle-\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle e_{i},\widetilde{\bm{\ell}}^{t}\rangle\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{\frac{\log(\lvert\Pi\rvert/\delta)}{T}}.

We use this in conjunction with (
6.1
) to get that with probability at least
1-\delta
:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle\rho_{t},\widetilde{\bm{\ell}}^{t}\rangle-\min_{i\in[N]}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle e_{i},\widetilde{\bm{\ell}}^{t}\rangle\leq\sqrt{\frac{\log\lvert\Pi\rvert}{2T}}+4\sqrt{\frac{\log(\lvert\Pi\rvert/\delta)}{T}}.

(6.2)

By Performance Difference Lemma, we know that for any
\pi
,

\displaystyle V^{\star}-V^{\rho_{t}}=\sum_{h=1}^{H}\En_{x_{h},a_{h}\sim d^{\rho_{t}}_{h}}\left[-A^{\star}(x_{h},a_{h})\right]=H\cdot\langle\rho_{t},\widetilde{\bm{\ell}}^{t}\rangle+\sum_{h=1}^{H}\En_{x_{h},a_{h}\sim d^{\rho_{t}}_{h}}\left[V^{\star}(x_{h})\right].

So therefore we have:

\displaystyle V^{\star}-\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}V^{\rho_{t}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}H\cdot\langle\rho_{t},\widetilde{\bm{\ell}}^{t}\rangle+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\En_{x_{h},a_{h}\sim d^{\rho_{t}}_{h}}\left[Q^{\star}(x_{h},\pi^{\star}(x_{h}))\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}H\cdot\langle\rho_{t},\widetilde{\bm{\ell}}^{t}\rangle+\max_{i\in[N]}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\En_{x_{h},a_{h}\sim d^{\rho_{t}}_{h}}\left[Q^{\star}(x_{h},\pi_{i}(x_{h}))\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}H\cdot\langle\rho_{t},\widetilde{\bm{\ell}}^{t}\rangle-\min_{i\in[N]}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}H\cdot\langle e_{i},\widetilde{\bm{\ell}}^{t}\rangle\lesssim H\sqrt{\frac{\log(\lvert\Pi\rvert/\delta)}{T}}.

(6.3)

In the second equality we use realizability, and the inequality uses (
6.2
).

To conclude the proof of
Theorem
6.1
, we set the RHS of (
6.3
) to
\varepsilon
, and observe that in every round
t\in[T]
,
\mathsf{AggreVaTe}
collects one online episode and calls the oracle
A
times.
∎

6.2  Lower Bound in Agnostic Setting

Realizability of the expert policy used for
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{exp}}
is a strong assumption in practice. For example, one might choose to parameterize
\Pi
as a class of neural networks, but one would like to use human annotators to give expert feedback on the actions taken by the learner; here, it is unreasonable to assume that realizability of the expert policy holds.

We sketch a lower bound in
Theorem
6.2
that shows that without realizability (
\pi_{\circ}\notin\Pi
), we can do no better than
\Omega(\mathfrak{C}(\Pi))
queries to a generative model or queries to
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{exp}}
.

Theorem 6.2

(informal)
.

For any
H\in\mathbb{N}
,
C\in[2^{H}]
, there exists a policy class
\Pi
with
\mathfrak{C}(\Pi)=\lvert\Pi\rvert=C
, expert policy
\pi_{\circ}\notin\Pi
, and family of MDPs
\mathcal{M}
with state space
\mathcal{X}
of size
O(2^{H})
, binary action space, and horizon
H
such that any algorithm that returns a
1/4
-optimal policy must either use
\Omega(C)
queries to a generative model or
\Omega(C)
queries to the
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{exp}}
.

Before sketching the proof, several remarks are in order.

\bullet

By comparing with
Theorem
6.1
,
Theorem
6.2
demonstrates that realizability of the expert policy is crucial for circumventing the dependence on spanning capacity via the expert oracle.

\bullet

In the lower bound construction of
Theorem
6.2
,
\pi_{\circ}
is an optimal policy. Furthermore, the optimal policy is not unique.

\bullet

While
\pi_{\circ}\notin\Pi
, the lower bound still has the property that
V^{\pi_{\circ}}=V^{\star}=\max_{\pi\in\Pi}V^{\pi}
; that is, the best-in-class policy
\widetilde{\pi}\coloneqq\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi}V^{\pi}
attains the same value as the optimal policy. This is possible because there exist multiple states for which
\widetilde{\pi}(x)\neq\pi_{\circ}(x)
, however these states have
d^{\widetilde{\pi}}(x)=0
. Thus, we rule out guarantees of the form

\displaystyle V^{\pi_{\circ}}-V^{\widehat{\pi}}\leq\penalty\ \underbrace{\min_{\pi\in\Pi}\penalty\ \left\{V^{\pi_{\circ}}-V^{\pi}\right\}}_{=0\text{ in construction}}\penalty\ +\varepsilon,

(6.4)

where the misspecification of the policy class
\Pi
is stated in terms of the suboptimality difference. We mention several related works in
Section
6.3
which give imitation learning guarantees similar to (
6.4
), but with the misspecification instead measured in terms of some information-theoretic quantity.

Proof Sketch of
Theorem
6.2
.

We present the construction as well as intuition for the lower bound, leaving out a formal information-theoretic proof. A formal proof can be obtained using similar arguments as
Theorem
4.3
.

Construction of MDP Family.

We describe the family of MDPs
\mathcal{M}
. In every layer, the state space is
\mathcal{X}_{h}=\{x_{(j,h)}:j\in[2^{h}]\}
, except at
\mathcal{X}_{H}
where we have an additional terminating state,
\mathcal{X}_{H}=\{x_{(j,h)}:j\in[2^{H}]\}\cup\{x_{\bot}\}
. The action space is
\mathcal{A}=\{0,1\}
.

The MDP family
\mathcal{M}=\{M_{b,f^{\star}}\}_{b\in\mathcal{A}^{H-1},f^{\star}\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{X}_{H}}}
is parameterized by a bit sequence
b\in\mathcal{A}^{H-1}
as well as a labeling function
f^{\star}\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{X}_{H}}
. The size of
\mathcal{M}
is
2^{H-1}\cdot 2^{2^{H}}
. We now describe the transitions and rewards for any
M_{b,f^{\star}}
. In the following, let
x_{b}\in\mathcal{X}_{H-1}
be the state that is reached by playing the sequence of actions
(b[1],b[2],\cdots,b[H-2])
for the first
H-2
layers.

\bullet

Transitions.
For the first
H-2
layers, the transitions are the same for
M_{b,f^{\star}}\in\mathcal{M}
. At layer
H-1
, the transition depends on
b
.

–

For any
h\in\{1,2,\dots,H-2\}
, the transitions are deterministic and given by a tree process: namely

\displaystyle P(x^{\prime}\mid x_{(j,h)},a)=\begin{cases}\mathbbm{1}\left\{x^{\prime}=x_{(2j-1,h+1)}\right\}&\text{if}\penalty\ a=0,\\
\mathbbm{1}\left\{x^{\prime}=x_{(2j,h+1)}\right\}&\text{if}\penalty\ a=1.\end{cases}

–

At layer
H-1
, for the state
x_{b}
, the transition is
P(x^{\prime}\mid x_{b},a)=\mathbbm{1}\left\{x^{\prime}=x_{\bot}\right\}
for any
a\in\mathcal{A}
. For all other states, the transitions are uniform to
\mathcal{X}_{H}
, i.e., for any
x\in\mathcal{X}_{H-1}\backslash\{x_{b}\}
,
a\in\mathcal{A}
, the transition is
P(\cdot\mid x,a)=\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{X}_{H}\backslash\{x_{\bot}\})
.

\bullet

Rewards.
The rewards depend on the
b\in\mathcal{A}^{H-1}
and
f^{\star}\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{X}_{H}}
.

–

The reward at layer
H-1
is
R(x,a)=\mathbbm{1}\left\{x=x_{b},a=b[H-1]\right\}
.

–

The reward at layer
H
is

\displaystyle R(x_{\bot},a)=0

for any
a\in\mathcal{A}
,

\displaystyle R(x,a)=\mathbbm{1}\left\{a=f^{\star}(x)\right\}

for any
x\neq x_{\bot}
,
a\in\mathcal{A}
.

From the description of the transitions and rewards, we can compute the value of
Q^{\star}(\cdot,\cdot)
.

\bullet

Layers
1,\cdots,H-2
: For any
x\in\mathcal{X}_{1}\cup\mathcal{X}_{2}\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{X}_{H-2}
and
a\in\mathcal{A}
, the
Q
-value is
Q^{\star}(x,a)=1
.

\bullet

Layer
H-1
: At
x_{b}
, the
Q
-value is
Q^{\star}(x_{b},a)=\mathbbm{1}\left\{a=b[H-1]\right\}
. For other states
x\in\mathcal{X}_{H-1}\backslash\{x_{b}\}
, the
Q
-value is
Q^{\star}(x,a)=1
for any
a\in\mathcal{A}
.

\bullet

Layer
H
: At
x_{\bot}
, the
Q
-value is
Q^{\star}(x_{\bot},a)=0
for any
a\in\mathcal{A}
. For other states
x\in\mathcal{X}_{H}\backslash\{x_{\bot}\}
, the
Q
-value is
Q^{\star}(x,a)=\mathbbm{1}\left\{a=f^{\star}(x)\right\}
.

Lastly, the optimal value is
V^{\star}=1
.

Expert Oracle.

The oracle
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{exp}}
returns the value of
Q^{\star}(x,a)
.

Policy Class.

The policy class
\Pi
is parameterized by bit sequences of length
H-1
. Denote the function
\mathrm{bin}:\{0,1,\dots,2^{H-1}\}\mapsto\mathcal{A}^{H-1}
that returns the binary representation of the input. Specifically,

\displaystyle\Pi\coloneqq\left\{\pi_{b}:b\in\left\{\mathrm{bin}(i):i\in\left\{0,1,\dots,C-1\right\}\right\}\right\},

where each
\pi_{b}
is defined such that
\pi_{b}(x)\coloneqq b[h]
if
x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}
, and
\pi_{b}(x)\coloneqq 0
otherwise. By construction it is clear that
\mathfrak{C}(\Pi)=\lvert\Pi\rvert=C
.

Lower Bound Argument.

Consider any
M_{b,f^{\star}}
where
b\in\left\{\mathrm{bin}(i):i\in\left\{0,1,\dots,C-1\right\}\right\}
and
f^{\star}\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{X}_{H}}
. There are two ways for the learner to identify a
1/4
-optimal policy in
M_{b,f^{\star}}
:

•

Find the value of
b
, and return the policy
\pi_{b}
, which has
V^{\pi_{b}}=1
.

•

Estimate
\widehat{f}\approx f^{\star}
, and return the policy
\pi_{\widehat{f}}
which picks arbitrary actions for any
x\in\mathcal{X}_{1}\cup\mathcal{X}_{2}\cup\dots\cup\mathcal{X}_{H-1}
and picks
\pi_{\widehat{f}}(x)=\widehat{f}(x)
on
x\in\mathcal{X}_{H}
.

We claim that in any case, the learner must either use many samples from a generative model or many calls to
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{exp}}
. First, observe that since the transitions and rewards at layers
1,\cdots,H-2
are known and identical for all
M_{b,f^{\star}}\in\mathcal{M}
, querying the generative model on these states does not provide the learner with any information. Furthermore, in layers
1,\cdots,H-2
, every
(x,a)
pair has
Q^{\star}(x,a)=1
, so querying
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{exp}}
on these
(x,a)
pairs also does not provide any information to the learner. Thus, we consider learners which query the generative model or the expert oracle at states in layers
H-1
and
H
.

In order to identify
b
, the learner must identify which
(x,a)
pair at layer
H-1
achieves reward of 1. They can do this either by (1) querying the generative model at a particular
(x,a)
pair and observing if
r(x,a)=1
(or if the transition goes to
x_{\bot}
); or (2) querying
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{exp}}
at a particular
(x,a)
pair and observing if
Q^{\star}(x,a)=0
(which informs the learner that
x_{b}=x
and
b[H-1]=1-a
). In either case, the learner must expend
\Omega(C)
queries in total in order to identify
b
.

To learn
f^{\star}
, the learner must solve a supervised learning problem over
\mathcal{X}_{H}\backslash x_{\bot}
. They can learn the identity of
f^{\star}(x)
by querying either the generative model or the expert oracle on
\mathcal{X}_{H}
. Due to classical supervised learning lower bounds
[
SB14
]
, learning
f^{\star}
requires

\displaystyle\Omega\left(\mathrm{VC}(\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{X}_{H}})\right)=\Omega(2^{H})\quad\text{queries.}

This concludes the proof sketch of
Theorem
6.2
.
∎

6.3  Bibliographical Remarks

The literature on imitation learning is vast; we state several of the most relevant works.

IL in Practice.

Imitation learning has been extensively used in autonomous driving
[
Pom88
,
AN04
,
BDD+16
,
BKO18
]
, robotic control
[
FYZAL17
,
SCPLBB20
]
, and game playing
[
ILPILA18
,
VBC+19
]
. The problem of autoregressive next-token prediction for large language models (LLMs) can be viewed as an imitation learning task
[
FBM24
]
.

Offline IL.

Offline approaches to IL require a dataset of expert demonstrations, i.e., trajectories of the form
\tau=(x_{1},\pi_{\circ}(x_{1}),\cdots,x_{H},\pi_{\circ}(x_{H}))
. Offline IL is typically solved via the behavior cloning algorithm
[
RB10
]
, which returns the policy which minimizes the 0/1 disagreement with the expert on the dataset via reduction to supervised learning. Other works study behavior cloning with losses other than the indicator loss.
[
FBM24
]
study behavior cloning with the log loss and investigate the dependence on horizon for deterministic/stochastic policy classes under the realizability assumption
\pi_{\circ}\in\Pi
.
[
RBHKF25
]
propose a
\rho
-estimator for agnostic offline IL, and show a sample complexity bound where the misspecification is measured in terms of the Hellinger distance
D_{\mathsf{H}}(\cdot,\cdot)
. Specifically they achieve a guarantee of the form (cf. (
6.4
)):

\displaystyle V^{\pi_{\circ}}-V^{\widehat{\pi}}\lesssim\min_{\pi\in\Pi}D_{\mathsf{H}}\left(\mathbb{P}^{\pi},\mathbb{P}^{\pi_{\circ}}\right)+\sqrt{\frac{\log\lvert\Pi\rvert/\delta}{n}},

where
\mathbb{P}^{\pi}
denotes the law over trajectories given by running
\pi
in the MDP.

Interactive IL.

Interactive settings for IL have been studied in a long line of work, dating back to the seminal results
[
RB10
,
RGB11
,
RB14
]
; some additional works include
[
SVGBB17
,
CB18
,
CKA20
,
YBC21
,
LZ22
]
. Several works study
active
imitation learning algorithms which are similar to our setting (which measures the number of calls to the oracle
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{exp}}
).
[
AJMF22
]
study a related expert action oracle under the assumption of linear value functions. They show that in a generative model, with
\mathrm{poly}(d)
resets and queries to an expert action oracle, one can learn an
\varepsilon
-optimal policy, thus circumventing known hardness results for the linear value function setting. Up to a factor of
A
, one can simulate queries to the expert action oracle by querying
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{exp}}(x,a)
for each
a\in\mathcal{A}
. Therefore, our lower bound in
Theorem
6.2
extends to this weaker setting.
[
SSSW23a
]
propose an algorithm which selectively queries a stochastic realizable expert, and they prove that this algorithm achieves regret bound scaling with the eluder dimension of the function class.

Tabular RL with Predictions.

We mention a few papers which do not strictly speaking fall under the umbrella of IL, but are relevant for the results in this chapter.
[
GM22
,
GPZKL22
]
study tabular RL with
inexact
value predictions for either the optimal
Q^{\star}
or
V^{\star}
. The main difference with our results is that they assume access to the entire table of values, while we study the agnostic RL setting with a large state space and formalize access to predictions via an expert oracle that is always exactly correct (
Definition
6.1
).

Chapter 7Online RL with Exploratory Resets

In this chapter, we study the sample complexity of learning under the
\mu
-reset interaction protocol, meaning that the learner is given sampling access to an additional exploratory reset distribution
\mu=\{\mu_{h}\}_{h\in[H]}
and can execute online rollouts from these
\mu_{h}
in addition to online rollouts from the starting distribution
d_{1}
.

To set the stage, in
Section
7.1
, we review a classical algorithm for this setting called Policy Search By Dynamic Programming (
PSDP
), which was introduced by
[
BKSN03
]
. We state the classical analysis of
PSDP
, which solves PAC RL under the assumption that the reset satisfies bounded concentrability and the policy class satisfies a strong
policy completeness
assumption. The rest of this chapter is devoted to understanding if it is possible to relax this policy completeness assumption.

1.

In
Section
7.1
, we investigate the performance of
PSDP
when the policy class only satisfies
realizability
, and we show that under slightly stronger assumptions,
PSDP
achieves exponential in horizon sample complexity; moreover, we give an algorithm-dependent lower bound which shows this result is tight.

2.

In
Section
7.2
, we turn to the fully agnostic setting, and give an information-theoretic lower bound showing that sample-efficient agnostic policy learning is not possible.

We conclude with open problems in
Section
7.3
.

7.1  Policy Search By Dynamic Programming

In this section, we provide a description of the
PSDP
algorithm and analyze its sample complexity. We show the standard upper bound for
PSDP
which has appeared in prior works
[
MHKL20
]
in
Section
7.1.1
. We also prove several new results about
PSDP
when only policy realizability is satisfied: namely if the reset distribution
\mu
satisfies stronger properties beyond bounded concentrability, we show exponential in
H
upper bounds in
Section
7.1.2
as well as a matching lower bound in
Section
7.1.3
. We also discuss in
Section
7.1.3
how our lower bounds against
PSDP
also apply against the
CPI
algorithm.

7.1.1
PSDP
Guarantee Under Policy Completeness

Policy Search By Dynamic Programming (
PSDP
) is a widely studied policy learning algorithm
[
BKSN03
]
that relies on
\mu
-reset access.
PSDP
constructs partial policies
{\widehat{\pi}}_{h:H}\in\Pi_{h:H}
, starting from layer
H
, and returns the estimated policy
{\widehat{\pi}}_{1:H}
. We provide pseudocode for
PSDP
in
Algorithm
8
.

Algorithm 8

PSDP

[
BKSN03
]

1:

Reset distributions
\mu=\{\mu_{h}\}_{h\in[H]}
, policy class
\Pi
.

2:

for

h=H,\cdots,1

do

3:

Initialize dataset
\mathcal{D}_{h}=\varnothing
.

4:

for

n
times
do
:
//
Collecting
(x_{h},a_{h},v_{h})
requires
\mu
-reset access.

5:

Sample
(x_{h},a_{h})
where
x_{h}\sim\mu_{h}
and
a_{h}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{A})
.

6:

Let
v_{h}\coloneqq\sum_{h^{\prime}=h}^{H}r_{h^{\prime}}
be the value of executing
a_{h}\circ{\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H}
from
x_{h}
.

7:

Set
\mathcal{D}_{h}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}_{h}\cup\{(x_{h},a_{h},v_{h})\}
.

8:

Call CB oracle:
{\widehat{\pi}}_{h}\coloneqq\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{(x_{h},a_{h},v_{h})\in\mathcal{D}_{h}}\frac{\mathbbm{1}\left\{a_{h}=\pi(x_{h})\right\}}{A}\cdot v_{h}
.

9:

Return

{\widehat{\pi}}_{1:H}
.

The classic analysis of
PSDP
requires two key assumptions:

1.

An exploration condition of
concentrability
(
Definition
2.1
).

2.

A representation condition called
policy completeness
, to be described next.

Policy Completeness.

Completeness assumptions on the function approximator class are often assumed in the study of RL algorithms (see discussion in
Section
2.3.2
).
PSDP
requires a notion called
policy completeness
, which ensures that the policy class is closed under the policy improvement operator
[
DJKALS18
,
MHKL20
]
.

Definition 7.1

(Policy Completeness)
.

A policy class
\Pi
satisfies policy completeness if for every
\pi\in\Pi
and
h\in[H]
, there exists a policy
\widetilde{\pi}\in\Pi
such that:

\displaystyle\text{for all $x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}$}:\quad\widetilde{\pi}_{h}(x)=\argmax_{a\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{\pi}(x,a).

This is a worst-case variant of policy completeness. As we will see, the analysis of
PSDP
only requires a weaker
\ell_{1}
variant of policy completeness, and the resulting suboptimality incurs an additive dependence on the
\ell_{1}
policy completeness error. Policy realizability (which only asserts that such a
\widetilde{\pi}
exists for
\pi^{\star}_{h+1:H}
at every
h\in[H]
) is implied by policy completeness.

Sample Complexity Guarantee for
PSDP
.

As a prototypical classical result on policy learning, we now state the guarantee for
PSDP
.

Theorem 7.1
.

Suppose the policy class
\Pi
satisfies policy completeness (
Definition
7.1
), and the reset distribution
\mu
satisfies concentrability with parameter
C_{\mathsf{conc}}
. With probability
1-\delta
,
PSDP
finds an
\varepsilon
-optimal policy using
\mathrm{poly}(C_{\mathsf{conc}},A,H,\varepsilon^{-1},\log\lvert\Pi\rvert,\log\delta^{-1})
samples from the reset distribution.

Before we prove
Theorem
7.1
, we first set up some additional notation. We define an averaged notion of policy completeness; compared to
Definition
7.1
, this notion is weaker since it only requires completeness to hold in an averaged sense over the reset
\mu
.

For readability, we slightly abuse notation: for
Q
-functions we denote
Q^{\pi}_{h}(x,\pi)\coloneqq Q^{\pi}_{h}(x,\pi(x))
. Similarly, we sometimes denote rewards as
R(x,\pi)\coloneqq R(x,\pi(x))
and transitions as
P(\cdot\mid x,\pi)\coloneqq P(\cdot\mid x,\pi(x))
.

Definition 7.2

(Average Policy Completeness)
.

Fix any policy class
\Pi
, as well as exploratory distribution
\mu=\{\mu_{h}\}_{h\in[H]}
. For any layer
h\in[H]
and policy
{\widehat{\pi}}\coloneqq{\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H}\in\Pi_{h+1:H}
we define the (average)
policy completeness error
, denoted
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}:\Pi_{h+1:H}\to\mathbb{R}
, as

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}})\coloneqq\min_{\pi_{h}\in\Pi_{h}}\penalty\ \En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\penalty\ Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,a)-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi_{h})\right].

Definition
7.2
is similar to previously defined notions of policy completeness
[
SG14
,
ABS23
]
. As a point of comparison, Definition 2 of
[
ABS23
]
defines the average policy completeness to be the worst case over the convex hull of suffix policies
{\widehat{\pi}}
, i.e.
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}\coloneqq\sup_{{\widehat{\pi}}\in\mathsf{Conv}(\Pi)}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}})
, while we define it as a function which takes as input a rollout policy
{\widehat{\pi}}
.

Proof of
Theorem
7.1
.

First, we state a standard generalization bound on the contextual bandit oracle invoked in
line
8
. With probability at least
1-\delta
, for every
h\in[H]
the returned policy
{\widehat{\pi}}_{h}
satisfies

\displaystyle\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{h})\right]\geq\max_{\pi_{h}\in\Pi_{h}}\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi_{h})\right]-\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}},\quad\text{where}\quad\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}\coloneqq O\left(\sqrt{\frac{A\log(\lvert\Pi\rvert/\delta)}{n}}\right).

(7.1)

For every
h\in[H]
, let us define:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\pi}_{h}^{\star}(x)\coloneqq\argmax_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\penalty\ Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,a),\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{\pi}_{h}\coloneqq\argmax_{\pi_{h}\in\Pi_{h}}\penalty\ \En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi_{h})\right]

Then we calculate:

\displaystyle V^{\star}-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{=}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\En_{x\sim d^{\pi^{\star}}_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{h})\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{\leq}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\En_{x\sim d^{\pi^{\star}}_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\widetilde{\pi}_{h}^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{h})\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(iii)}{\leq}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\left\|\frac{d^{\pi^{\star}}_{h}}{\mu_{h}}\right\|_{\infty}\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\widetilde{\pi}_{h}^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{h})\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(iv)}{\leq}C_{\mathsf{conc}}\cdot\sum_{h=1}^{H}\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\widetilde{\pi}_{h}^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{h})\right]

\displaystyle=C_{\mathsf{conc}}\cdot\sum_{h=1}^{H}\left(\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\widetilde{\pi}_{h}^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\widetilde{\pi}_{h})\right]+\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\widetilde{\pi}_{h})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{h})\right]\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(v)}{\leq}HC_{\mathsf{conc}}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}+C_{\mathsf{conc}}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H}).

Here,
(i)
follows by the Performance Difference Lemma,
(ii)
is due to the optimality of
\widetilde{\pi}^{\star}_{h}
,
(iii)
is due to nonnegativity of
Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\widetilde{\pi}_{h}^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{h})
,
(iv)
is due to the definition of
C_{\mathsf{conc}}
, and
(v)
follows by
Definition
7.2
and Eq. (
7.1
). Therefore, if the policy completeness error is zero, then we have a bound which is at most
HC_{\mathsf{conc}}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}
, and therefore
PSDP
returns an
\varepsilon
-optimal policy using
\mathrm{poly}(C_{\mathsf{conc}},A,H,\log\lvert\Pi\rvert,\varepsilon^{-1},\log\delta^{-1})
samples.
∎

7.1.2  Upper Bounds for
PSDP
with Policy Realizability

The analysis of
PSDP
in
Theorem
7.1
crucially relied on the policy completeness assumption. Now we ask if policy completeness can be relaxed, i.e.,

Can we still get guarantees for
PSDP
with only realizability?

It is easy to see that policy realizability is
insufficient
for
PSDP
even for horizon
H=2
, as shown in
Figure
7.1
. Similar to lower bounds for offline RL
[
FKSX21
]
, the construction relies on
overcoverage
, as
\mu
has nonzero mass on a nonreachable state
\bar{s}_{1}
, which is somewhat unnatural. In the construction,
PSDP
may not even be consistent, since one can take
\gamma
to be arbitrarily close to 0 so that with constant probability
PSDP
returns a
(1+\gamma)
-suboptimal policy.

In this section, we circumvent the lower bound and show that if we make stronger assumptions on the reset distribution
\mu
,
PSDP
achieves consistency:

1.

If
\Pi
is realizable and the reset
\mu
has bounded pushforward concentrability (
Definition
2.2
),
Theorem
7.2
achieves
(C_{\mathsf{push}})^{O(H)}
sample complexity.

2.

If
\Pi
is realizable and the reset
\mu
is admissible (
Definition
7.3
) with bounded concentrability,
Theorem
7.3
achieves
(C_{\mathsf{conc}})^{O(H)}
sample complexity.

The two upper bounds are in general incomparable, as there exist settings in which one achieves a better guarantee than the other. In addition, to the best of our knowledge, neither result is implied by any known results for policy learning—note that the trivial bound of
A^{H}
achieved by importance sampling
[
KMN99
,
AJKS19
]
can be much larger when
C_{\mathsf{push}}\ll A
.

Figure 7.1:
Lower bound for
PSDP
without policy completeness.
Red
arrows represent action
0
and
blue
arrows represent action
1
. In
purple
we denote the expectation of the stochastic reward. Let
\gamma>0
be an arbitrarily small constant. At layer
h=2
, with constant probability,
PSDP
selects
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\widehat{\pi}}^{(2)}\leftarrow 0}
since
\En_{x\sim\mu_{2}}V^{\pi_{0}}(x)=1/2
and
\En_{x\sim\mu_{2}}V^{\pi_{1}}(x)=1/2+\gamma
. Conditioned on
{\widehat{\pi}}^{(2)}=0
, we have
\En_{x\sim\mu_{1}}V^{\pi_{0}\circ{\widehat{\pi}}^{(2)}}(x)=3/4
while
\En_{x\sim\mu_{1}}V^{\pi_{1}\circ{\widehat{\pi}}^{(2)}}(x)=1/4
, so therefore
PSDP
selects
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\widehat{\pi}}^{(1)}\leftarrow 0}
. The returned policy
{\widehat{\pi}}^{(1)}\circ{\widehat{\pi}}^{(2)}
is
(1+\gamma)
-suboptimal on
d_{1}
. Note that
\mu=\{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\}
satisfies
C_{\mathsf{conc}}=4
, and that
\Pi
satisfies realizability.

7.1.2.1  Policy Realizability + Pushforward Concentrability

Theorem 7.2
.

Suppose
\Pi
is realizable, and the reset
\mu
satisfies pushforward concentrability with parameter
C_{\mathsf{push}}
. With high probability,
PSDP
returns an
\varepsilon
-optimal policy using

\displaystyle\mathrm{poly}((C_{\mathsf{push}})^{H},A,\log\lvert\Pi\rvert,\varepsilon^{-1})\quad\text{samples.}

The proof relies on the following lemma, which relates the policy completeness error to the pushforward concentrability coefficient of
\mu
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Fix any layer
h\in[H]
. For any suffix policy
{\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H}
we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H})\leq C_{\mathsf{push}}\cdot\En_{x^{\prime}\sim\mu_{h+1}}\left[V^{\star}(x^{\prime})-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime})\right].

Proof of
Lemma
7.1
.

We have the following computation:

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H})

\displaystyle=\min_{\pi_{h}\in\Pi_{h}}\penalty\ \En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\penalty\ Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,a)-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi_{h})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\penalty\ Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,a)-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi^{\star})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{\star}(x,\pi^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi^{\star})\right]

\displaystyle=\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[r(x,\pi^{\star})+\En_{x^{\prime}\sim P(\cdot\mid x,\pi^{\star})}V^{\star}(x^{\prime})\right]-\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[r(x,\pi^{\star})+\En_{x^{\prime}\sim P(\cdot\mid x,\pi^{\star})}V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime})\right]

\displaystyle=\En_{x\sim\mu_{h},x^{\prime}\sim P(\cdot\mid x,\pi^{\star})}\left[V^{\star}(x^{\prime})-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime})\right].

(7.2)

The first inequality is due to the realizability
\pi^{\star}\in\Pi
, and the second one is due to the optimality of
\pi^{\star}
.
Now we will perform a change of measure to relate the bound in Eq. (
7.2
) to the error of
{\widehat{\pi}}
on the layer
h+1
.

\displaystyle\En_{x\sim\mu_{h},x^{\prime}\sim P(\cdot\mid x,\pi^{\star})}\left[V^{\star}(x^{\prime})-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime})\right]

\displaystyle=\En_{x^{\prime}\sim\mu_{h+1}}\left[\frac{\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}P(x^{\prime}\mid x,\pi^{\star})}{\mu_{h+1}(x^{\prime})}\cdot\left(V^{\star}(x^{\prime})-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathsf{push}}\cdot\En_{x^{\prime}\sim\mu_{h+1}}\left[V^{\star}(x^{\prime})-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime})\right],

where the inequality uses the nonnegativity of
V^{\star}(x^{\prime})-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime})
and the definition of pushforward concentrability. Plugging this back into Eq. (
7.2
) proves
Lemma
7.1
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem
7.2
.

Using Performance Difference Lemma we have for the learned policy
{\widehat{\pi}}\in\Pi
:

\displaystyle V^{\star}-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{h=1}^{H}\En_{x\sim d^{{\widehat{\pi}}}_{h}}\left[V^{\star}(x)-Q^{\star}(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{h})\right]=\sum_{h=1}^{H}\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[\frac{d^{{\widehat{\pi}}_{h}}(x)}{\mu_{h}(x)}\left(V^{\star}(x)-Q^{\star}(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{h})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathsf{conc}}\cdot\sum_{h=1}^{H}\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[V^{\star}(x)-Q^{\star}(x,{\widehat{\pi}})\right]\leq C_{\mathsf{conc}}\cdot\sum_{h=1}^{H}\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[V^{\star}(x)-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x)\right].

The first inequality uses the fact that
{\widehat{\pi}}\in\Pi
as well as
V^{\star}(x)\geq Q^{\star}(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{h})
, and the second inequality uses the latter fact again. From here, we apply an inductive argument to bound the suboptimality
\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}[V^{\star}(x)-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x)]
for all
h\in[H]
. Fix any
h\in[H]
. We have

\displaystyle\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[V^{\star}(x)-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x)\right]

\displaystyle=\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{\star}(x,\pi^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{\star}(x,\pi^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi^{\star})+\max_{a}Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,a)-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}})\right]

\displaystyle=\En_{x\sim\mu_{h},x^{\prime}\sim P(\cdot\mid x,\pi^{\star})}\left[V^{\star}(x^{\prime})-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime})\right]+\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[\max_{a}Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,a)-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}})\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathsf{push}}\En_{x^{\prime}\sim\mu_{h+1}}\left[V^{\star}(x^{\prime})-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime})\right]+\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}+\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H})

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{\mathrm{push}}\cdot\En_{x^{\prime}\sim\mu_{h+1}}\left[V^{\star}(x^{\prime})-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime})\right]+\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}},

(7.3)

where the last inequality uses
Lemma
7.1
. Recursive application of
(
7.3
)
and the fact that
\En_{x\sim\mu_{H}}[V^{\star}(x)-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x)]=\En_{x\sim\mu_{H}}[r(x,\pi^{\star})-r(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{H})]\leq\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}
gives us

\displaystyle\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[V^{\star}(x)-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x)\right]\leq H\cdot(2C_{\mathsf{push}})^{H}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}},

so therefore the final suboptimality of
PSDP
is at most

\displaystyle V^{\star}-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}\leq C_{\mathsf{conc}}\cdot\sum_{h=1}^{H}\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[V^{\star}(x)-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x)\right]\leq H^{2}\cdot(2C_{\mathsf{push}})^{H+1}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}.

Choosing
n=\mathrm{poly}((C_{\mathsf{push}})^{H},A,\log\lvert\Pi\rvert,\varepsilon^{-1})
so that the right hand side is at most
\varepsilon
proves the final bound.
∎

7.1.2.2  Policy Realizability + Admissibility + Concentrability

Definition 7.3
.

We say a distribution
\mu
is admissible if for every
h\in[H]
there exists some
\pi_{b}\in\Delta(\Pi)
:

\displaystyle\mu_{h}(x)=d^{\pi_{b}}_{h}(x)\quad\text{for all}\penalty\ x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}.

Theorem 7.3
.

Suppose
\Pi
is realizable, and the reset
\mu
(1) satisfies concentrability with parameter
C_{\mathsf{conc}}
, and (2) is admissible. With high probability,
PSDP
finds an
\varepsilon
-optimal policy using
\mathrm{poly}((C_{\mathsf{conc}})^{H},A,\log\lvert\Pi\rvert,\varepsilon^{-1})
samples.

To prove
Theorem
7.3
, we first establish a few helper lemmas on the errors of the learned policy
{\widehat{\pi}}
.

Lemma 7.2
.

For any layer
h\in[H]
and admissible distribution
\nu\in\Delta(\mathcal{X}_{h})
, we have

\displaystyle\max_{\pi\in\Pi_{h}}\penalty\ \En_{x\sim\nu}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi)-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}})\right]\leq C_{\mathsf{conc}}\left(\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}+\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H})\right).

Proof.

We calculate that

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\max_{\pi\in\Pi_{h}}\penalty\ \En_{x\sim\nu}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi)-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{h})\right]

\displaystyle=\max_{\pi\in\Pi_{h}}\En_{x\sim\nu}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi)-\max_{a}Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,a)\right]+\En_{x\sim\nu}\left[\max_{a}Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,a)-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{h})\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathsf{conc}}\cdot\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[\max_{a}Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,a)-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{h})\right]

\displaystyle=C_{\mathsf{conc}}\cdot\Bigg(\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[\max_{a}Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,a)\right]-\max_{\pi\in\Pi_{h}}\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi)\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 80.00012pt+\max_{\pi\in\Pi_{h}}\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi)\right]-\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{h})\right]\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathsf{conc}}\left(\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}+\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H})\right).

In the first inequality we use the fact that
\nu
is admissible, so we can use concentrability to relate the density ratios
\|\nu/\mu\|_{\infty}
.
∎

Additional Notation.
In the subsequent analysis, for any distribution
\nu
we denote
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}},\nu)
to be the policy completeness error under distribution
\nu
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}},\nu)\coloneqq\min_{\pi_{h}\in\Pi_{h}}\penalty\ \En_{x\sim\nu}\left[\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\penalty\ Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,a)-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi)\right].

For any partial policy
\pi_{h:t-1}
, we also denote
\nu\circ\pi_{h:t-1}\in\Delta(\mathcal{X}_{t})
to denote the distribution over states in layer
t
which is achieved by first sampling a state
x_{h}\sim\nu
then rolling out with partial policy
\pi_{h:t-1}
.

Lemma 7.3
.

For any layer
h\in[H]
and admissible distribution
\nu\in\Delta(\mathcal{X}_{h})
, we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H},\nu)\leq(H-h)\cdot C_{\mathsf{conc}}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}+C_{\mathsf{conc}}\cdot\sum_{h^{\prime}=h+1}^{H}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h^{\prime}+1:H})

Proof.

Using the definition of policy completeness we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H},\nu)

\displaystyle\leq\En_{x\sim\nu}\left[\max_{a}Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,a)-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi^{\star})\right]\leq\En_{x\sim\nu}\left[Q^{\star}(x,\pi^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi^{\star})\right].

Now, we apply a recursive argument, which gives us

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H},\nu)

\displaystyle\leq\En_{x\sim\nu}\left[Q^{\star}(x,\pi^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi^{\star})\right]

\displaystyle=\En_{x^{\prime}\sim\nu\circ\pi^{\star}}\left[Q^{\star}(x^{\prime},\pi^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime},{\widehat{\pi}})\right]

\displaystyle=\En_{x^{\prime}\sim\nu\circ\pi^{\star}}\left[Q^{\star}(x^{\prime},\pi^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime},\pi^{\star})+Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime},\pi^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime},{\widehat{\pi}})\right]

Because
\nu
is admissible, so is
\nu\circ\pi^{\star}
. Therefore, the second term in the sum is bounded using
Lemma
7.2
:

\displaystyle\En_{x^{\prime}\sim\nu\circ\pi^{\star}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime},\pi^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime},{\widehat{\pi}})\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathsf{conc}}\left(\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+2:H})+\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}\right).

The first term in the sum can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\En_{x^{\prime}\sim\nu\circ\pi^{\star}}\left[Q^{\star}(x^{\prime},\pi^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime},\pi^{\star})\right]=\En_{{x^{\prime\prime}}\sim\nu\circ\pi^{\star}\circ\pi^{\star}}\left[Q^{\star}(x^{\prime\prime},\pi^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime\prime},{\widehat{\pi}})\right].

Applying recursion, we get the final bound of

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H},\nu)

\displaystyle\leq(H-h)\cdot C_{\mathsf{conc}}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}+C_{\mathsf{conc}}\cdot\sum_{h^{\prime}=h+1}^{H}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h^{\prime}+1:H}).

This concludes the proof of
Lemma
7.3
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem
7.3
.

We compute the suboptimality as

\displaystyle V^{\star}-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{h=1}^{H}\En_{x\sim d^{\star}_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}})\right]

(Performance Difference Lemma)

\displaystyle\leq HC_{\mathsf{conc}}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}+C_{\mathsf{conc}}\left(\sum_{h=1}^{H}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H})\right).

(
Lemma
7.2
)

Now we apply
Lemma
7.3
to show that the policy completeness error can be bounded by the downstream policy completeness errors, using the admissibility of
\mu
.

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003ptV^{\star}-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}

\displaystyle\leq HC_{\mathsf{conc}}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}+C_{\mathsf{conc}}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H})

\displaystyle=HC_{\mathsf{conc}}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}+C_{\mathsf{conc}}\cdot\left(\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{2:H})+\sum_{h=2}^{H}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H})\right)

\displaystyle\leq HC_{\mathsf{conc}}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}+C_{\mathsf{conc}}\cdot\left((H-1)C_{\mathsf{conc}}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}+(1+C_{\mathsf{conc}})\cdot\sum_{h=2}^{H}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H})\right)

(
Lemma
7.3
)

\displaystyle\lesssim H(C_{\mathsf{conc}})^{2}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}+(1+C_{\mathsf{conc}})^{2}\cdot\left(\sum_{h=2}^{H}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H})\right)

\displaystyle=H(C_{\mathsf{conc}})^{2}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}+(1+C_{\mathsf{conc}})^{2}\cdot\left(\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{3:H})+\sum_{h=3}^{H}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H})\right)

\displaystyle\leq H(C_{\mathsf{conc}})^{2}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}

\displaystyle\hskip 30.00005pt+(1+C_{\mathsf{conc}})^{2}\cdot\left((H-2)C_{\mathsf{conc}}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}+(1+C_{\mathsf{conc}})\sum_{h=3}^{H}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H})\right)

(
Lemma
7.3
)

\displaystyle\lesssim H\left(1+C_{\mathsf{conc}}\right)^{3}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}+(1+C_{\mathsf{conc}})^{3}\left(\sum_{h=3}^{H}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H})\right).

Continuing this way (and observing that
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{H+1}=\varnothing)=0
) we get a final bound of

\displaystyle V^{\star}-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}\lesssim H(1+C_{\mathsf{conc}})^{H}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}.

Setting
n=\mathrm{poly}((C_{\mathsf{conc}})^{H},A,\log\lvert\Pi\rvert,\varepsilon^{-1})
makes the RHS at most
\varepsilon
, thus proving
Theorem
7.3
.
∎

7.1.3  Lower Bounds for
PSDP
and
CPI

Now we will show that exponential error compounding is unavoidable for
PSDP
in the absence of policy completeness.
PSDP
relies on a reduction to a contextual bandit oracle. For the lower bound statement, we will assume that
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}>0
is a fixed constant and
PSDP
is equipped with a
worst case
oracle
\mathsf{CB}_{\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}}
which for every layer
h\in[H]
always returns an
arbitrary policy

{\widehat{\pi}}_{h}
satisfying

\displaystyle\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{h})\right]\geq\max_{\pi\in\Pi}\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi)\right]-\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}.

Thus, the lower bound statement has the flavor of a statistical query lower bound
[
Kea98
]
, which also assumes a worst-case response up to accuracy
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}
.

Theorem 7.4
.

Let
H\geq 2
. Fix any
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}>0
and parameter
C_{\mathsf{push}}\geq 5H
. There exists a tabular MDP
M
with
S=O(H^{2})
states,
A=O(H)
actions, and horizon
H
, realizable policy class
\Pi
of size
2^{\widetilde{O}(H)}
, and exploratory distribution
\mu
which is admissible and satisfies pushforward concentrability with parameter
C_{\mathsf{push}}
, so that
PSDP
equipped with oracle
\mathsf{CB}_{\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}}
returns a policy
{\widehat{\pi}}
:

\displaystyle V^{\star}-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}\geq(C_{\mathsf{push}})^{\Omega(H)}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}.

Theorem
7.4
is a converse to the positive results of
Theorem
7.2
and
7.3
, showing that
PSDP
can have exponential in
H
sample complexity. The lower bound construction in
Theorem
7.4
as well as the earlier one from
Figure
7.1
are given by tabular MDPs. Thus, even simple tabular RL algorithms which use online access to the MDP can solve these constructions! This indicates an
algorithmic limitation
of using dynamic programming to solve policy learning.

Lastly, we also remark that the constructions in
Figure
7.1
and
Theorem
7.4
also apply to
CPI

[
KL02
]
; we refer the reader to
[
AJKS19
]
for an exposition of the
CPI
algorithm. At a high level, the
CPI
algorithm generates a sequence of policy iterates
\pi^{(1)},\pi^{(2)},\cdots
such that each policy iterate improves upon the previous one and terminates whenever:

\displaystyle\max_{\widetilde{\pi}\in\Pi}\penalty\ \En_{x\sim d^{\pi^{(t)}}_{\mu}}\left[Q^{\pi^{(t)}}(x,\widetilde{\pi})-Q^{\pi^{(t)}}(x,\pi^{(t)})\right]\leq\varepsilon.

where
d^{\pi^{(t)}}_{\mu}
is the occupancy measure obtained by running the current iterate
\pi^{(t)}
starting from the reset
\mu
and
\varepsilon>0
is some predefined threshold which represents the accuracy to which
CPI
solves the policy improvement problem. Thus, if it is not possible to greatly improve (by at least
\varepsilon
) the average
Q
-function by selecting a different policy
\widetilde{\pi}
, then
CPI
will terminate. In our constructions, one can check that if we initialize to the all-zeros policy
\pi^{(1)}\equiv 0
, then
CPI
will terminate immediately even though
\pi^{(1)}
has constant suboptimality.

In the rest of this section, we will prove
Theorem
7.4
.

7.1.3.1  Lower Bound Construction

Figure 7.2:
Lower bound construction for
Theorem
7.4
. To avoid clutter, we do not illustrate the highway states as well as any boring states which are only reachable by taking highway actions
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
at layer 0, since their role is only to make sure that the construction satisfies admissibility.

Our lower bound construction is illustrated in
Figure
7.2
.

For notational convenience, we number the layers starting with
h=0
, so that there are
H+1
layers.

State and Action Spaces.

At
h=0
there is a single state
x_{\circ}
and the action set is
\mathcal{A}_{0}=\{0,1,\mathfrak{a}_{1},\cdots,\mathfrak{a}_{H}\}
. For
h\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{h}=\underbrace{\left\{x_{h,0},x_{h,1}\cdots x_{h,H}\right\}}_{\text{$H+1$ boring states}}\cup\underbrace{\left\{x_{h,\diamond}\right\}\cup\left\{x_{h,\star}\right\}}_{\text{2 special states}}\cup\underbrace{\left\{\bar{x}_{h\to h+1},\bar{x}_{h\to h+2},\cdots\bar{x}_{h\to H}\right\}}_{\text{$H-h$ highway states}},

except for
\mathcal{X}_{H}
which does not have the special state
x_{h,\diamond}
. The action set is
\mathcal{A}_{h}=\{0,1\}
.

Policy Class.

The policy class
\Pi
is taken to be all open-loop policies over each layer’s action space:

\displaystyle\Pi\coloneqq\left\{\pi:\forall x\in\mathcal{X}_{h},\pi_{h}(x)\equiv a_{h},(a_{0},a_{1},\cdots,a_{H})\in\prod_{h=0}^{H}\mathcal{A}_{h}\right\}.

Reset Distribution.

At layer
h=0
we have
d_{0}=\mu_{0}=\delta_{x_{\circ}}
. At layer
h\geq 1
, the distribution
\mu_{h}
puts
1/C_{\mathsf{push}}
mass on each of the non-diamond states
\{x_{h,0},x_{h,1}\cdots x_{h,H}\}\cup\{x_{h,\star}\}\cup\{\bar{x}_{h\to h+1},\bar{x}_{h\to h+2},\cdots\bar{x}_{h\to H}\}
and the rest on
x_{h,\diamond}
. Therefore
x_{h,\diamond}
has mass
at least

p\coloneqq(1-\frac{2H+1}{C_{\mathsf{push}}})
. We have
p>1/2
as long as
C_{\mathsf{push}}\geq 5H
.

Transitions.

At
h=0
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr(\cdot\mid x_{\circ},a)=\begin{cases}\delta_{x_{1,\diamond}}&\text{if}\penalty\ a=0\\
\delta_{x_{1,\star}}&\text{if}\penalty\ a=1\\
\mu_{1}&\text{if}\penalty\ a=\mathfrak{a}_{1}\\
\delta_{\bar{x}_{1\to h^{\prime}}}&\text{if}\penalty\ a=\mathfrak{a}_{h^{\prime}},h^{\prime}\geq 2.\\
\end{cases}

For
h\geq 1
, we have:

•

Boring States:
At the boring state
x_{h,i}
, we always transit to the corresponding boring state in the next layer
x_{h+1,i}
regardless of the action.

•

Highway States:
On the highway state
\bar{x}_{h\to h+1}
, we transit to
\mu_{h+1}
regardless of the action. On highway states
\bar{x}_{h\to h^{\prime}}
for
h^{\prime}>h+1
we transit to
\bar{x}_{h+1,h^{\prime}}
regardless of the action.

•

Special States:
We have

\displaystyle P(\cdot\mid x_{h,\diamond},a)=\begin{cases}\delta_{x_{h+1,H-h}}&\text{if}\penalty\ a=0\\
\delta_{x_{h+1,\star}}&\text{if}\penalty\ a=1,\end{cases}\quad\text{and}\quad P(\cdot\mid x_{h,\star},a)=\begin{cases}\delta_{x_{h+1,0}}&\text{if}\penalty\ a=0\\
\delta_{x_{h+1,H-h+1}}&\text{if}\penalty\ a=1.\end{cases}

Rewards.

All the rewards are at layer
H
:

\displaystyle R(x_{H,\star},0)=0,\quad R(x_{H,\star},1)=C_{\mathsf{push}}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}},\quad\text{and}\quad\forall\penalty\ i\in\{0,1,\cdots,H\}:\penalty\ R(x_{H,i},\cdot)=\frac{C_{\mathsf{push}}^{i}p^{i-1}}{i}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}},

Properties of the Construction.

Now we list several properties of the construction which are more or less immediate to verify.

1.

The state space is of size
O(H^{2})
, the action space is of size
O(H)
, and the policy class is of size
(H+2)\cdot 2^{H}
.

2.

Due to the transitions for the highway states, the distribution
\mu
is admissible at all layers
h\geq 0
.

3.

The minimum probability that
\mu_{h}
places on any state
x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}
is at least
\min\{1/C_{\mathsf{push}},p\}\geq 1/C_{\mathsf{push}}
, so therefore pushforward concentrability is satisfied with parameter
C_{\mathsf{push}}
.

4.

The optimal policy is the all-ones policy,
\pi^{\star}_{h}\equiv 1
for all
h\geq 0
. Therefore
\Pi
is realizable.

7.1.3.2  Analysis of
PSDP

We will show inductively that
PSDP
returns the all-zeros policy
{\widehat{\pi}}_{h}\equiv 0
for all
h\in[H]
.

•

At layer
H
, the only state for which the value of taking
a_{H}=0
and
a_{H}=1
differ is on
x_{H,\star}
, which is sampled under
\mu_{H}
with probability
1/C_{\mathsf{push}}
. The gap between values is
\En_{x\sim\mu_{H}}\left[r(x,1)-r(x,0)\right]=\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}
, so we set
\mathsf{CB}_{\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}}
to return
{\widehat{\pi}}_{H}\equiv 0
.

•

At layer
H-1
, the two states for which there is a gap in value are the special states
x_{H-1,\diamond}
and
x_{H-1,\star}
. We can compute that

\displaystyle\En_{x\sim\mu_{H-1}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}_{H}}(x,0)-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}_{H}}(x,1)\right]\geq p\cdot C_{\mathsf{push}}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}-\frac{1}{C_{\mathsf{push}}}\cdot\frac{C_{\mathsf{push}}^{2}p\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}}{2}=\frac{C_{\mathsf{push}}p\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}}{2}>\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}.

Here we use the fact that
\mu_{H-1}(x_{H-1,\diamond})\geq p>1/2
, as well as the assumed lower bound on
C_{\mathsf{push}}
. Therefore,
\mathsf{CB}_{\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}}
must return
{\widehat{\pi}}_{H-1}\equiv 0
.

•

Suppose we are at layer
h
and for all
h^{\prime}>h

PSDP
selects
{\widehat{\pi}}_{h^{\prime}}\equiv 0
. Then the gap in value between action 0 and action 1 is

\displaystyle\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H}}(x,0)-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H}}(x,1)\right]

\displaystyle\geq p\cdot\frac{C_{\mathsf{push}}^{H-h}p^{H-h-1}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}}{H-h}-\frac{1}{C_{\mathsf{push}}}\cdot\frac{C_{\mathsf{push}}^{H-h+1}p^{H-h}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}}{H-h+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{\mathsf{push}}^{H-h}p^{H-h}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}}{(H-h)(H-h+1)}>\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}.

The last equality uses the fact that
C_{\mathsf{push}}p\geq 5H/2
.

•

Continuing this way, we can see that for all
h\geq 1
,
PSDP
equipped with
\mathsf{CB}_{\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}}
selects
{\widehat{\pi}}_{h}\equiv 0
. We can calculate that:

\displaystyle Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}_{1:H}}(x_{\circ},a)\begin{cases}=0&\text{if}\penalty\ a=1\\[10.00002pt]
=\frac{C_{\mathsf{push}}^{H-1}p^{H-2}}{H}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}&\text{if}\penalty\ a=0\\[10.00002pt]
\leq\left(\frac{C_{\mathsf{push}}^{H-1}p^{H-2}}{H}+\frac{C_{\mathsf{push}}^{H-1}p^{H-1}}{H-1}\right)\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}\leq\frac{2C_{\mathsf{push}}^{H-1}p^{H-1}}{H-1}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}.&\text{if}\penalty\ a=\mathfrak{a_{i}}\penalty\ \text{for any}\penalty\ i\in[H]\end{cases}

For the last case, we use the rough estimate that
\mu_{h}
places
1/C_{\mathsf{push}}
mass on
x_{h,H}
and the rest elsewhere.

Plugging in the optimal value
V^{\star}
we have that the suboptimality of
PSDP
is at least

\displaystyle V^{\star}-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}\geq\left(\frac{C_{\mathsf{push}}^{H}p^{H-1}}{H}-\frac{2C_{\mathsf{push}}^{H-1}p^{H-1}}{H-1}\right)\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}=(C_{\mathsf{push}})^{\Omega(H)}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}.

This concludes the proof of
Theorem
7.4
. ∎

7.2  Agnostic Policy Learning is Impossible for
\mu
-Resets

In this section, we ask if it is possible to remove representational assumptions such as completeness or realizability altogether. Our main result is
Theorem
7.5
, which shows that one cannot achieve sample-efficient agnostic policy learning under
\mu
-reset access.

Theorem 7.5
.

For any
H\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a policy class
\Pi
of size
2^{H}
, a family of MDPs
\mathcal{M}
over a state space of size
2^{O(H)}
, binary action space, horizon
H
, and a reset distribution
\mu
satisfying
C_{\mathsf{conc}}(\mu;\Pi,M)=6
for all
M\in\mathcal{M}
, so that any proper deterministic algorithm that returns a
1/16
-optimal policy must use at least
2^{\Omega(H)}
samples from
\mu
-reset access for some MDP in
\mathcal{M}
.

The proof is given in
Section
7.2.1
.

Figure 7.3:
Construction used for
Theorem
7.5
.

Key Ideas for
Theorem
7.5
.

An example can be found in
Figure
7.3
. Similar to the construction for
Theorem
4.3
, we use the rich observation combination lock. There is a slight twist, however. We introduce a set of
distractor
latent states
\{s_{h}^{\mathsf{d}}\}_{h\geq 2}
, which are not reachable from the initial distribution
d_{1}
, and we set
\mu_{h}
to be the uniform distribution over all observations in layer
h
. Thus, the exploratory distribution
\mu
has
overcoverage
over these unreachable states. The distractor states have the same latent transitions as the good states, and the only difference is that the reward at
s_{H}^{\mathsf{d}}
is flipped compared to the reward at
s_{H}^{\mathsf{g}}
. This causes rollouts from
\mu_{h}
to be noninformative. As for some rough intuition, observe that the distribution of rewards for executing
any
open-loop policy
\pi_{h:H}
from
\mu_{h}
with
h\geq 2
is
\mathrm{Ber}(1/2)
. This is shown by the following casework:

•

If
\pi_{h:H}=\pi^{\star}_{h:H}
, then we get a reward of 1 by either sampling
x\sim\psi(s_{h}^{\mathsf{g}})
with probability
1/4
and getting reward 1 at
s_{H}^{\mathsf{g}}
or sampling
x\sim\psi(s_{h}^{\mathsf{b}})
with probability
1/2
and getting reward
\mathrm{Ber}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)
at
s_{h}^{\mathsf{b}}
. Thus the distribution is
\mathrm{Ber}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)
.

•

Similar reasoning holds if
\pi_{h:H}=\pi^{\star}_{h:H-1}\circ(1-\pi^{\star}_{H})
, but with the reward of 1 coming from sampling the states
x\sim\psi(s_{h}^{\mathsf{d}})
.

•

If
\pi_{h:H}
is any other policy, then it always reaches
s_{H}^{\mathsf{b}}
and it gets reward
\mathrm{Ber}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)
.

Therefore, observing the reward distribution obtained by executing open-loop policies reveals
no information
about
\pi^{\star}
; due to the rich observations, executing non-open loop policies does not really help, and the learner cannot really learn any information about the transition dynamics from the reset
\mu
. Again, the best the learner can do is online RL which requires
2^{\Omega(H)}
samples.

Remark.
If the learner had local simulator access, then it could easily decode states starting from layer
H
, going backwards, since the reward distributions for a particular
(x_{H},a_{H})
pair are different depending on the latent state
\phi(x_{H})
. This idea is precisely the intuition that motivates our algorithm for the hybrid resets interaction protocol in
Chapter
8
.

7.2.1  Proof of
Theorem
7.5

Lower Bound Construction.

We define a family of Block MDPs
\mathcal{M}=\{M_{\pi^{\star},\phi}\}_{\pi^{\star}\in\Pi,\phi\in\Phi}
which are parameterized by an optimal policy
\pi^{\star}\in\Pi
and a decoding function
\phi\in\Phi
(to be described).

•

Policy Class:
Again, the policy class
\Pi
is taken to be open loop policies:

\displaystyle\Pi\coloneqq\{\pi:\forall x\in\mathcal{X}_{h},\pi_{h}(x)\equiv a_{h},(a_{1},\cdots,a_{H})\in\mathcal{A}^{H}\}.

•

Latent MDP:
The latent state space
\mathcal{S}
is layered where each
\mathcal{S}_{h}\coloneqq\{s_{h}^{\mathsf{g}},s_{h}^{\mathsf{b}},s_{h}^{\mathsf{d}}\}
is comprised of a good, a bad, and a distractor state. We abbreviate the state as
\{\mathsf{g},\mathsf{b},\mathsf{d}\}
if the layer
h
is clear from context. The starting state is always
\mathsf{g}
. The action space
\mathcal{A}=\{0,1\}
. Let
\pi^{\star}\in\Pi
be any policy, which can be represented by a vector in
(\pi^{\star}_{1},\cdots,\pi^{\star}_{H})\in\{0,1\}^{H}
. The latent transitions/rewards of an MDP parameterized by
\pi^{\star}\in\Pi
are as follows for every
h\in[H]
:

\displaystyle P_{\mathsf{lat}}(\cdot\mid s,a)=\begin{cases}\delta_{s_{h+1}^{\mathsf{g}}}&\text{if}\penalty\ s=s_{h}^{\mathsf{g}},a=\pi^{\star}_{h}\\
\delta_{s_{h+1}^{\mathsf{d}}}&\text{if}\penalty\ s=s_{h}^{\mathsf{d}},a=\pi^{\star}_{h}\\
\delta_{s_{h+1}^{\mathsf{b}}}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ R_{\mathsf{lat}}(s,a)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if}\penalty\ s=s_{H}^{\mathsf{g}},a=\pi^{\star}_{H}\\
1&\text{if}\penalty\ s=s_{H}^{\mathsf{d}},a\neq\pi^{\star}_{H}\\
\mathrm{Ber}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)&\text{if}\penalty\ s=s_{H}^{\mathsf{b}}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

•

Rich Observations:
The observation state space
\mathcal{X}
is layered where each
\mathcal{X}_{h}\coloneqq\{x_{h}^{(1)},\cdots,x_{h}^{(m)}\}
with
m=2^{H+2}
. The decoding function class
\Phi
is the collection of all decoders which for every
h\geq 2
assigns
s_{h}^{\mathsf{g}},s_{h}^{\mathsf{d}}
to disjoint subsets of
\mathcal{X}_{h}
of size
2^{H}
and
s_{h}^{\mathsf{b}}
to the rest:

\displaystyle\Phi\coloneqq\Big\{\phi:\penalty\ \mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathcal{S}:\penalty\

\displaystyle\forall\penalty\ x_{1}\in\mathcal{X}_{1},\penalty\ \phi(x_{1})=\mathsf{g},

\displaystyle\hskip-50.00008pt\forall\penalty\ h\geq 2,\penalty\ \left\lvert\left\{x_{h}\in\mathcal{X}_{h}:\phi(x_{h})=\mathsf{g}\right\}\right\rvert=2^{H}\text{ and }\left\lvert\left\{x_{h}\in\mathcal{X}_{h}:\phi(x_{h})=\mathsf{d}\right\}\right\rvert=2^{H}\Big\},

\displaystyle\hskip-80.00012pt\text{so that}\penalty\ \lvert\Phi\rvert=\left(\binom{2^{H+2}}{2^{H}}\cdot\binom{2^{H+2}-2^{H}}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1}=2^{2^{\widetilde{O}(H)}}.

In the MDP parameterized by
\phi\in\Phi
, the emission for every
s\in\mathcal{S}
is
\psi(s)=\mathrm{Unif}\left(\left\{x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}:\phi(x)=s\right\}\right)
.

•

Exploratory Distribution:
The exploratory distribution
\mu=\{\mu_{h}\}_{h\in[H]}
is set to be
\mu_{h}=\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{X}_{h})
.

We establish several facts about any
M_{\pi^{\star},\phi}\in\mathcal{M}
defined by the construction.

•

The distribution
\mu
has bounded concentrability:
C_{\mathsf{conc}}(\mu;\Pi,M)\leq 4
.

•

The policy class
\Pi
does not satisfy realizability, since the optimal policy at layer
H
requires one to take different actions depending on whether the latent state is
\mathsf{g}
or
\mathsf{d}
.

Notation for Algorithm.

Similar to the proof of
Theorem
4.3
, we use
\mathsf{Alg}
to denote a deterministic algorithm that collects
T
samples, i.e., full-length episodes under
\mu
-reset access. For any
t\in[T]
we define
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
to be the sigma-field of everything observed in the first
t-1
episodes. We further define for any
h\in[H]
the filtration
\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}
to be the sigma-field of everything observed in the first
t-1
episodes as well as the first
h-1
steps of the
t
-th sample. For the
\mu
-reset model,
\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}=\sigma(\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\{(X_{t,i},A_{t,i},R_{t,i})\}_{h_{\bot}\leq i\leq h-1})
. Here,
h_{\bot}
is the starting layer of episode
t
, which is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
; furthermore, the action
A_{t,h}
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\cup\{X_{t,h}\}
(since
\mathsf{Alg}
is deterministic).

Sample Complexity Lower Bound.

We will use
Theorem
A.1
to prove our lower bound. First we need to instantiate the parameter space. We will let
\Theta\coloneqq\{(\pi^{\star},\phi):\penalty\ \pi^{\star}\in\Pi,\phi\in\Phi\}
so that
\mathcal{M}=\{M_{\theta}\}_{\theta\in\Theta}=\{M_{\pi^{\star},\phi}\}_{\pi^{\star}\in\Pi,\phi\in\Phi}
. We further denote the subsets

\displaystyle\Theta_{0}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(\pi^{\star},\phi):\penalty\ \pi^{\star}\in\Pi\penalty\ \text{s.t.}\penalty\ \pi^{\star}_{H}=0,\phi\in\Phi\}

\displaystyle\Theta_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(\pi^{\star},\phi):\penalty\ \pi^{\star}\in\Pi\penalty\ \text{s.t.}\penalty\ \pi^{\star}_{H}=1,\phi\in\Phi\}

The observation space
\mathcal{Y}
is defined as the set of observations over
T
rounds as well as returned policy for an algorithm interacting with the MDP, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}\coloneqq(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{A}\times[0,1])^{HT}\times\Pi.

(As a convention, we can assume that each sample collected by
\mathsf{Alg}
in the MDP is of length
H
; if
\mathsf{Alg}
decides to rollout from
\mu_{h}
at an intermediate layer
h\geq 2
then we can simply append “dummy states” to the prefix of the trajectory, which does not change the analysis.)

For an observation
y\in\mathcal{Y}
we define the final returned policy as
y^{\pi}
. The loss function is given by

\displaystyle L((\pi^{\star},\phi),y)\coloneqq\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi^{\star}\neq y^{\pi}\right\}.

Then we have for any
y\in\mathcal{Y}
,
(\pi^{\star}_{0},\phi_{0})\in\Theta_{0}
, and
(\pi^{\star}_{1},\phi_{1})\in\Theta_{1}
that

\displaystyle L((\pi^{\star}_{0},\phi_{0}),y)+L((\pi^{\star}_{1},\phi_{1}),y)\geq 1\coloneqq 2\Delta,

since the last bit of
y^{\pi}
can be either 0 or 1, thus only matching exactly one of
\pi^{\star}_{0}
and
\pi^{\star}_{1}
.

Now we are ready to apply
Theorem
A.1
. We get that for any
\mathsf{Alg}
, we must have

\displaystyle\sup_{(\pi^{\star},\phi)\in\Pi\times\Phi}\En_{Y\sim\Pr^{M_{\pi^{\star},\phi},\mathsf{Alg}}}\left[V^{\star}-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sup_{(\pi^{\star},\phi)\in\Pi\times\Phi}\En_{Y\sim\Pr^{M_{\pi^{\star},\phi},\mathsf{Alg}}}\left[\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi^{\star}=Y^{\pi}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sup_{(\pi^{\star},\phi)\in\Pi\times\Phi}\En_{Y\sim\Pr^{M_{\pi^{\star},\phi},\mathsf{Alg}}}\left[L((\pi^{\star},\phi),Y)\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{8}\cdot\max_{\nu_{0}\in\Delta(\Theta_{0}),\nu_{1}\in\Delta(\Theta_{1})}\left(1-D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}},\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{8}\cdot\left(1-D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{0}),\mathsf{Alg}},\Pr^{\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{1}),\mathsf{Alg}}\right)\right).

It remains to compute an upper bound
D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{0}),\mathsf{Alg}},\Pr^{\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{1}),\mathsf{Alg}}\right)
which holds for any
\mathsf{Alg}
. This is accomplished by the following lemma.

Lemma 7.4
.

For any deterministic
\mathsf{Alg}
that adaptively collects
T=2^{O(H)}
samples via
\mu
-reset access, we have

\displaystyle D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{0}),\mathsf{Alg}},\Pr^{\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{1}),\mathsf{Alg}}\right)\leq\frac{T^{4}H}{2^{H-10}}.

Plugging in
Lemma
7.4
, we conclude that for any
\mathsf{Alg}
that collects
2^{cH}
samples for sufficiently small constant
c>0
must be
1/16
-suboptimal in expectation. This concludes the proof of
Theorem
7.5
.∎

7.2.2  Proof of
Lemma
7.4
(TV Distance Calculation for
Theorem
7.5
)

Since the proof is similar to that of
Lemma
4.1
we omit some intermediate calculations. In the rest of the proof we denote
\nu_{0}\coloneqq\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{0})
and
\nu_{1}\coloneqq\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{1})
. We have

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003ptD_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}},\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{h=1}^{H-1}\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h+1}\mid X_{t,h},A_{t,h},\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right],\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h+1}\mid X_{t,h},A_{t,h},\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right]\right)\right]}_{\text{transition TV distance}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\underbrace{\sum_{t=1}^{T}\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}\mid X_{t,H},A_{t,H},\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right],\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}\mid X_{t,H},A_{t,H},\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\right)\right]}_{\text{reward TV distance}}.

We bound each term separately.

Transition TV Distance.

Using triangle inequality and
Lemma
7.5
we get that for any
t\in[T],h\in[H-1]
:

\displaystyle\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h+1}\mid X_{t,h},A_{t,h},\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right],\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h+1}\mid X_{t,h},A_{t,h},\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right]\right)\right]\leq\frac{t}{2^{H-3}}.

(7.4)

Reward TV Distance.

Using triangle inequality, the fact that rewards are in
\{0,1\}
, and
Lemma
7.6
we get

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}\mid X_{t,H},A_{t,H},\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right],\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}\mid X_{t,H},A_{t,H},\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid X_{t,H},A_{t,H},\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid X_{t,H},A_{t,H},\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert\right]

\displaystyle\leq t\cdot\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-9}}.

(7.5)

Final Bound.

Thus, combining Eqs. (
7.4
) and (
7.5
) we can conclude that:

\displaystyle D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}},\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\right)\leq\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-3}}+\frac{T^{4}H}{2^{H-9}}\leq\frac{T^{4}H}{2^{H-10}}.

This concludes the proof of
Lemma
7.4
.∎

Lemma 7.5

(Transition TV Distance for the Construction in
Theorem
7.5
)
.

For any
t\in[T],h\in[H]
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right]-\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{X}_{h})\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{t}{2^{H-2}},

\displaystyle\left\|\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right]-\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{X}_{h})\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{t}{2^{H-2}}.

Proof of
Lemma
7.5
.

We prove the bound for
\nu_{0}
, as the proof for
\nu_{1}
is identical. If we sample
X_{t,h}
directly from the
\mu
-reset distribution, then the result immediately follows since the distribution of
X_{t,h}=\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{X}_{h})
. Otherwise, denote

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}^{\prime}=\sigma(\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1},\{\phi(X):X\in\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\},\{\mathbbm{1}\left\{A=\pi^{\star}(X)\right\}:(X,A)\in\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\})

to be the annotated sigma-field which also includes the latent state labels for all of the previous observions as well as whether the action taken followed
\pi^{\star}
or not. Let us denote
\ell=\phi(X_{t,h})\in\{\mathsf{g},\mathsf{b},\mathsf{d}\}
to be the latent state label of the next observation. Observe that the label
\ell
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}^{\prime}
since the filtration
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}^{\prime}
includes
\phi(X_{t,h-1})
as well as
\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{t,h-1}=\pi^{\star}(X_{t,h-1})\right\}
. Furthermore denote
\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}
to denote the total number of observations that we have encountered already in layer
h
and
\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}
to denote the observations we have encountered whose label is
\ell
.

Under the uniform distribution over decoders, the assignment of the remaining observations is equally likely. Therefore we can write the distribution of
X_{t,h}
as:

\displaystyle\text{if}\penalty\ \ell=\mathsf{g}:

\displaystyle\quad\Pr^{\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{0}),\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}=x\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}^{\prime}\right]=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2^{H}}&\text{if}\penalty\ x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\\
0&\text{if}\penalty\ x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}-\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\\
\frac{1}{2^{H}}\cdot\frac{2^{H}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\rvert}{2^{H+2}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\rvert}&\text{if}\penalty\ x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}-\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\text{if}\penalty\ \ell=\mathsf{b}:

\displaystyle\quad\Pr^{\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{0}),\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}=x\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}^{\prime}\right]=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2^{H+1}}&\text{if}\penalty\ x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\\
0&\text{if}\penalty\ x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}-\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\\
\frac{1}{2^{H+1}}\cdot\frac{2^{H+1}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\rvert}{2^{H+2}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\rvert}&\text{if}\penalty\ x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}-\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\text{if}\penalty\ \ell=\mathsf{d}:

\displaystyle\quad\Pr^{\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{0}),\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}=x\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}^{\prime}\right]=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2^{H}}&\text{if}\penalty\ x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\\
0&\text{if}\penalty\ x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}-\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\\
\frac{1}{2^{H}}\cdot\frac{2^{H}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\rvert}{2^{H+2}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\rvert}&\text{if}\penalty\ x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}-\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\end{cases}

We elaborate on the calculation for the last probability in each case. Suppose
\ell=\mathsf{g}
. Then for any
x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}-\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}
which has not been observed yet we assign
\phi(x)=\ell
in

\displaystyle\binom{2^{H+2}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\rvert-1}{2^{H}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\rvert-1}\text{ ways out of }\binom{2^{H+2}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\rvert}{2^{H}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\rvert}\text{ assignments.}

\displaystyle\quad\Longrightarrow\phi(x)=\mathsf{g}\text{ with probability }\frac{2^{H}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\rvert}{2^{H+2}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\rvert}.

For each assignment where
\phi(x)=\mathsf{g}
we will select it with probability
1/2^{H}
since the emission is uniform, giving us the final probability as claimed. A similar calculation can be done for the cases where
\ell=\mathsf{b},\mathsf{d}
.

Therefore we can calculate the final bound that

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\left\|\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}^{\prime}\right]-\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{X}_{h})\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}}\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}=x\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right]-\frac{1}{2^{H+2}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\begin{cases}\frac{\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\mathsf{g}}\rvert}{2^{H}}+\frac{\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\mathsf{b}}\rvert+\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\mathsf{d}}\rvert}{2^{H+2}}+\left\lvert\frac{2^{H}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\mathsf{g}}\rvert}{2^{H}}-\frac{2^{H+2}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\rvert}{2^{H+2}}\right\rvert&\text{if}\penalty\ \ell=\mathsf{g},\\[5.0pt]
\frac{\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\mathsf{b}}\rvert}{2^{H+1}}+\frac{\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\mathsf{g}}\rvert+\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\mathsf{d}}\rvert}{2^{H+2}}+\left\lvert\frac{2^{H+1}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\mathsf{b}}\rvert}{2^{H+1}}-\frac{2^{H+2}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\rvert}{2^{H+2}}\right\rvert&\text{if}\penalty\ \ell=\mathsf{b},\\[5.0pt]
\frac{\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\mathsf{d}}\rvert}{2^{H}}+\frac{\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\mathsf{b}}\rvert+\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\mathsf{g}}\rvert}{2^{H+2}}+\left\lvert\frac{2^{H}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\mathsf{g}}\rvert}{2^{H}}-\frac{2^{H+2}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\rvert}{2^{H+2}}\right\rvert&\text{if}\penalty\ \ell=\mathsf{d}.\end{cases}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4\cdot\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\rvert}{2^{H}}\leq\frac{4t}{2^{H}}.

Since
\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right]=\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}[X_{t,h}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}^{\prime}]
, we have by convexity of
\ell_{1}
norm and Jensen’s inequality,

\displaystyle\left\|\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right]-\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{X}_{h})\right\|_{1}\leq\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\left\|\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}^{\prime}\right]-\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{X}_{h})\right\|_{1}\right]\leq\frac{4t}{2^{H}},

which concludes the proof of
Lemma
7.5
.
∎

Lemma 7.6

(Reward TV Distance for the Construction in
Theorem
7.5
)
.

For any
t\in[T]
we have

\displaystyle\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid X_{t,H},A_{t,H},\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert\right]

\displaystyle\leq t\cdot\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-8}},

\displaystyle\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid X_{t,H},A_{t,H},\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert\right]

\displaystyle\leq t\cdot\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-8}}.

Proof of
Lemma
7.6
.

Let us denote
\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\coloneqq\sigma(X_{t,H},A_{t,H},\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1})
.

We will prove the first inequality of
Lemma
7.6
; the second inequality is obtained using similar arguments.

Peeling Off Bad Event.

First, let us peel off the event that
\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}
has repeated observations: denoting
\mathcal{E}_{N}\coloneqq\{X_{t,h}\notin\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\penalty\ \forall t\in[T],h\in[H]\}
, we have

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert\right]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}[\mathcal{E}_{N}^{c}]+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H}}+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert\right],

(7.6)

where the last inequality follows by an identical argument as
Lemma
4.5
. Therefore, it suffices to bound the expectation only for the
\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}
which have no repeated states.

Inductive Claim.

Now we define the event
\mathcal{E}_{R,t}
to be the event that among the first
t
episodes,
\mathsf{Alg}
never performs an online rollout (meaning it starts from layer 1) which follows
\pi^{\star}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{R,t}\coloneqq\left\{\forall t^{\prime}\leq t:A_{t,1:H-1}\neq\pi^{\star}\right\}.

A subtle point is that unlike the reward TV distance calculation for
Theorem
4.3
, the event
\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}
is
not measurable
with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}
(since there is still uncertainty as to what
\pi^{\star}
is). This causes some technical complications in the proof. To remedy this, we can consider working with an augmented filtration which appends a special token
\top
at the end of every online trajectory that
\mathsf{Alg}
takes if the sequence of actions
A_{t,1:H-1}
matches
\pi^{\star}
;
now

\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}
is measurable with respect to the augmented filtration (namely the event
\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}
holds if the augmented contains no special tokens
\top
). This augmentation does not affect the overall argument, and for the rest of the proof we assume that
\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}
has been augmented in this way.

Central to our proof is the following claim that
\mathcal{E}_{R,t}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}
happens with high probability:

\displaystyle\text{for all}\penalty\ t\in[T]:\quad\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{R,t}^{c}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right]\leq t\cdot\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-7}}.

(7.7)

Now we will establish
Claim
7.7
using an inductive argument. The base case of
t=0
trivially holds. Now suppose that
Claim
7.7
holds at time
t-1
. Then

\displaystyle\hskip-10.00002pt\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{R,t}^{c}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}^{c}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right]+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[A_{t,1:H-1}=\pi^{\star}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq(t-1)\cdot\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-7}}+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[A_{t,1:H-1}=\pi^{\star}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]\right]
