\displaystyle\leq(t-1)\cdot\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-7}}+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{t,1:H-1}=\pi\right\}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\pi^{\star}=\pi\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq(t-1)\cdot\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-7}}+\frac{1}{2^{H-1}}+\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-6}}\leq t\cdot\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-7}},

Here, the second-to-last inequality uses
Lemma
7.7
and the fact that
A_{t,H-1}
can only match a single policy
\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}
.

Casework on Reward TV Distance.

Armed with
Claim
7.7
, we now return to the proof of the reward TV distance calculation. We consider two cases. In the first case, the
t
-th trajectory is generated by an online rollout from
h=1
with the sequence of actions
A_{1:H}
. In the second case, the
t
-th trajectory is generated by first querying the
\mu
-reset model starting from
h_{\bot}\geq 2
, then rolling out with the sequence of actions
A_{h_{\bot}:H}
.

Case 1: Online Rollout from Layer 1.
First, we peel off the probability of
\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}
occuring:

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert\right]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}^{c}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right]+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\cdot\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert\right]

\displaystyle\leq(t-1)\cdot\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-7}}+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\cdot\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert\right].

(7.8)

Now we compute

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\phi(X_{t,H})=\mathsf{g}\wedge A_{t,H}=0\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\phi(X_{t,H})=\mathsf{b}\right\}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{=}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[A_{t,1:H}=\pi^{\star}\circ 0\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]+\frac{1}{2}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[A_{t,1:H-1}\neq\pi^{\star}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{=}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\left(\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{t,1:H}=\pi\circ 0\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{t,1:H-1}\neq\pi\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\pi^{\star}=\pi\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}},\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(iii)}{\leq}\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-6}}+\frac{\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}}{2^{H-1}}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\left(\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{t,1:H}=\pi\circ 0\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{t,1:H-1}\neq\pi\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(iv)}{\leq}\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-7}}+\frac{\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}}{2}.

(7.9)

For equality
(i)
we use the fact that if the
X_{t,H}
has a good label then we must have taken
\pi^{\star}
for the first
H-1
layers. For equality
(ii)
we use the fact that the indicators are measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}
and
\{\pi^{\star}=\pi\}
. For
(iii)
we apply a change-of-measure argument using
Lemma
7.7
. For
(iv)
we use the fact that the sequence of actions
A_{t,1:H-1}
can match at exactly one of the policies in
\Pi_{1:H-1}
.

Note that the other side of the inequality can be shown analogously. Therefore by plugging in Eq. (
7.9
) into (
7.8
) we get the bound that

\displaystyle\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert\right]\leq t\cdot\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-7}}.

(7.10)

Case 2:
\mu
-Reset Rollout from Layer
h_{\bot}\geq 2
.
Let us analyze the second case. Using the construction details,

\displaystyle\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]

\displaystyle=\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\phi(X_{t,H})=\mathsf{g}\wedge A_{t,H}=0\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\phi(X_{t,H})=\mathsf{b}\right\}+\mathbbm{1}\left\{\phi(X_{t,H})=\mathsf{d}\wedge A_{t,H}=1\right\}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]

\displaystyle=\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\phi(X_{t,h_{\bot}})=\mathsf{g}\wedge A_{t,h_{\bot}:H}=\pi^{\star}\circ 0\right\}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{2}\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{t,h_{\bot}:H-1}\neq\pi^{\star}\right\}+\mathbbm{1}\left\{\phi(X_{t,h_{\bot}})=\mathsf{b}\wedge A_{t,h_{\bot}:H-1}=\pi^{\star}\right\}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\phi(X_{t,h_{\bot}})=\mathsf{d}\wedge A_{t,h_{\bot}:H}=\pi^{\star}\circ 1\right\}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{h_{\bot}:H-1}}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\pi^{\star}=\pi\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]\bigg(\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{t,h_{\bot}:H}=\pi\circ 0\right\}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,h_{\bot}})=\mathsf{g}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{t,h_{\bot}:H-1}\neq\pi\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{t,h_{\bot}:H-1}=\pi\right\}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,h_{\bot}})=\mathsf{b}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{t,h_{\bot}:H}=\pi\circ 1\right\}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,h_{\bot}})=\mathsf{d}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]\bigg).

We apply
Lemma
7.9
separately to the terms inside the parentheses for every
\pi
. Then using a casework argument on the value of
A_{t,h_{\bot}:H}
and then averaging over the posterior of
\pi^{\star}
gives

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert\leq\frac{TH}{2^{H-5}}.

(7.11)

Putting It Together.

To conclude, the worst-case TV distance is the maximum of the two bounds we have shown in Eqs. (
7.10
) and (
7.11
), so therefore plugging into Eq. (
7.6
) we have

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert\right]\leq\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H}}+\max\left\{t\cdot\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-7}},\frac{TH}{2^{H-5}}\right\}\leq t\cdot\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-8}}.

The proof of second inequality is obtained similarly, as one just needs to change the law to be under
\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}
in the above argument. This concludes the proof of
Lemma
7.6
.
∎

Lemma 7.7

(Posterior of
\pi^{\star}
)
.

Fix any
t\in[T]
. Assume that
\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}
contains no repeated states. Then

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right\}\cdot\left\|\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\pi^{\star}=\cdot\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]-\mathrm{Unif}(\Pi_{1:H-1})\right\|_{1}\leq\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-6}}.

Proof.

In what follows, all of the probabilities
\Pr[\cdot]\coloneqq\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}[\cdot]
. Let
[x]_{+}\coloneqq\max\{x,0\}
. We can compute that

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right\}\cdot\left\|\Pr\left[\pi^{\star}=\cdot\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]-\mathrm{Unif}(\Pi_{1:H-1})\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right\}\cdot\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\left\lvert\Pr\left[\pi^{\star}=\pi\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]-\frac{1}{2^{H-1}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right\}\cdot 2\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\left[\Pr\left[\pi^{\star}=\pi\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]-\frac{1}{2^{H-1}}\right]_{+}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right\}\cdot\frac{2}{2^{H-1}}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\left[\frac{\Pr\left[\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]}{\Pr\left[\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]}-1\right]_{+}

\displaystyle\leq 2\max_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\left[\frac{\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right\}\cdot\Pr\left[\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]}{\Pr\left[\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]}-1\right]_{+}.

(7.12)

We now proceed by explicitly calculating the conditional distribution of
\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}
for every choice of optimal policy
\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}
. We will show that regardless of the choice
\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}
, the conditional distribution looks roughly like the uniform distribution over observations with a
\mathrm{Ber}(1/2)
reward at the end of every trajectory.

First, we will break up the distribution into trajectories:

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]=\left(\prod_{i<t}\Pr\left[\tau_{i}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]\right)\cdot\Pr\left[(X_{t,h_{\bot}:H},A_{t,h_{\bot}:H})\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right].

(7.13)

Claim 3
.

Fix any
i\in[t]
. If
\tau_{i}
is generated by sampling the
\mu
-reset distribution at some layer
h_{\bot}\geq 2
and then rolling out, we have for every
\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\tau_{i}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]\in\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{2^{(H+2)\cdot(H-h_{\bot}+1)}}\cdot\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1},\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1}\right]

We start by showing
Claim
3
. In our proof, we assume that
h_{\bot}=2
(the proof is easy to adapt to any
h_{\bot}\geq 2
with minor modification). Fix any index
i\in[t]
and let
\tau_{i}=(X_{2:H},A_{2:H},R)
be the
i
-th trajectory. Also fix any
\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}
. Then we can calculate

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\Pr\left[(X_{2:H},A_{2:H},R)\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\phi_{2}}\Pr\left[(X_{2:H},A_{2:H},R)\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\phi_{2}\right]\Pr\left[\phi_{2}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell\in\{\mathsf{g},\mathsf{b},\mathsf{d}\}}\sum_{\phi_{2}:\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\ell}\Pr\left[(X_{2:H},A_{2:H},R)\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\phi_{2}\right]\Pr\left[\phi_{2}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right].

We can separately analyze the sum for the different choices of the label of the initial state
X_{2}
. First, we do the case where
\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{b}
:

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\sum_{\phi_{2}:\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{b}}\Pr\left[(X_{2:H},A_{2:H},R)\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\phi_{2}\right]\Pr\left[\phi_{2}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\phi_{2}:\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{b}}\Pr\left[(X_{2},A_{2})\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\phi_{2}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\Pr\left[(X_{3:H},A_{3:H},R)\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\phi_{2},X_{2},A_{2}\right]\Pr\left[\phi_{2}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{=}\frac{1}{2^{H+2}}\sum_{\phi_{2}:\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{b}}\Pr\left[(X_{3:H},A_{3:H},R)\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\phi_{2},X_{2},A_{2}\right]\Pr\left[\phi_{2}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{=}\frac{1}{2^{H+2}}\sum_{\phi_{2}:\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{b}}\Pr\left[(X_{3:H},A_{3:H},R)\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{b}\right]\Pr\left[\phi_{2}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\Pr\left[\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{b}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]}{2^{H+2}}\Pr\left[(X_{3:H},A_{3:H},R)\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{b}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\vdots

\displaystyle\overset{(iii)}{=}\frac{\Pr\left[\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{b}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]}{2^{H+2}}\times\frac{\Pr\left[\phi_{3}(X_{3})=\mathsf{b}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{b}\right]}{2^{H+1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\dots\times\frac{\Pr\left[\phi_{H}(X_{H})=\mathsf{b}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\left\{\phi_{h}(X_{h})=\mathsf{b},2\leq h\leq H-1\right\}\right]}{2^{H+1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\Pr\left[R\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\left\{\phi_{h}(X_{h})=\mathsf{b},2\leq h\leq H\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\Pr\left[\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{b}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]}{2^{H+2}}\times\frac{\Pr\left[\phi_{3}(X_{3})=\mathsf{b}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{b}\right]}{2^{H+1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\dots\times\frac{\Pr\left[\phi_{H}(X_{H})=\mathsf{b}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\left\{\phi_{h}(X_{h})=\mathsf{b},2\leq h\leq H-1\right\}\right]}{2^{H+1}}\times\frac{1}{2}.

The equality
(i)
follows because the first state is chosen
\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{X}_{2})
and the action
A_{2}
is the one selected by
\mathsf{Alg}
via the policy
\pi^{(i)}
which is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{i-1}
. The equality
(ii)
follows because the distribution over the next state is only determined by
\mathcal{F}_{i-1}
(which includes some information about the decoder
\phi_{3}
) and the labeling
\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{b}
. Equality
(iii)
follows by applying chain rule over and over, noting that since
\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{b}
it must be the case that
\phi_{h}(X_{h})=\mathsf{b}
for all
h>2
, and therefore the probability of observing any given observation with a bad label is
1/2^{H+1}
.

Now we apply the posterior state label calculation of
Lemma
7.8
(using the fact that
\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}
contains no repeated states) to each term in the previous display to get that:

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\sum_{\phi_{2}:\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{b}}\Pr\left[(X_{2:H},A_{2:H},R)\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\phi_{2}\right]\Pr\left[\phi_{2}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]

\displaystyle\in\frac{1}{4}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2^{H+2}}\right)^{H-1}\cdot\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1},\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1}\right].

(7.14)

Next, we consider other terms in the sum. To bound the quantity

\displaystyle\sum_{\phi_{2}:\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{g}}\Pr\left[(X_{2:H},A_{2:H},R)\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\phi_{2}\right]\Pr\left[\phi_{2}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right],

a bit more care is required. In this case, we start off in the good latent state, and depending on whether the sequence of actions
A_{2:H}
is equal to
\pi^{\star}
we transit to the bad latent state. Let us denote
\overline{h}\geq 2
denote the first layer at which
a_{\overline{h}}
deviates from
\pi_{\overline{h}}
. Then using similar reasoning we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\phi_{2}:\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{g}}\Pr\left[(X_{2:H},A_{2:H},R)\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\phi_{2}\right]\Pr\left[\phi_{2}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\Pr\left[\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{g}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]}{2^{H+2}}\times\cdots\times\frac{\Pr\left[\phi_{\overline{h}}(X_{\overline{h}})=\mathsf{g}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\left\{\phi_{h}(X_{h})=\mathsf{g},\penalty\ \forall h<\overline{h}\right\}\right]}{2^{H}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\frac{\Pr\left[\phi_{\overline{h}+1}(X_{\overline{h}+1})=\mathsf{b}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\left\{\phi_{h}(X_{h})=\mathsf{g},\penalty\ \forall h\leq\overline{h}\right\}\right]}{2^{H+1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\vdots

\displaystyle\quad\times\frac{\Pr\left[\phi_{H}(X_{H})=\mathsf{b}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\left\{\phi_{h}(X_{h})=\mathsf{g},\penalty\ \forall h\leq\overline{h}\right\},\left\{\phi_{h}(X_{h})=\mathsf{b},\penalty\ \forall\overline{h}<h<H\right\}\right]}{2^{H+1}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\Pr\left[R\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\left\{\phi_{h}(X_{h})=\mathsf{g},\penalty\ \forall h\leq\overline{h}\right\},\left\{\phi_{h}(X_{h})=\mathsf{b},\penalty\ \forall\overline{h}<h<H\right\}\right].

Note that the conditional reward distribution given the latent state labels is

\displaystyle\Pr\left[R=1\mid\cdots\right]=\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{2:H}=\pi\circ 0\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{2:H-1}\neq\pi\right\}.

Again by applying the posterior calculation of
Lemma
7.8
we get

\displaystyle\sum_{\phi_{2}:\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{g}}\Pr\left[(X_{2:H},A_{2:H},R)\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\phi_{2}\right]\Pr\left[\phi_{2}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]

\displaystyle\in\begin{cases}\left(\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{2:H}=\pi\circ 0\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{2:H-1}\neq\pi\right\}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{1}{2^{H+2}}\right)^{H-1}\cdot\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1},\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1}\right]&\text{if}\penalty\ R=1\\[5.0pt]
\left(\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{2:H}=\pi\circ 1\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{2:H-1}\neq\pi\right\}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{1}{2^{H+2}}\right)^{H-1}\cdot\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1},\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1}\right]&\text{if}\penalty\ R=0\end{cases}

(7.15)

The last term in the sum, with
\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{d}
is similar, so we get that

\displaystyle\sum_{\phi_{2}:\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{d}}\Pr\left[(X_{2:H},A_{2:H},R)\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\phi_{2}\right]\Pr\left[\phi_{2}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]

\displaystyle\in\begin{cases}\left(\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{2:H}=\pi\circ 1\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{2:H-1}\neq\pi\right\}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{1}{2^{H+2}}\right)^{H-1}\cdot\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1},\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1}\right]&\text{if}\penalty\ R=1\\[5.0pt]
\left(\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{2:H}=\pi\circ 0\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{2:H-1}\neq\pi\right\}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{1}{2^{H+2}}\right)^{H-1}\cdot\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1},\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1}\right]&\text{if}\penalty\ R=0\end{cases}

(7.16)

(Note that the first indicators have been swapped in the previous display compared to Eq. (
7.15
).)

Summing Eqs. (
7.14
), (
7.15
), and (
7.16
), and applying casework on the different choices of
A_{2:H}
we get that

\displaystyle\Pr\left[(X_{2:H},A_{2:H},R)\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]\in\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2^{H+2}}\right)^{H-1}\cdot\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1},\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1}\right],

thus concluding the proof of
Claim
3
.

Claim 4
.

If
\tau_{i}
is generated by an online rollout, we have for every
\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}
,

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right\}\Pr\left[\tau_{i}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]\in\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right\}\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{2^{(H+2)\cdot H}}\cdot\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H},\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H}\right].

Now we prove
Claim
4
. Most of the hard work has already been done in the proof of
Claim
3
. Note that by construction
\phi_{1}(X_{1})=\mathsf{g}
. Using a similar calculation we have

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\Pr\left[(X_{1:H},A_{1:H},r)\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]

\displaystyle\in\begin{cases}\left(\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{1:H}=\pi\circ 0\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{1:H-1}\neq\pi\right\}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2^{H+2}}\right)^{H}\cdot\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H},\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H}\right]&\text{if}\penalty\ R=1\\[5.0pt]
\left(\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{1:H}=\pi\circ 1\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{1:H-1}\neq\pi\right\}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2^{H+2}}\right)^{H}\cdot\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H},\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H}\right]&\text{if}\penalty\ R=0\end{cases}

However, observe that under the event
\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}
we know that
A_{1:H-1}\neq\pi^{\star}
, so the first indicator cannot be
=1
in either case; so multiplying both sides of the previous display by
\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right\}
gives us the result of
Claim
4
.

To tidy up, we also state the calculation on the last trajectory, which does not include the prefactor of
\frac{1}{2}
because there are no observed rewards at the end:

Claim 5
.

\displaystyle\Pr\left[(X_{t,h_{\bot}:H},A_{t,h_{\bot}:H})\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]\in\frac{1}{2^{(H+2)\cdot(H-h_{\bot}+1)}}\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H},\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H}\right].

Now with
Claim
3
,
4
, and
5
in hand, we can finally return to computing a bound on Eq. (
7.12
). Letting
O
denote the total number of observations in
\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}
(which can be at most
TH
), we have for any
\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}
,

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right\}\Pr\left[\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]

\displaystyle\in\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right\}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{t-1}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2^{H+2}}\right)^{O}\cdot\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{TH},\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{TH}\right]\eqqcolon\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right\}\cdot[\underline{B},\overline{B}].

Moreover, for any
\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}
we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]=\frac{1}{2^{H-1}}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\Pr\left[\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]\geq\frac{2^{H-1}-T}{2^{H-1}}\cdot\underline{B}.

The last inequality follows because there are at most
T
different action sequences which have been executed by online trajectories in
\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}
, so therefore for all but at most
T
policies we have
\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right\}\Pr\left[\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]=\Pr\left[\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]
. Thus we arrive at the bound

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right\}\left\|\Pr\left[\pi^{\star}=\cdot\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]-\mathrm{Unif}(\Pi_{1:H-1})\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 2\max_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\left[\frac{\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right\}\Pr\left[\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]}{\Pr\left[\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]}-1\right]_{+}\leq 2\left[\frac{\overline{B}}{\left(1-T/{2^{H-1}}\right)\cdot\underline{B}}-1\right]_{+}

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\left(\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H-2}}\right)^{2TH+1}-1\right)\leq 2\cdot\frac{2T^{2}H+T}{2^{H-2}}\exp\left(\frac{2T^{2}H+T}{2^{H-2}}\right)\leq\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-6}}.

The second to last inequality uses the fact that
1+y\leq e^{y}
and
e^{y}-1\leq ye^{y}
, and the last inequality uses the fact that
T=2^{O(H)}
. This concludes the proof of
Lemma
7.7
.
∎

Lemma 7.8

(Posterior of New State Label)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be any filtration of
T
trajectories as well as annotations
\phi(x)
for a subset of observations
x\in\mathcal{F}
. Let
\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}
be any policy. Fix any
h\geq 2
, and let
x_{\mathrm{new}}\in\mathcal{X}_{h}-\mathcal{F}
. Then

\displaystyle\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(x_{\mathrm{new}})=\mathsf{g}\mid\mathcal{F},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]-\frac{1}{4}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{T}{2^{H}},

\displaystyle\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(x_{\mathrm{new}})=\mathsf{d}\mid\mathcal{F},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]-\frac{1}{4}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{T}{2^{H}},

\displaystyle\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(x_{\mathrm{new}})=\mathsf{b}\mid\mathcal{F},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{T}{2^{H}}.

Proof.

Let us denote
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
to be the completely annotated
\mathcal{F}
which includes all labels
\{\phi(X):X\in\mathcal{F}\}
. We will show that the conclusion of the lemma applies to every completion
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
, and since

\displaystyle\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(x_{\mathrm{new}})=\cdot\mid\mathcal{F},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]=\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(x_{\mathrm{new}})=\cdot\mid\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]\mid\mathcal{F},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right],

this will imply the result by Jensen’s inequality and convexity of
\lvert\cdot\rvert
.

We calculate the good label probability:

\displaystyle\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(x_{\mathrm{new}})=\mathsf{g}\mid\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]=\frac{2^{H}-\lvert\left\{X\in\mathcal{F}:\phi(X)=\mathsf{g}\right\}\rvert}{2^{H+2}-\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert}.

For the lower bound we have

\displaystyle\frac{2^{H}-\lvert\left\{X\in\mathcal{F}:\phi(X)=\mathsf{g}\right\}\rvert}{2^{H+2}-\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert}\geq\frac{2^{H}-T}{2^{H+2}}=\frac{1}{4}\cdot\left(1-\frac{T}{{2^{H}}}\right).

For the upper bound we have

\displaystyle\frac{2^{H}-\lvert\left\{x\in\mathcal{F}:\phi(x)=\mathsf{g}\right\}\rvert}{2^{H+2}-\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert}\leq\frac{2^{H}}{2^{H+2}-T}=\frac{1}{4}\cdot\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H+2}}\right)^{-1}\leq\frac{1}{4}\cdot\left(1+\frac{T}{{2^{H}}}\right),

which holds as long as
T\leq 2^{H}
. Combining both upper and lower bounds proves the lemma for the good label. The rest of the calculations are similar, so we omit them. This concludes the proof of
Lemma
7.8
.
∎

Lemma 7.9

(Posterior of State Label with Rollout)
.

Fix any
t\in[T]
. Suppose that episode
t
is sampled using the
\mu
-reset at layer
h_{\bot}\geq 2
, and that
\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}
contains no repeated states. Then for any
\pi\in\Pi_{h_{\bot}:H-1}
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,h_{\bot}})=\mathsf{g}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]-\frac{1}{4}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{TH}{2^{H-3}},

\displaystyle\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,h_{\bot}})=\mathsf{d}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]-\frac{1}{4}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{TH}{2^{H-3}},

\displaystyle\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,h_{\bot}})=\mathsf{b}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{TH}{2^{H-3}}.

Proof.

We will prove the result with
h_{\bot}=2
, and it is easy to adapt it to the general case (in fact the setting where
h_{\bot}>2
only results in tighter bounds). Using repeated application of chain rule and
Lemma
7.8
we get

\begin{aligned} \Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,2})=\mathsf{g}\wedge(X_{t,2:H},A_{t,2:H})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]&\in\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{1}{2^{H+2}}\right)^{H-1}\cdot\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1},\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1}\right]\\
\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,2})=\mathsf{b}\wedge(X_{t,2:H},A_{t,2:H})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]&\in\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{2^{H+2}}\right)^{H-1}\cdot\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1},\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1}\right]\\
\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,2})=\mathsf{d}\wedge(X_{t,2:H},A_{t,2:H})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]&\in\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{1}{2^{H+2}}\right)^{H-1}\cdot\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1},\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1}\right].\end{aligned}

Let’s prove the first inequality in the lemma statement. By Bayes Rule we have

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,2})=\mathsf{g}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,2})=\mathsf{g}\wedge(X_{t,2:H},A_{t,2:H})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]}{\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[(X_{t,2:H},A_{t,2:H})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,2})=\mathsf{g}\wedge(X_{t,2:H},A_{t,2:H})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]}{\sum_{\ell\in\{\mathsf{g},\mathsf{b},\mathsf{d}\}}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,2})=\ell\wedge(X_{t,2:H},A_{t,2:H})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]}.

From here it is easy to compute the upper bound

\displaystyle\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,2})=\mathsf{g}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]\leq\frac{1}{4}\cdot\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H-1}}\right)^{2H}\leq\frac{1}{4}+\frac{TH}{2^{H-3}}.

as well as the lower bound

\displaystyle\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,2})=\mathsf{g}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]\geq\frac{1}{4}\cdot\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{2H}\geq\frac{1}{4}-\frac{TH}{2^{H-3}}.

The other two inequalities are similarly shown, and this concludes the proof of
Lemma
7.9
.
∎

7.3  Open Problem: Benefit of Realizability

We state the following open problem, which might be of interest to the reader.

Question 7.1
.

Is there a benefit to policy realizability under the
\mu
-reset interaction protocol?

While we show that here,
PSDP
and
CPI
are not sample-efficient (
Theorem
7.4
), we do not have an information-theoretic lower bound. Intriguingly, the lower bound for the agnostic setting (
Theorem
7.5
) critically relies on the agnostic property (since the optimal policy
\pi^{\star}_{H}\notin\Pi_{H}
in the construction). On the upper bound side, it is unclear how to leverage the realizability of the policy class to design sample-efficient learning algorithms which do not incur the exponential in
H
dependence that
PSDP
and
CPI
do.

This question can be viewed as the policy-based analogue of the question raised by
[
MFR24
]
on whether it is possible to achieve sample-efficient learning with standard online access if one assumes only coverability and value function realizability (
Q^{\star}\in\mathcal{F}
).

7.4  Bibliographical Remarks

We will highlight several works which are relevant to the
\mu
-reset setting.

Abstract Policy Classes.

For abstract policy classes, the predominant approaches are Policy Search by Dynamic Programming (
PSDP
)
[
BKSN03
]
and Conservative Policy Iteration (
CPI
)
[
KL02
]
(see also
[
Sch14
,
SG14
,
BHS22
,
ABS23
]
). Roughly speaking, these approaches rely on access to a supervised learning oracle w.r.t. the given policy class. In particular,
PSDP
is a backbone of many contemporary theoretical works in RL
[
MHKL20
,
UXL+23
,
AFK24
,
MFR23
,
MBFR24
]
. Both
PSDP
and
CPI
operate under the
\mu
-reset setting, assume policy completeness, and achieve similar guarantees.

Smoothly-Parameterized Policy Classes.

In practice, one uses smoothly-parameterized policy classes
\Pi=\{\pi_{\theta}\}_{\theta\in\Theta}
(as given by neural networks with some architecture). Here, many works have studied policy gradient methods such as REINFORCE
[
Wil92
]
, Policy Gradient
[
SMSM99
]
, and Natural Policy Gradient
[
Kak01
]
. Empirically this has given rise to state-of-the-art algorithms for policy optimization
[
SLMJA17
,
SWDRK17
]
. A line of theoretical works have focused on tabular settings
[
SEM20
,
AKLM21
,
Xia22
,
ZCHCLC23
]
, which natural satisfy policy completeness. Other work studies policy gradient methods
[
AHKS20
,
ZCA21
,
LWGJS24
,
SCKM23
]
for the restricted setting of linear MDPs
[
JYWJ20
]
, designing exploratory algorithms which do not require
\mu
-reset access (but policy completeness is naturally satisfied). For the log-linear policy class parameterization (which captures neural policies), many works build on the
compatible function approximation
framework
[
SMSM99
]
, which can be viewed as a closure assumption for the gradient updates, see the papers
[
AKLM21
,
AR22
,
YDGLX22
]
. In addition, we highlight the works
[
BR24
,
HJ24
,
SKM25
]
which study policy gradient methods but require
\mu
-reset access as well some type of completeness/closure or gradient domination assumptions for global optimality guarantees.

Chapter 8Hybrid Resets

Now we study the hybrid resets interaction protocol, which combines both the local simulator as well as
\mu
-resets. We have already shown negative results in this thesis (
Theorem
4.3
and
7.5
) for these interaction protocols individually. These results rule out sample-efficient learners which can adapt to the intrinsic notion of complexity as measured by coverabilty/concentrability.

The main result in this chapter is a new algorithm called
PLHR
which leverages hybrid resets to perform sample-efficient learning. We give an overview of our results and approach in
Section
8.1
. To provide some intuition, we first give a algorithm for a warmup setting in
Section
8.2
before diving into the exposition of the main algorithm in
Section
8.3
. Open problems are discussed in
Section
8.4
, and all proofs are deferred to
Section
8.5
.

8.1  Overview

The previous negative results (
Theorem
4.3
and
7.5
) motivate us to consider hybrid reset access, where we handle the
exploration challenge
via exploratory resets, and the
error amplification challenge
via local simulator access. For value-based learning,
[
MFR24
]
show that local simulator access can overcome the notorious
double sampling problem
, which leads to error amplification. Furthermore, local simulator access circumvents the lower bound construction used to prove
Theorem
7.5
. Given this, it is conceivable that local simulators might provide significant power in agnostic policy learning.

Our main positive result in this chapter formalizes this intuition, where we provide a new algorithm that leverages hybrid resets for sample-efficient learning in Block MDPs (
Section
2.1
). Block MDPs are perhaps the simplest setting with large state spaces for developing RL algorithms, as well as a stepping stone to more challenging settings such as low-rank MDPs or coverable MDPs (the
PSDP
/
CPI
setting). Since our lower bound constructions are all Block MDPs, a positive result here already indicates the significant power of hybrid resets.

Theorem 8.1
.

Let
M
be a Block MDP of horizon
H
with
S
states and
A
actions. Let
\Pi
be any policy class. Suppose we are given an exploratory reset distribution
\mu=\{\mu_{h}\}_{h=1}^{H}
which satisfies pushforward concentrability (
Definition
2.2
) with parameter
C_{\mathsf{push}}
and can be factorized as
\mu_{h}=\psi\circ\nu_{h}
for some
\nu_{h}\in\Delta(\mathcal{S}_{h})
for all
h\in[H]
.
1
1

1

The factorization assumption is made for technical convenience, and can be removed (see
Section
8.5.2.1
).
With probability at least
1-\delta
,
PLHR
(
Algorithm
12
) returns an
\varepsilon
-optimal policy using

\displaystyle\mathrm{poly}\left(C_{\mathsf{push}},S,A,H,\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\log\lvert\Pi\rvert,\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\quad\text{samples from hybrid resets.}

To support the presentation of our main result, we first present a simplified algorithm called
PLHR.D
for an easier setting in
Section
8.2
, then present
PLHR
in
Section
8.3
.

We now give several remarks on
Theorem
8.1
.

•

As a caveat, we require the exploratory distribution
\mu
to satisfy
pushforward concentrability
, a strengthened version of concentrability introduced by
[
XJ21
]
. However, it can be checked that in the lower bound of
Theorem
7.5
, the constructed resets
\mu
indeed satisfy bounded pushforward concentrability.

•

Hybrid resets enables new statistical guarantees which are impossible with just local simulator access (cf.
Theorem
4.3
) and
\mu
-resets (cf.
Theorem
7.5
).

•

As discussed in
Chapter
7
,
PSDP
provably fails in the absence of policy completeness, and even policy realizability does not help. In contrast,
Theorem
8.1
achieves sample-efficient learning in the agnostic setting. Therefore, at least in Block MDPs, policy completeness is not an information theoretic barrier, only an algorithmic barrier.

•

Departing from prior work on Block MDPs, we do not require decoder realizability, namely that the learner is given a decoder class
\Phi\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{X}}
which satisfies
\phi\in\Phi
. With decoder realizability, sample-efficient learning is possible with standard online RL access. Since an (approximately) realizable policy class of size
\log\lvert\Pi\rvert\leq\mathrm{poly}(S,A,\log\lvert\Phi\rvert,1/\varepsilon)
can be constructed from a decoder class by a standard covering argument,
Theorem
8.1
provides substantially stronger guarantees than previously known (albeit under the stronger hybrid reset access).

Key Technical Insights for the Upper Bound.

The fundamental challenge in agnostic policy learning is to simultaneously estimate the values of all policies
\{V^{\pi}\}_{\pi\in\Pi}
in a statistically efficient manner. As discussed in
Section
2.3
, in the absence of any structure, this can require
\Omega(\min\{A^{H},\lvert\mathcal{X}\rvert A,\lvert\Pi\rvert\})
samples. Unfortunately, this sample complexity is too large for most practical scenarios.

To improve upon this result, prior works in agnostic policy learning have identified
additional structure
which facilitates the simultaneous estimation of
\{V^{\pi}\}_{\pi\in\Pi}
. For example,
[
SDMMS21
]
utilize autoregressive extrapolation when the MDP is low-rank, and
\mathsf{POPLER}
(
Algorithm
3
) constructs policy-specific Markov Reward Processes to take advantage of the sunflower property of
\Pi
.

This chapter adds a new technical tool called the
policy emulator
to this burgeoning toolbox (see
Definition
8.1
). A policy emulator, denoted
\widehat{M}
, is a carefully constructed tabular MDP which for a given parameter
\varepsilon>0
satisfies

\displaystyle\text{for all $\pi\in\Pi$:}\quad\lvert V^{\pi}-\widehat{V}^{\pi}\rvert\leq\varepsilon.

(8.1)

Here,
V^{\pi}
denotes the value of
\pi
in the underlying MDP, while
\widehat{V}^{\pi}
denotes the value of
\pi
in the policy emulator
\widehat{M}
. Once the policy emulator has been constructed, returning an
O(\varepsilon)
-optimal policy is straightforward by simply returning
\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi}\widehat{V}^{\pi}
. In this sense, the policy emulator is a “minimal object” for agnostic policy learning. In fact, we show in
Section
8.4.1
that every pushforward-coverable MDP admits a policy emulator of bounded size. The remaining question is: how can we construct this policy emulator using few samples?

Our key contribution is to devise a statistically efficient method for constructing this policy emulator in a bottom-up manner, leveraging the power of hybrid resets. As a warmup, we first explore a simpler scenario in
Section
8.2
where the latent dynamics of the Block MDP are deterministic and the learner has the capability to draw samples from the emission function
\psi(\cdot)
. Here, the emulator can directly be constructed over the latent state space
\mathcal{S}
in a model-based fashion. We then study the fully general setting in
Section
8.3
. Here, we construct the emulator directly over
\mathrm{poly}(C_{\mathsf{push}},S,A,H,\varepsilon^{-1},\log\lvert\Pi\rvert,\log\delta^{-1})
random observations sampled from the reset distributions
\mu_{1},\cdots\mu_{H}
. We will prove that the transitions/rewards of this policy emulator can be accurately estimated so that the guarantee in (
8.1
) holds.

8.2
PLHR.D
: Algorithm and Results for Warmup Setting

We first study an easier setting
and provide a simplified algorithm that illustrates the main approach that we will take in the general setting (in
Section
8.3
).

8.2.1  Warmup Setting: Deterministic Dynamics + Sampling Access to Emissions

We make the following simplications:

Assumption 2
.

Assume that:

1.

M
has deterministic latent transitions
P_{\mathsf{lat}}
and (possibly) stochastic rewards
R_{\mathsf{lat}}
.

2.

The learner is given both local simulator access and sampling access to the emission function
\psi
.

Intuitively,
Assumption
2
simplifies the problem considerably. Sampling access to the emission enables us to directly estimate the latent reward function
R_{\mathsf{lat}}
. Furthermore, we can associate a single observation
x\sim\psi(s)
with each state allowing us to query for
x^{\prime}\sim P(\cdot\mid s,a)
.
However, the fundamental challenge of identifying the
latent transition

\phi(x^{\prime})
remains, which is the main focus of
PLHR.D
. A few remarks are in order:

•

Without loss of generality, we can restrict ourselves to the open-loop policy class
\Pi_{\mathsf{open}}=\{\pi:\forall x\in\mathcal{X}_{h},\pi_{h}(x)\equiv a_{h},(a_{1},\cdots,a_{H})\in\mathcal{A}^{H}\}
. The reasoning is as follows. The optimal policy
\pi^{\star}
for
M
is constant over
\mathrm{supp}(\psi(s))
for every
s\in\mathcal{S}
. Due to deterministic latents, there exists some
\widetilde{\pi}\in\Pi_{\mathsf{open}}
which experiences the same (latent) trajectory
(s_{1}^{\star},a_{H}^{\star},\cdots,s_{H}^{\star},a_{H}^{\star})
that
\pi^{\star}
experiences. Such a policy
\widetilde{\pi}
achieves the optimal value from the fixed starting latent state
s_{1}
, even though it may not be the optimal policy
\pi^{\star}
that achieves the optimal value from
every
state.

•

We implicitly require knowledge of the latent state space
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{S}_{H}
in order to sample from
\psi
. The main algorithm,
PLHR
, will only require knowledge of a bound
\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert\leq S
.

•

Sampling access to the emission is more powerful than
\mu
-reset access, since a reset distribution with
C_{\mathsf{push}}=S
can be simulated for any
h\in[H]
by first
s\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{S}_{h})
then sampling
x\sim\psi(s)
.

Additional Notation: Monte Carlo Rollouts.

Our algorithms (both
PLHR.D
and
PLHR
) interact with the environment primarily by collecting Monte Carlo rollouts from states (or distributions over states). For a partial policy
\pi_{h:h^{\prime}}
, starting state
x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}
, and sample size
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote the algorithmic primitive
\mathsf{MC}(x,\pi_{h:h^{\prime}},n)
that:

(1)

Collects
n
rollouts
\{(x_{h}^{(t)},a_{h}^{(t)},r_{h}^{(t)},\cdots,x_{h^{\prime}}^{(t)},a_{h^{\prime}}^{(t)},r_{h^{\prime}}^{(t)})\}_{t\in[n]}
by running
\pi_{h:h^{\prime}}
starting from state
x
,

(2)

Returns the estimate
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\sum_{h\leq k\leq h^{\prime}}r_{k}^{(t)}
.

We overload the notation and use
\mathsf{MC}(d,\pi_{h:h^{\prime}},n)
for
d\in\Delta(\mathcal{X}_{h})
to denote a Monte Carlo estimate which first samples
x_{h}^{(t)}\sim d
then rolls out with
\pi_{h:h^{\prime}}
.

8.2.2  The
PLHR.D
Algorithm and Analysis Sketch

Algorithm 9

PLHR.D
(Policy Learning for Hybrid Resets, Deterministic Version)

1:

Sampling access to emission
\psi(\cdot)
, policy class
\Pi=\Pi_{\mathsf{open}}
, parameter
\varepsilon>0
.

2:

Initialize
\widehat{M}_{\mathsf{lat}}=\varnothing
, test policies
\{\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{h}\}_{h\in[H]}=\{\varnothing\}_{h\in[H]}
, and confidence sets
\mathcal{P}=\{\mathcal{S}\}_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}
.

3:

for
all
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}

do

//
Estimate all rewards.

4:

Estimate
\widehat{R}_{\mathsf{lat}}(s,a)
via Monte Carlo to precision
\varepsilon/H^{2}
.

5:

Initialize current layer index
\ell\leftarrow H
.

6:

while

\ell\neq 0

do

7:

If

\ell=H

then
go to line
11
.

8:

for
all
(s_{\ell},a_{\ell})\in\mathcal{S}_{\ell}\times\mathcal{A}

do

//
Construct transitions at layer
\ell
.

9:

Set
\mathcal{P}(s_{\ell},a_{\ell})\leftarrow{\textsf{Decoder.D}}(s_{\ell},a_{\ell},\widehat{M}_{\mathsf{lat}},\mathcal{P},\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{\ell+1})
.
//
Algorithm
10

10:

Set
\widehat{P}_{\mathsf{lat}}(s_{\ell},a_{\ell})\in\mathcal{P}(s_{\ell},a_{\ell})
arbitrarily.

//
Construct test policies and refit transitions.

11:

Set
(\ell_{\mathsf{next}},\widehat{M}_{\mathsf{lat}},\mathcal{P},\{\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{h}\}_{h\in[H]})\leftarrow{\textsf{Refit.D}}(\ell,\widehat{M}_{\mathsf{lat}},\mathcal{P},\{\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{h}\}_{h\in[H]},\varepsilon)
.
//
Algorithm
11

12:

Update current layer index
\ell\leftarrow\ell_{\mathsf{next}}
.

13:

Return

{\widehat{\pi}}\leftarrow\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi}\widehat{V}^{\pi}(s_{1})
.

Algorithm 10

Decoder.D
(Decoder, Deterministic Version)

1:

Tuple
(s_{h},a_{h})
, estimated MDP
\widehat{M}_{\mathsf{lat}}
, confidence sets
\mathcal{P}
,
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}
-valid test policies
\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{h+1}
.

2:

Sample an observation
x_{h+1}\sim P(\cdot\mid s_{h},a_{h})
.

3:

for

(s,s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}_{h+1}\times\mathcal{S}_{h+1}

do

4:

Estimate
V_{\mathsf{mc}}(x_{h+1}\mid\pi_{s,s^{\prime}})\leftarrow\mathsf{MC}(x_{h+1},\pi_{s,s^{\prime}},\widetilde{O}(1/\varepsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}^{2}))
to precision
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}/2
.

5:

Return

\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{out}}\leftarrow\mathcal{P}(s_{h},a_{h})\cap\{s\in\mathcal{S}_{h+1}:\penalty\ \forall s^{\prime}\neq s,\penalty\ \lvert V_{\mathsf{mc}}(x_{h+1}\mid\pi_{s,s^{\prime}})-\widehat{V}^{\pi_{s,s^{\prime}}}(s)\rvert\leq 2\varepsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}\}
.

Algorithm 11

Refit.D
(Refit, Deterministic Version)

1:

Layer
h
, estimated MDP
\widehat{M}_{\mathsf{lat}}
, confidence sets
\mathcal{P}
, test policies
\{\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{h}\}_{h\in[H]}
, parameter
\varepsilon>0
.

2:

Set tolerance
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}\coloneqq 2^{5}\cdot\varepsilon/H
.

3:

for

(s,s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}_{h}\times\mathcal{S}_{h}

do

//
Compute candidate test policies at layer
h

4:

Let
\pi_{s,s^{\prime}}\leftarrow\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi}\lvert\widehat{V}^{\pi}(s)-\widehat{V}^{\pi}(s^{\prime})\rvert
.

5:

Estimate to precision
\varepsilon/H
:

\displaystyle V_{\mathsf{mc}}(s\mid\pi_{s,s^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathsf{MC}(\psi(s),\pi_{s,s^{\prime}},\widetilde{O}(H^{2}/\varepsilon^{2})),

\displaystyle V_{\mathsf{mc}}(s^{\prime}\mid\pi_{s,s^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathsf{MC}(\psi(s^{\prime}),\pi_{s,s^{\prime}},\widetilde{O}(H^{2}/\varepsilon^{2})).

6:

Set
\mathsf{Violations}\leftarrow\{(s,\pi)\text{\penalty\ estimated in \hyperref@@ii[line:eval-policy]{line\penalty\ \ref*{line:eval-policy}} s.t.\penalty\ }\lvert V_{\mathsf{mc}}(s\mid\pi)-\widehat{V}^{\pi}(s)\rvert\geq\varepsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}-\varepsilon/H\}
.

7:

if

\mathsf{Violations}=\varnothing

then

//
No violations found, so return test policies.

8:

Set
\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{h}=\cup_{s,s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{h}}\{\pi_{s,s^{\prime}}\}
, and
return

(h-1,\widehat{M}_{\mathsf{lat}},\mathcal{P},\{\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{h}\}_{h\in[H]})
.

9:

else
//
Refit transitions to handle violations

10:

for

(s,\pi)\in\mathsf{Violations}

do

11:

Let
\tau=(\bar{s}_{h}=s,\cdots\bar{s}_{H})
be the states obtained by executing
\pi
from
s
in
\widehat{M}_{\mathsf{lat}}
.

12:

for
each
\bar{s}\in\tau

do

13:

Estimate
V_{\mathsf{mc}}(\bar{s}\mid\pi)\leftarrow\mathsf{MC}(\bar{s},\pi,\widetilde{O}(H^{4}/\varepsilon^{2}))
to precision
\varepsilon/H^{2}
.

14:

for
each
\bar{s}\in\tau
such that
\lvert V_{\mathsf{mc}}(\bar{s}\mid\pi)-\widehat{R}_{\mathsf{lat}}(\bar{s},\pi)-V_{\mathsf{mc}}(\widehat{P}_{\mathsf{lat}}(\bar{s},\pi)\mid\pi)\rvert\geq 4\varepsilon/H^{2}
:

15:

Update
\mathcal{P}(\bar{s},\pi)\leftarrow\mathcal{P}(\bar{s},\pi)\penalty\ \backslash\penalty\ \widehat{P}_{\mathsf{lat}}(\bar{s},\pi)
.

16:

Reset
\widehat{P}_{\mathsf{lat}}(s,a)\in\mathcal{P}(s,a)
arbitrarily for all
(s,a)
updated in
line
15
.

17:

Return

(\ell,\widehat{M}_{\mathsf{lat}},\mathcal{P},\{\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{h}\}_{h\in[H]})
where
\ell
is the max layer for which transitions were updated in
line
16
.

Now, we present an algorithm
PLHR.D
(
Algorithm
9
), which achieves the following guarantee.

Theorem 8.2
.

Let
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
be given and suppose that
Assumption
2
holds. Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
,
PLHR.D
(
Algorithm
9
) finds an
\varepsilon
-optimal policy using

\displaystyle\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{S^{5}A^{2}H^{5}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\quad\text{samples.}

The proof of
Theorem
8.2
is found in
Section
8.5.1
. In the rest of this section, we will explain
PLHR.D
and illustrate the main ideas.

PLHR.D
is an inductive algorithm that works from layer
H
down to layer
1
. It maintains an estimated latent MDP
\widehat{M}_{\mathsf{lat}}
, which approximates the ground truth latent transitions and rewards, as well as two other objects: transition confidence sets
\mathcal{P}
, which assigns a set of plausible next states to each state-action pair, and a set of
S^{2}
many test policies
\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}
, which it uses to estimate the latent transitions.
In the pseudocode and analysis, we use
\widehat{V}^{\pi}(\cdot)
and
\widehat{Q}^{\pi}(\cdot,\cdot)
to denote the value function and
Q
-function on the estimated
\widehat{M}_{\mathsf{lat}}
. Furthermore, we let
P_{\mathsf{lat}}(s,a)
(resp.
\widehat{P}_{\mathsf{lat}}
) denote the latent state which
(s,a)
transitions to in
M
(resp.
\widehat{M}_{\mathsf{lat}}
).

At every layer
h\in[H]
,
PLHR.D
tries to enforce three invariant properties:

1.

Policy Evaluation Accuracy.
For all pairs
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}_{h}\times\mathcal{A}
and
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{open}}
:
\lvert Q^{\pi}(s,a)-\widehat{Q}^{\pi}(s,a)\rvert\leq\Gamma_{h}
, where the error bound
\Gamma_{h}
grows linearly with
H-h
.

2.

Confidence Set Validity.
For all pairs
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}_{h}\times\mathcal{A}
, we have
P_{\mathsf{lat}}(s,a)\in\mathcal{P}(s,a)
.

3.

Test Policy Validity.
The
S^{2}
many test policies for layer
h
, i.e.
\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{h}:=\{\pi_{s,s^{\prime}}\}_{s,s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{h}}\subseteq\Pi_{\mathsf{open}}
, are defined for pairs of states
s,s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{h}
and are
valid
(maximally distinguishing and accurate):

\displaystyle\pi_{s,s^{\prime}}=\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi_{h:H}}\penalty\ \lvert\widehat{V}^{\pi}(s)-\widehat{V}^{\pi}(s^{\prime})\rvert,\quad\text{and}\quad\max_{\bar{s}\in\{s,s^{\prime}\}}\penalty\ |V^{\pi_{s,s^{\prime}}}(\bar{s})-\widehat{V}^{\pi_{s,s^{\prime}}}(\bar{s})|\leq\varepsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}.

(8.2)

Crucially, the accuracy level
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}

does not grow
with
H-h
.

Error Decomposition.

To motivate these three properties, we first state a standard error decomposition for
Q
-functions, and then show how
PLHR.D
controls each of terms separately. In what follows, fix some tuple
(s,a)
. We denote
R_{\mathsf{lat}}=R_{\mathsf{lat}}(s,a)
and
P_{\mathsf{lat}}=P_{\mathsf{lat}}(s,a)
, as well as the estimated counterparts
\widehat{R}_{\mathsf{lat}},\widehat{P}_{\mathsf{lat}}
similarly. The Bellman error for
(s,a)
can be decomposed as follows:

\displaystyle\left\lvert Q^{\pi}(s,a)-\widehat{Q}^{\pi}(s,a)\right\rvert\leq\underbrace{\left\lvert R_{\mathsf{lat}}-\widehat{R}_{\mathsf{lat}}\right\rvert}_{\text{reward error}}+\underbrace{\left\lvert\widehat{V}^{\pi}(P_{\mathsf{lat}})-\widehat{V}^{\pi}(\widehat{P}_{\mathsf{lat}})\right\rvert}_{\text{transition error}}+\underbrace{\left\lvert V^{\pi}(P_{\mathsf{lat}})-\widehat{V}^{\pi}(P_{\mathsf{lat}})\right\rvert}_{\text{policy eval.\penalty\ error at next layer}}.

(8.3)

Controlling the reward error is easy: we can simply collect i.i.d. samples using sampling access to
\psi
to estimate
\widehat{R}_{\mathsf{lat}}
up to
\varepsilon
accuracy (see
line
4
). Furthermore, if (A) holds at layer
h+1
, then we can bound the last term of Eq. (
8.3
) by
\Gamma_{h+1}
. Controlling the transition error requires more work, since the learner only gets to see observations
x_{\mathsf{new}}\sim P(\cdot\mid{}s,a)
, but not the latent state
\phi(x_{\mathsf{new}})
.

Decoding via Test Policies.

Our main insight is to estimate the latent state
\phi(x_{\mathsf{new}})
by using rollouts from
x_{\mathsf{new}}
to compare value functions with other latent states. Denoting
V_{\mathsf{mc}}(x_{\mathsf{new}}\mid\pi)
to be a Monte-Carlo estimate of
V^{\pi}(x_{\mathsf{new}})
, if we find some
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{h+1}
such that

\displaystyle V_{\mathsf{mc}}(x_{\mathsf{new}}\mid\pi)\approx\widehat{V}^{\pi}(s^{\prime}),\quad\text{for all}\penalty\ \pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{open}},

(8.4)

then we declare the latent state of
x_{\mathsf{new}}
to be
s^{\prime}
.
This allows us to bypass the statistical hardness of learning the decoder function
\phi
itself,
but, unfortunately, estimating
V^{\pi}(x_{\mathsf{new}})

for all

\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{open}}
seems to require number of samples proportional to
\mathfrak{C}(\Pi_{\mathsf{open}})=A^{H}

[
JLRSS23
]
. In other words, there is nothing better than just executing each policy one-by-one. However, in our algorithm, the test policies
\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}
allow us to circumvent this. In
Decoder.D
(
Algorithm
10
), we use
\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}
to run a “tournament” with only
S^{2}
Monte Carlo rollouts from
x_{\mathsf{new}}
to estimate the confidence set
\mathcal{P}
of plausible latent states. In
line
5
of
Decoder.D
, the confidence set is updated to be

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(s,a)\leftarrow\mathcal{P}(s,a)\cap\Big\{s\in\mathcal{S}_{h+1}:\penalty\ \forall s^{\prime}\neq s,\penalty\ \lvert V_{\mathsf{mc}}(x_{\mathsf{new}}\mid\pi_{s,s^{\prime}})-\widehat{V}^{\pi_{s,s^{\prime}}}(s)\rvert\lesssim\varepsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}\Big\}.

(8.5)

We show in
Lemma
8.1
that test policy validity (C) at layer
h+1
implies that the confidence set (
8.5
) is valid (B) for layer
h
and furthermore, setting the transition to be any
\widehat{P}_{\mathsf{lat}}\in\mathcal{P}
allows us to extrapolate to statement (
8.4
), thus giving us a bound on the transition error. As we have shown a bound for all three terms in Eq. (
8.3
), we conclude that (A) also holds at layer
h
.

Refitting Latent Dynamics.

Refit.D
(
Algorithm
11
) computes test policies for layer
h
that satisfy (C) after we have estimated the transitions/rewards. It does so by solving the maximally distinguishing planning problem ((
8.2
), left) in
\widehat{M}_{\mathsf{lat}}
for each
s,s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{h}
. Since (A) holds at layer
h
, these policies are guaranteed to be accurate; however, test policies are required to satisfy a higher level of accuracy
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}\ll\Gamma_{h}
which
does not increase with the horizon
. To provide intuition on why the higher level of accuracy is required for the test policies, we refer the reader to
Figure
8.1
.

Figure 8.1:
Illustration of how certifying accuracy of test policies prevents error amplification. Suppose we want to learn the transition
P_{\mathsf{lat}}(s_{h-1},a_{h-1})=s_{h}
. In
M_{\mathsf{lat}}
, all policies get value 0 from both
s_{h}
and
\bar{s}_{h}
, with the exception of a special
\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}{\widetilde{\pi}}
that gets value
2\Gamma_{h}
from
s_{h}
; in
\widehat{M}_{\mathsf{lat}}
all policies get value
\Gamma_{h}
from
s_{h}
and value 0 from
\bar{s}_{h}
. Thus,
\widehat{M}_{\mathsf{lat}}
satisfies
(A)
but any test policy
\pi_{s_{h},\bar{s}_{h}}\in\Pi
will not satisfy (C). It is unlikely that
\pi_{s_{h},\bar{s}_{h}}=\color[rgb]{0.55,0.14,1}{\widetilde{\pi}}
is selected, and if we execute any other
\pi
from the true transition
s_{h}
, we will observe value
0
, and thus decode the transition to
\widehat{P}_{\mathsf{lat}}(s_{h-1},a_{h-1})=\bar{s}_{h}
. Therefore,
\lvert Q^{\pi}(s_{h-1},a_{h-1})-\widehat{Q}^{\pi}(s_{h-1},a_{h-1})\rvert=2\Gamma_{h}
, thus
doubling
the policy evaluation error from layer
h
to
h-1
. Unchecked, this could cause exponential (in
H
) error amplification. Certifying test policy accuracy prevents this, as
Refit.D
would detect the violation
\lvert V^{\pi}(s_{h})-\widehat{V}^{\pi}(s_{h})\rvert=\Gamma_{h}\gg\varepsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}
for any
\pi\in\Pi
and refit
\widehat{M}_{\mathsf{lat}}
instead.

Fortunately, since there are only
S^{2}
test policies we can use Monte Carlo rollouts to check whether they are
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}
-accurate. If they are, we simply decrement to layer
h-1
and continue (
line
8
). If not, the rollouts will find a “certificate of inaccuracy”: some tuple
(s,\pi)
for which
\lvert\widehat{V}^{\pi}(s)-V^{\pi}(s)\rvert
is large, which we can use to find and delete an erroneous transition in
\widehat{P}_{\mathsf{lat}}
from a confidence set. Since this update can occur at some layer
\ell\gg h
,
\widehat{M}_{\mathsf{lat}}
may no longer satisfy the inductive hypotheses, so
Refit.D
restarts the outer loop of
PLHR.D
at the maximum layer
\ell
for which some transition was updated (
line
17
). Critically, we show in
Lemma
8.2
that refitting never deletes the true
P_{\mathsf{lat}}
, so revisiting only happens
SA\cdot(S-1)
times.

Performance of Estimated Policy.

Eventually,
PLHR.D
will terminate at layer
h=1
. Thanks to (A), we can evaluate all
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{open}}
on the fully constructed
\widehat{M}_{\mathsf{lat}}
and return the policy
{\widehat{\pi}}
which achieves the highest value. The inductive argument we have outlined shows that
{\widehat{\pi}}
is an
\varepsilon
-optimal policy and that
PLHR.D
uses
\mathrm{poly}(S,A,H,\varepsilon^{-1})
samples.

8.3
PLHR
: Algorithm and Main Results

In this section, we extend our result in
Theorem
8.2
to handle the general setting. We give our main algorithm,
PLHR
, which takes inspiration from
PLHR.D
. We show how
PLHR
leverages hybrid resets to solve agnostic policy learning, with sample complexity that scales with the pushforward concentrability
C_{\mathsf{push}}
of the reset distribution
\mu
, a measure of the intrinsic difficulty of exploration.

First, we restate our main result of
Theorem
8.1
with the precise dependence on the problem parameters.

Theorem
8.1
.

Let
M
be a Block MDP of horizon
H
with
S
states and
A
actions, and let
\Pi
be any policy class. Suppose we are given an exploratory reset distribution
\mu=\{\mu_{h}\}_{h=1}^{H}
which satisfies pushforward concentrability with parameter
C_{\mathsf{push}}
and can be factorized as
\mu_{h}=\psi\circ\nu_{h}
for some
\nu_{h}\in\Delta(\mathcal{S}_{h})
for all
h\in[H]
. With probability at least
1-\delta
, the
PLHR
algorithm (
Algorithm
12
) returns an
\varepsilon
-optimal policy using

\displaystyle\frac{C_{\mathsf{push}}^{4}S^{24}A^{30}H^{39}}{\varepsilon^{18}}\cdot\mathrm{polylog}\left(C_{\mathsf{push}},S,A,H,\lvert\Pi\rvert,\varepsilon^{-1},\delta^{-1}\right)\quad\text{samples from hybrid resets.}

The proof is deferred to
Section
8.5.2
. In the rest of this section, we discuss the main aspects of
PLHR
and provide intuition for how it addresses new technical challenges once we relax
Assumption
2
.

8.3.1  Algorithm Overview

We now present an overview of
PLHR
, whose pseudocode can be found in
Algorithm
12
. Similar to
PLHR.D
, it uses two subroutines:
Decoder
, found in
Algorithm
13
, and
Refit
, found in
Algorithm
14
. Overall,
PLHR
has a similar structure to
PLHR.D
, but it requires several new ideas to address several challenges to circumvent needing
Assumption
2
:

•

Under
Assumption
2
, the learner had sampling access to the emission function
\psi
; as a consequence, we could construct an estimate of the latent MDP
\widehat{M}_{\mathsf{lat}}
which was defined over the latent state space
\mathcal{S}
. Sampling access to
\psi
was crucial since it allowed us to disambiguate observations. If the learner only has access to the reset distribution
\mu
, it is nontrivial even to estimate the latent reward function
R_{\mathsf{lat}}
, since we cannot access the decoder for observations
x\sim\mu
.

•

In
PLHR.D
, even though we were supplied a policy class
\Pi
, we could instead use the open-loop policy class
\Pi_{\mathsf{open}}
as a proxy, since we were guaranteed that
\max_{\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{open}}}V^{\pi}\geq\max_{\pi\in\Pi}V^{\pi}
. If the MDP has stochastic latent transitions,
\Pi_{\mathsf{open}}
might not contain any good policy. Thus, we need to directly evaluate the given policies
\pi\in\Pi
in order to solve the agnostic policy learning problem.

Policy Emulators.

To address these challenges, we take the more straightforward approach: instead of trying to construct latent transitions/rewards,
we directly construct an MDP
\widehat{M}
over observations
. The MDP
\widehat{M}
has a restricted state space
\mathcal{X}[\widehat{M}]\subseteq\mathcal{X}
but inherits the same action space
\mathcal{A}
and horizon
H
. Unlike the standard approach taken in tabular RL, we cannot hope to approximate the dynamics of the true MDP
M
in an information theoretic sense, as the transition
P(\cdot\mid x,a)
is an
\lvert\mathcal{X}\rvert
-dimensional object (requiring
\Omega(\lvert\mathcal{X}\rvert)
samples to estimate). Taking a step back, all we need is that
\widehat{M}
enables accurate policy evaluation, i.e., denoting
\widehat{V}^{\pi}
to be the value function of
\pi
on
\widehat{M}
, we have
\max_{\pi\in\Pi}\penalty\ \lvert V^{\pi}-\widehat{V}^{\pi}\rvert\leq\varepsilon
. In this sense
\widehat{M}
is a “minimal object” which allows us to emulate the values of all policies
\pi\in\Pi
. This is formalized in the following definition.
2
2

2

Similar terminology of an
emulator
is defined in
[
GMR24
]
. Their definition formalizes what it means for estimated transitions to approximate certain Bellman backup operations, and is tailored to linear MDPs.
In the sequel, we denote
\mathcal{X}_{h}[\widehat{M}]
and
\mathcal{X}_{h:H}[\widehat{M}]
to be the restriction of the state space of
\widehat{M}
to the given layer(s).

Definition 8.1

(Policy Emulator)
.

Let
\Pi
be a policy class and
M
be an MDP. Fix any
\nu\in\Delta(\mathcal{X})
. We say
\widehat{M}
is an
\varepsilon
-accurate
policy emulator
for
\nu
if there exists
\widehat{\nu}\in\Delta(\mathcal{X}[\widehat{M}])
such that:

\displaystyle\max_{\pi\in\Pi}\penalty\ \left\lvert\En_{x\sim\nu}[V^{\pi}(x)]-\En_{x\sim\widehat{\nu}}[\widehat{V}^{\pi}(x)]\right\rvert\leq\varepsilon.

Definition
8.1
naturally extends the concept of
uniform convergence

[
SB14
]
to the interactive setting of policy learning. Clearly, if
\widehat{M}
is an
\varepsilon
-accurate policy emulator for the starting distribution
d_{1}
, we can find an
O(\varepsilon)
-optimal policy. One inspiration for
Definition
8.1
is the Trajectory Tree algorithm (
Algorithm
2
), which can be viewed as a way to use local simulator access to build a policy emulator with
\lvert\mathcal{X}[\widehat{M}]\rvert=\widetilde{O}(H\mathfrak{C}(\Pi)/\varepsilon^{2})
states, requiring sample complexity scaling with the worst-case notion of complexity
\mathfrak{C}(\Pi)
(see
Theorem
4.1
).

In contrast,
PLHR
utilizes the reset distribution
\mu
to construct a policy emulator with state space and sample complexity scaling with the instance-dependent notion of complexity
C_{\mathsf{push}}
. We do this in an inductive fashion, working back from layer
H
to layer 1.

•

At every layer
h
, we sample
\mathrm{poly}(C_{\mathsf{push}},S,A,H,\varepsilon^{-1},\log\lvert\Pi\rvert)
states from
\mu_{h}
to form the policy emulator’s state space
\mathcal{X}_{h}[\widehat{M}]
. The rewards of every tuple
(x_{h},a_{h})\in\mathcal{X}_{h}[\widehat{M}]\times\mathcal{A}
are estimated via the local simulator.

•

Once the transitions of
\widehat{M}
has been constructed from layer
h+1
onward, we call
Decoder
on every
(x_{h},a_{h})\in\mathcal{X}_{h}[\widehat{M}]\times\mathcal{A}
.
Decoder
first samples a dataset
\mathcal{D}
of transitions from
P(\cdot\mid x_{h},a_{h})
(in
line
3
) and then performs Monte Carlo rollouts over observations in
\mathcal{D}
using test policies
\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{h+1}
(in
line
6
). In contrast with
PLHR.D
, since
PLHR
directly works in observation space, the test policies are defined for pair of observations
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{h+1}[\widehat{M}]
, not pairs of latent states.
Decoder
estimates a transition function
\widehat{P}(\cdot\mid x_{h},a_{h})\in\Delta(\mathcal{X}_{h+1}[\widehat{M}])
as well as a confidence set
\mathcal{P}(x_{h},a_{h})\subseteq\Delta(\mathcal{X}_{h+1}[\widehat{M}])
.

•

After transitions at layer
h
are constructed, we call
Refit
which tries to compute accurate test policies
\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{h}
for layer
h
. If
Refit
succeeds, then
PLHR
continues the decoding/refitting loop at layer
h-1
. Otherwise,
Refit
searches in the policy emulator
\widehat{M}
for an inaccurate transition
\widehat{P}(\cdot|\bar{x},\bar{a})
and updates it. The layer index
\ell
is set to the maximum layer for which an
(\bar{x},\bar{a})
is updated, and
PLHR
restarts at that layer
\ell
.

Eventually,
PLHR
will reach layer 1, giving a fully-constructed policy emulator
\widehat{M}
. Returning the best policy in
\widehat{M}
is guaranteed to be a near-optimal policy for the true MDP
M
.

Algorithm 12

PLHR
(Policy Learning for Hybrid Resets)

1:

Reset distributions
\mu=\{\mu_{h}\}_{h\in[H]}
, policy class
\Pi
, parameters
\varepsilon>0
and
\delta\in(0,1)
.

2:

Initalize policy emulator
\widehat{M}=\varnothing
, test policies
\{\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{h}\}_{h\in[H]}=\{\varnothing\}_{h\in[H]}
, transition confidence sets
\mathcal{P}=\varnothing
.

3:

Set
n_{\mathsf{reset}}\asymp\tfrac{C_{\mathsf{push}}SA^{2}}{\varepsilon^{3}}\cdot\log\tfrac{SA\lvert\Pi\rvert}{\delta}
.

4:

for

h=1,\cdots,H

do

//
Initialize policy emulator

5:

Sample
n_{\mathsf{reset}}
observations from
\mu_{h}
and add to
\mathcal{X}_{h}[\widehat{M}]
.

6:

for
every
(x_{h},a_{h})\in\mathcal{X}_{h}[\widehat{M}]\times\mathcal{A}

do

7:

Estimate
\widehat{R}(x_{h},a_{h})\leftarrow\mathsf{MC}(x_{h},a_{h},\widetilde{O}(H^{2}/\varepsilon^{2}))
.

8:

Initialize
\mathcal{P}(x_{h},a_{h})=\Delta(\mathcal{X}_{h+1}[\widehat{M}])
.

9:

Set current layer index
\ell\leftarrow H
.

10:

while

\ell\neq 0

do

11:

If

\ell=H
:
go to line
15
.

//
Construct transitions at layer
\ell

12:

for
each
(x_{\ell},a_{\ell})\in\mathcal{X}_{\ell}[\widehat{M}]\times\mathcal{A}

do

13:

Set
\mathcal{P}(x_{\ell},a_{\ell})\leftarrow{\textsf{Decoder}}((x_{\ell},a_{\ell}),\widehat{M},\mathcal{P},\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{\ell+1},\varepsilon,\delta)

//
See
Algorithm
13

14:

Set
\widehat{P}(\cdot\mid x_{\ell},a_{\ell})\in\mathcal{P}(x_{\ell},a_{\ell})
arbitrarily.

//
Construct test policies and refit transitions.

15:

Set
(\ell_{\mathsf{next}},\widehat{M},\{\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{h}\}_{h\in[H]},\mathcal{P})\leftarrow{\textsf{Refit}}(\ell,\widehat{M},\mathcal{P},\{\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{h}\}_{h\in[H]},\varepsilon,\delta)
.
//
See
Algorithm
14

16:

Update current layer index
\ell\leftarrow\ell_{\mathsf{next}}
.

17:

Return

{\widehat{\pi}}\leftarrow\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi}\En_{x_{1}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{X}_{1}[\widehat{M}])}[\widehat{V}^{\pi}(x_{1})]
.

8.3.2
Decoder
Subroutine

In this section, we explain
Decoder
, which for a given
(x_{h},a_{h})
pair computes a confidence set of transitions
\mathcal{P}(x_{h},a_{h})
over the policy emulator states in the next layer
\mathcal{X}_{h+1}[\widehat{M}]
. The main salient difference with
Decoder.D
is that we now adopt a more sophisticated confidence set construction to ensure that arbitrary policies
\pi\in\Pi
can be emulated by
\widehat{M}
.

Algorithm 13

Decoder

1:

Tuple
(x_{h},a_{h})
, policy emulator
\widehat{M}
, confidence sets
\mathcal{P}
,
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{dec}}
-valid test policies
\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{h+1}
, parameters
\varepsilon>0
,
\delta\in(0,1)
.

2:

Set
n_{\mathsf{dec}}\asymp\tfrac{S^{2}A^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot\log\tfrac{C_{\mathsf{push}}SAH\lvert\Pi\rvert}{\varepsilon\delta}
,
n_{\mathsf{mc}}\asymp\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot\log\tfrac{C_{\mathsf{push}}SAH\lvert\Pi\rvert}{\varepsilon\delta}
.

3:

Sample dataset of
n_{\mathsf{dec}}
observations
\mathcal{D}\sim P(\cdot\mid x_{h},a_{h})
.

4:

for
every
x^{(i)}\in\mathcal{D}

do

//
Individually decode every observation

5:

for
every
(x,x^{\prime})\in\mathcal{X}_{h+1}[\widehat{M}]\times\mathcal{X}_{h+1}[\widehat{M}]

do
:

6:

Estimate
V_{\mathsf{mc}}(x^{(i)}\mid\pi_{x,x^{\prime}})\leftarrow\mathsf{MC}(x^{(i)},\pi_{x,x^{\prime}},n_{\mathsf{mc}})
.

7:

Define:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}[x^{(i)}]\leftarrow\left\{x\in\mathcal{X}_{h+1}[\widehat{M}]:\penalty\ \forall x^{\prime}\neq x,\penalty\ \left\lvert V_{\mathsf{mc}}(x^{(i)}\mid\pi_{x,x^{\prime}})-\widehat{V}^{\pi_{x,x^{\prime}}}(x)\right\rvert\leq\varepsilon_{\mathsf{dec}}+2\varepsilon\right\}.

8:

Define
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{obs}}
as the decoder graph with
//
See
Definition
8.2

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{L}}\coloneqq\mathcal{D},\quad\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{R}}\coloneqq\mathcal{X}_{h+1}[\widehat{M}],\quad\text{and decoder function}\penalty\ \mathcal{T}.

9:

Return
:
\mathcal{P}
defined using Eq.
(
8.6
)
.

We first introduce an intermediate object, called the decoder graph.

Definition 8.2

(Decoder Graph)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{L}},\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{R}}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
, and let
\mathcal{T}:\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{L}}\mapsto 2^{\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{R}}}
be a decoder function. The
decoder graph
, denoted
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{obs}}
, is defined as the bipartite graph with vertices
V=\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{L}}\cup\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{R}}
and edges
E=\{(x_{l},x_{r}):x_{l}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{L}},x_{r}\in\mathcal{T}[x_{l}]\}
.

In words, the decoder graph
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{obs}}
draws an edge from every observation
x_{l}
sampled from the transition to observations
x_{r}
sampled from the reset if the value functions for all test policies are similar. Thus, the decoder graph
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{obs}}
summarizes the similarity information encoded by individually decoding each observation.

The other ingredient is a notion of
pushforward distribution
, which, when supplied a distribution over observations, collapses a policy
\pi
to a distribution over actions.

Definition 8.3

(Pushforward Distribution/Policy)
.

Let
\nu\in\Delta(\mathcal{X})
be a distribution over observations. For any policy
\pi:\mathcal{X}\to\Delta(\mathcal{A})
, define the
pushforward distribution
, denoted
\pi_{\sharp}\nu\in\Delta(\mathcal{A})
, as

\displaystyle\left[\pi_{\sharp}\nu\right](a)\coloneqq\En_{x\sim\nu}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi(x)=a\right\}\right]\quad\text{for all}\penalty\ a\in\mathcal{A}.

For any
\pi\in\Pi
, the emission
\psi:\mathcal{S}\to\Delta(\mathcal{X})
induces a pushforward distribution; we slightly abuse notation and call the function
\pi_{\sharp}\psi:\mathcal{S}\to\Delta(\mathcal{A})
the
pushforward policy
.

Confidence Set Construction.

Now we are ready to specify the confidence set construction of
Decoder
. Denote
\{\mathbb{C}_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
to be the connected components of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{obs}}
. For any
\mathbb{C}\in\{\mathbb{C}_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
, denote
\mathbb{C}_{\mathsf{L}}\subseteq\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{L}}
and
\mathbb{C}_{\mathsf{R}}\subseteq\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{R}}
to be the left/right observation sets respectively. In what follows, we use
p(\cdot\mid\mathbb{C}_{\mathsf{R}})
to denote the conditional distribution over
\mathbb{C}_{\mathsf{R}}
, i.e.,
p(x\mid\mathbb{C}_{\mathsf{R}})=p(x)/p(\mathbb{C}_{\mathsf{R}})\cdot\mathbbm{1}\left\{x\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathsf{R}}\right\}
. Given a decoder graph
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{obs}}
and input confidence set
\mathcal{P}(x_{h},a_{h})
, the updated confidence set is defined for
\beta\coloneqq\widetilde{O}((\sqrt{SA^{2}}+S)\varepsilon)
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}\coloneqq\Big\{p\in\mathcal{P}(x_{h},a_{h}):\penalty\

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbb{C}\in\{\mathbb{C}_{j}\}}\left\lvert p(\mathbb{C}_{\mathsf{R}})-\frac{\lvert\mathbb{C}_{\mathsf{L}}\rvert}{\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{L}}\rvert}\right\rvert\leq 3\varepsilon,

\displaystyle\quad\max_{\pi\in\Pi}\penalty\ \sum_{\mathbb{C}\in\{\mathbb{C}_{j}\}}\frac{\lvert\mathbb{C}_{\mathsf{L}}\rvert}{\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{L}}\rvert}\cdot\left\|\pi_{\sharp}\mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb{C}_{\mathsf{L}})-\pi_{\sharp}p(\cdot\mid\mathbb{C}_{\mathsf{R}})\right\|_{1}\leq\beta\Big\}.

(8.6)

Intuition for (
8.6
).

We give some intuition for the construction in (
8.6
), and refer the reader to the example in
Figure
8.2
. The high level goal is to find a set of distributions
\mathcal{P}(x_{h},a_{h})
supported on
\mathcal{X}_{h+1}[\widehat{M}]
such that if we plug any
\widehat{P}\in\mathcal{P}(x_{h},a_{h})
into our policy emulator, the policy evaluation error is bounded, i.e.,

\displaystyle Q^{\pi}(x_{h},a_{h})\approx\widehat{R}(x_{h},a_{h})+\En_{x^{\prime}\sim\widehat{P}}[\widehat{V}^{\pi}(x^{\prime})],\quad\text{for all}\quad\pi\in\Pi.

In particular, we need every
\widehat{P}\in\mathcal{P}
to witness accurate policy emulation for the distribution
P=P(\cdot\mid x_{h},a_{h})
, so we require
\widehat{P}
to satisfy a bound on:

\displaystyle\max_{\pi\in\Pi}\penalty\ \left\lvert\En_{x^{\prime}\sim P}[V^{\pi}(x)]-\En_{x^{\prime}\sim\widehat{P}}[\widehat{V}^{\pi}(x^{\prime})]\right\rvert.

(8.7)

Now we discuss how the constraints for
\mathcal{P}
control this policy emulation error for every
\widehat{P}\in\mathcal{P}
. Intuitively, the connected components
\{\mathbb{C}_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{obs}}
represent a “soft” clustering of observations, since all observations in a given connected component
\mathbb{C}\in\{\mathbb{C}_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
have similar
Q
-functions for every test policy. We further prove that this implies that the
Q
-functions are similar within
\mathbb{C}
for every
\pi\in\Pi
. Now we discuss the constraints.

Figure 8.2:
Confidence set construction example with
H=2
. At layer
2
, the MDP has 4 latent states,
s_{2,A}
,
s_{2,B}
,
s_{2,C}
, and
s_{2,D}
. Since
\mu_{2}
has uniform mass, we sample representative observations from each latent state in our policy emulator
\widehat{M}
. Now consider using
Decoder
to learn the transition
P(\cdot\mid x_{1},a_{1})
. We cannot disambiguate between observations from
s_{2,A}
and
s_{2,B}
via test policies (similarly for
s_{2,C}
and
s_{2,D}
). Thus, the learned decoder graph
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{obs}}
has the two connected components as shown. The
marginal constraint
enforces that every
\widehat{P}\in\mathcal{P}
must place half the mass on observations from
s_{2,A}
and
s_{2,B}
and the other half on observations from
s_{2,C}
and
s_{2,D}
. This is enough to ensure that the policy evaluation error for
\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{1}
are controlled (cf. Eq. (
8.7
)). However, it is not enough to ensure that policy evaluation error for
\pi_{\mathrm{mix}}
is controlled, since
\pi_{\mathrm{mix}}
is not constant over each connected component. As an example, consider the
\widehat{P}
which puts uniform mass on the observations from
s_{2,B}
and
s_{2,D}
(the orange blocks). We have
Q^{\pi_{\mathrm{mix}}}(x_{1},a_{1})=1
while
\widehat{Q}^{\pi_{\mathrm{mix}}}(x_{1},a_{1})=0
. This explains why we need the
pushforward constraint
, which requires that the pushforward distribution of
\pi_{\mathrm{mix}}
is matched on every connected component.

•

Marginal Constraint
: The first condition expresses a TV distance constraint on the marginals over connected components: that is, the estimated distribution
\widehat{P}
must place a similar amount of mass on each connected component as we observe in the samples from
P
. This ensures that for all
a\in\mathcal{A},\pi\in\Pi
:

\displaystyle\En_{x^{\prime}\sim P}[Q^{\pi}(x^{\prime},a)]\approx\En_{x^{\prime}\sim\widehat{P}}[\widehat{Q}^{\pi}(x^{\prime},a)].

•

Pushforward Constraint
: However, the marginal constraint is insufficient for accurate policy emulation because in general, policies in the given class
\Pi
are
not constant
over a given
\mathbb{C}
. We give an example of this in
Figure
8.2
. To address this, we need to ensure that over each
\mathbb{C}
, the pushforward distributions also match. This is precisely captured in an averaged sense by the second condition.

We show that the set of
\mathcal{P}
which satisfies both constraints yields a bound on the policy emulation error (
8.7
).

Technical Tool: Projected Measures.

The technical challenge in establishing Eq. (
8.7
) is that the high-dimensional
P
is supported on
\mathcal{X}
, while we want to approximate it with
\widehat{P}
supported on the states
\mathcal{X}_{h+1}[\widehat{M}]\subseteq\mathcal{X}
of the policy emulator. To address this, we introduce a notion of
projected measures
onto the state space
\mathcal{X}_{h+1}[\widehat{M}]
, denoted
\mathsf{Proj}:\Delta(\mathcal{S})\to\Delta(\mathcal{X}_{h+1}[\widehat{M}])
(see
Definition
8.6
for a formal definition), which approximates
\psi\circ d
for any distribution over latent states
d
. Using the triangle inequality on Eq. (
8.7
), we can decompose the policy emulation error using the projected measure as an intermediary quantity:

(
8.7
)

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\left\lvert\En_{x^{\prime}\sim P}[V^{\pi}(x^{\prime})]-\En_{x^{\prime}\sim\mathsf{Proj}(P_{\mathsf{lat}})}[V^{\pi}(x^{\prime})]\right\rvert}_{\text{projection error}}\penalty\ +\penalty\ \underbrace{\left\lvert\En_{x^{\prime}\sim\mathsf{Proj}(P_{\mathsf{lat}})}[V^{\pi}(x^{\prime})]-\En_{x^{\prime}\sim\mathsf{Proj}(P_{\mathsf{lat}})}[\widehat{V}^{\pi}(x^{\prime})]\right\rvert}_{\text{policy eval.\penalty\ error at next layer}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\penalty\ \underbrace{\left\lvert\En_{x^{\prime}\sim\mathsf{Proj}(P_{\mathsf{lat}})}[\widehat{V}^{\pi}(x^{\prime})]-\En_{x^{\prime}\sim\widehat{P}}[\widehat{V}^{\pi}(x^{\prime})]\right\rvert}_{\text{transition error}}

This decomposition generalizes Eq. (
8.5
) to the stochastic BMDP setting. To obtain a bound on the projection error, we observe that pushforward concentrability implies that the observations sampled from
\mu
are sufficiently representative of observations from the transition
P
, and therefore
\mathsf{Proj}(P_{\mathsf{lat}})
approximates
P
well. Similar to the analysis of
Decoder.D
, a bound on the policy evaluation error at the next layer can be shown via induction. Lastly, our analysis shows that the construction (
8.6
) admits a bound on the transition error.

Algorithm 14

Refit

1:

Layer
h
, policy emulator
\widehat{M}
, confidence sets
\mathcal{P}
, test policies
\{\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{h}\}_{h\in[H]}
, parameters

and
\delta\in(0,1)
.

Set
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}\coloneqq 80\cdot H\varepsilon
,

for
every
(x,x^{\prime})\in\mathcal{X}_{h}[\widehat{M}]\times\mathcal{X}_{h}[\widehat{M}]

do
:
//
Construct candidate test policies at layer
h

4:

Define
\pi_{x,x^{\prime}}\leftarrow\argmax_{\pi\in\mathcal{A}\circ\Pi_{h+1:H}}\lvert\widehat{V}^{\pi}(x)-\widehat{V}^{\pi}(x^{\prime})\rvert
.

Estimate:
//

\displaystyle V_{\mathsf{mc}}(x\mid\pi_{x,x^{\prime}})\leftarrow\mathsf{MC}(x,\pi_{x,x^{\prime}},n_{\mathsf{mc}}),\quad V_{\mathsf{mc}}(x^{\prime}\mid\pi_{x,x^{\prime}})\leftarrow\mathsf{MC}(x^{\prime},\pi_{x,x^{\prime}},n_{\mathsf{mc}})

\mathsf{Violations}\leftarrow\{(x,\pi)\text{\penalty\ estimated in \hyperref@@ii[line:mc1]{line\penalty\ \ref*{line:mc1}} such that\penalty\ }\lvert V_{\mathsf{mc}}(x\mid\pi)-\widehat{V}^{\pi}(x)\rvert\geq\varepsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}\}
.

and
Return

(h-1,\widehat{M},\mathcal{P},\{\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{h}\}_{h\in[H]})
.

else

//
Refit transitions to handle violations

for

(x,\pi)\in\mathsf{Violations}

for

: Estimate

.

Define for every
(\bar{x},\bar{a})\in\mathcal{X}_{h:H}[\widehat{M}]\times\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle\Delta(\bar{x},\bar{a})\coloneqq\widehat{R}(\bar{x},\bar{a})+\En_{x^{\prime}\sim\widehat{P}(\cdot\mid\bar{x},\bar{a})}\left[Q_{\mathsf{mc}}(x^{\prime},\pi(x^{\prime})\mid\pi)\right]-Q_{\mathsf{mc}}(\bar{x},\bar{a}\mid\pi).

13:

for
every
(\bar{x},\bar{a})
such that
\lvert\Delta(\bar{x},\bar{a})\rvert\geq\varepsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}/(8H)

do
:

//
Define loss vectors, overwriting if already defined.

14:

Set
\ell_{\mathrm{loss}}(\bar{x},\bar{a})\coloneqq\mathsf{sign}(\Delta(\bar{x},\bar{a}))\cdot Q_{\mathsf{mc}}(\cdot,\pi(\cdot)\mid\pi)\in[0,1]^{\mathcal{X}_{h(\bar{x})+1}[\widehat{M}]}

//
OMD update with negative entropy Bregman Divergence on violations.

15:

for
every
(\bar{x},\bar{a})
from
line
